# CLAUDE.md

This file provides guidance to Claude Code (claude.ai/code) when working with code in this repository.

## 作業のルール

- **全体像はコードではなく文書から掴む。** 「どこで何が起きているか」「誰が状態を持つか」「いつ走るか」
  は `DEVELOP/OWNERSHIP.md` / `DEVELOP/CALLSTACK.md` / `DEVELOP/SPEC.md` / この CLAUDE.md が一次情報。
  当たりを付けてから必要なファイルだけを読む。手順は `.claude/skills/overview/SKILL.md`(`/overview`)。
- **検証は変更箇所に対応させる。** 既定は `npm run typecheck` のみで、これは常に走らせる。
  `npm run test:physics` は `src/physics/` を触ったときだけ。ヘッドレス実行検証(`/verify`)は
  ユーザーが実行時の動作確認を明示的に求めたときだけ。変更と無関係な検証に時間を使わない。
- **リファクタリングの判断基準は `/refactor`(一般方針)と `/refactor-fixed`(このプロジェクトで確定済みの
  責務境界)。** 責務配置の判断を新たに下した/変えたら、`.claude/skills/refactor-fixed/SKILL.md` を
  同じ変更セットで書き換える(追記ではなく、全体が整合するように書き直す。古い判断は残さない)。
- **改名は痕跡を残さない。** コードにも文書にも歴史的経緯を書かない。「旧」「former」「previously」
  「〜だった」の類は禁止。互換用の旧名エイリアスも残さず、旧名は全文検索して 0 件にする。
- **コメントは `/comment` の方針に従う。あらゆる変更で、書く前と書き換えた後に必ず見直す。**
  大原則は「そのモジュールの責務外に言及しない」。書いてよいのはモジュール/関数の「なにをするか」
  「どう使うか」と、相当に非自明な実装の理由だけ。「どう実装しているか」「なにをしないか」
  「誰が使うか」「以前どうだったか」「何を変えたか」は書かない。冗長なコメントは書き換えのたびに
  増えるので、変更セットごとに自分の書いたコメントを点検する。逆に、**関数・メソッドの直前の
  呼出規約コメントと、10行以上の関数の文脈コメントは、無いこと自体が不足**とみなす。大規模な変更を
  終えた後は `/comment-cleanup` で既存コメントを一括点検する(過剰と不足の両方向)。
- **関数・機能の追加要求を受けたら、書き始める前に必ず `/add-feature` の手順を通す。** まず既存の
  類似実装を探し、再利用可能な形で適切なモジュールにあればそれを呼ぶ(追加実装しない)。そうでなければ
  適切なモジュールへ再利用可能な形で実装し、**既存側もその呼び出しに置き換えるまでを同じ変更セットで**
  行う。数行の処理でも例外にしない。
- **共通化するかどうかは、参照箇所の数ではなく「今後も使う可能性があるか」で決める。** これはコード
  からは判別できないので、`DEVELOP/SPEC.md`「実装される可能性のある機能」節を見て、決まらなければ
  ユーザーに問う。ユーザーが可能性に言及したものは同節へ記録する。
- **`memos/hedalu244/refactoring_todo.md` は todo リスト。** 経緯は残さず、完了した項目は消す
  (そこで下した責務配置の判断は消す前に `/refactor-fixed` へ移す)。
- **デバッグ用のコードは、頼まれたことだけを、頼まれた形でそのまま書く。** デバッグ用コードの価値は
  「何が起きてほしいかがコードを読んだだけで自明」であることにあり、それを損なう実装は本番コードの
  過剰実装より悪い。よって以下は**要求されない限り一切書かない**: URL クエリ・環境変数・設定値による
  分岐、有効/無効の切り替え、件数や強度のパラメータ化、毎フレーム走る仕掛け、状態(カウンタ・フラグ・
  「一度だけ」の保守)、失敗時のフォールバック。値は定数を直書きし、呼び出しは1箇所から1回にする。
  「デバッグしやすいように気を利かせる」判断そのものが要求の逸脱にあたる。

## 設計文書 — 更新は義務

**`src/` を変更したら、同じ変更セットの中で下記の文書も更新する。**「あとでまとめて」は禁止。
判定手順と全面再生成用のプロンプトは `.claude/skills/develop-docs/SKILL.md`(`/develop-docs`)にある。
`src/**/*.ts` を編集すると PostToolUse フック(`.claude/hooks/remind-after-src-edit.mjs`)が
コメント方針の自己点検と文書の同期判定を促す。

- **この CLAUDE.md**(下記 Architecture / Controls) — モジュールの責務と「なぜそうなっているか」の解説。
  ファイルの新設・削除・移動・改名、責務の移動、キー割り当てや操作系の変更、定数の意味が変わる変更で
  更新する。**古い記述を残すより消す方がよい。**
- `DEVELOP/CALLSTACK.md` — per-frame 呼び出し依存木(update / sync / render)。**呼び出し順と実行条件の
  一次情報。** 毎フレーム走る関数の追加・削除・改名・順序変更・条件変更で更新する。
- `DEVELOP/OWNERSHIP.md` — インスタンス保持木と状態の正本(source of truth)。クラスの new 位置・
  フィールドの所有・参照共有・キャッシュが動いたら更新する。
- `DEVELOP/SPEC.md` — 「どう振舞うべきか」の仕様。プレイヤーから見える挙動・数値が変わったら更新する。

役割の切り分け: この CLAUDE.md は**散文の解説**(責務と理由)、DEVELOP/ の2文書は**機械的な事実**
(順序と所有)。両者が食い違ったら **DEVELOP/ を正とし、CLAUDE.md を直す。**
リファクタリングのやることリストは `memos/hedalu244/refactoring_todo.md`、論点ごとの todo は
同フォルダの各 `*_todo.md` を参照する(こちらも CLAUDE.md より新しい)。

## Current state

A playable LEO (low Earth orbit) shooting game: TypeScript + Webpack + npm, Three.js `WebGPURenderer` (via the `three/webgpu` entry point). Real-scale/real-time Earth orbit (player starts at 420 km / 97° inclination), body-frame RCS translation, manual rotation with RCS damping and a prograde-hold autopilot, time warp, lead-marker gunnery against enemies that shoot back with lead-aimed plasma, shell casings and destruction debris on accurate orbital physics, win on destroying all enemies, lose on being shot down or on atmospheric reentry (drag decay → adiabatic-heating overheat or dynamic-pressure breakup). Also: Earth-shadow lighting, target-board bullet-pass markers, an orbit-planning map mode ([M]: Earth-centered camera, with the camera's frame and the planned trajectory's frame selected independently on two separate panels, click the planned trajectory to place a maneuver node — several nodes can be chained, each frozen as an absolute post-burn state — W/S/A/D/Q/E adjust Δv, [N] auto-warps to the next node, BURN guide shows remaining Δv until the actual orbit matches the planned elements within tolerance), and four stages (stage 2 = Molniya-orbit enemies, unlocked once stage 1 has been cleared — tracked via `unlock-manager.ts`'s `UnlockManager`, which persists per-stage clear counts to localStorage and toasts newly-unlocked stages; stage 0 = near-combat training scoreattack, stage 00 = infinite survival; stage 0/00 are always unlocked; `?stage=` URL param skips the select screen). Each frame is `Game.update` → `Game.sync` → `Game.render`; the render phase draws `cameraSystem.activeCamera` — combat chase cam (near 2 / far 6e7) or the map camera (near = `dist / OVERVIEW_CAMERA_NEAR_RATIO` clamped to at most `CELESTIAL_SHELL_RADIUS × cos(screen-diagonal half-FOV) × OVERVIEW_CAMERA_NEAR_SHELL_MARGIN` (=0.9), so it scales with the current zoom distance instead of a fixed value while never clipping the star/celestial-grid shell — even at the screen's corners — at extreme zoom-out / far = `dist × OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_RATIO` clamped to `[OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_MIN, OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_MAX]` = `[1.5e10, 1e16]`, so it too grows with zoom rather than clipping distant objects at a fixed range) —. Stars/sun/moon billboards follow the active camera position, and the star/celestial-grid shell radius is fixed at `CELESTIAL_SHELL_RADIUS` (=1.35e10, 10% inside the far-plane floor so shell geometry never sits on it) independent of the far plane, so the map zoom-out doesn't exit the star shell.

`memos/mikanixonable/dev.md` is explicitly marked as human-authored only ("この文書は人間のみが記入できる") — do not edit it. Read it for project context, but leave modifications to the user.

### Naming: render / update / build / sync (no `draw`)

Per-frame function names must reflect what they actually do, consistently across the codebase:

- **`render`** — reserved for functions that literally call the three.js renderer's `render(scene, camera)` (only `Game.render` qualifies). A function that only constructs or mutates scene state is not a "render" function even if it runs in the render phase (e.g. `Player.syncPlayer`, `EnvironmentScene.sync`).
- **`update`** — advances or mutates logical/simulation data (not three.js mesh state directly).
- **`build`** — registers new meshes/objects into the scene (construction, not per-frame).
- **`sync`** — matches the transform/appearance of already-registered meshes (or HUD DOM elements) to logical data that was already computed elsewhere. Prefer plain `sync*` names; don't tack `Render` onto a sync method name (e.g. `syncCamera`, not `syncRenderCamera`) — it blurs the render/sync boundary this rule exists to keep clear.
- **`draw`** is never used — rename on sight.

The rAF loop in `main.ts` makes this split structural: it calls `Game.update(dt)` → `Game.sync()` → `Game.render()` as three separate top-level phases. **Only `update` takes `dt`** — nothing in the sync phase may advance anything, so no `sync` anywhere takes one (a display-side refresh cadence, e.g. `HudPanels`, holds a `performance.now()` deadline instead). `update` must not touch THREE.js objects; `sync` builds the frame's `FloatingOrigin` and pushes already-computed logical state into meshes/DOM; `render` only issues draw calls. Keep sub-functions on the correct side of that line — state that merely happens to be read at render time still belongs with the system that owns it.

- `npm run dev` — start webpack-dev-server at http://localhost:8080
- `npm run build` — production build to `docs/` (the committed GitHub Pages output — running it rewrites the published bundle)
- `npm run typecheck` — `tsc --noEmit`
- `npm run test:physics` — regression tests for `src/physics/*.ts` (pure functions, no DOM/THREE deps): compiles `tests/physics/*.test.ts` + the physics modules to CommonJS via `tsconfig.test.json` (outDir `tests/dist`, gitignored) and runs them in node with `node:assert/strict` (no extra devDependency). triangular-point stability flipping across it, and the collinear-point clearance reproducing the registry's own threshold on both sides) together with `visibleBodyIds`/`systemChainAt`/`systemMembersAt` (stars/planets/small bodies visible by default, a focused body's parent, children and siblings surfacing with no toggle set, a class toggle admitting that whole class, depth counted from the star, an unregistered or `undefined` focus still leaving stars/planets visible, the system chain walking moon→earth→sun by camera position and collapsing to the star alone near it, and `systemMembersAt` excluding a star's own children while never repeating the body it starts from); `shortcut-hint.test.ts`'s `shortcutKeyLabel` special-casing; and `plan.test.ts`'s `segmentDurationFrom`/`nodeTimeRange` (a lunar-orbit segment sized from the moon-centered state and μ, a transfer orbit near periapsis not being shortened to the circular period of its periapsis radius, and the node time range following whatever span the `DisplayDurationSource` reports). Where no clean theoretical value exists (RK4 drift, J2 rate, table-boundary continuity), the test pins the *measured* current behavior with a loose margin rather than an idealized bound — run this before/after any change under `src/physics/`.
- `npm run export-assets` — regenerates the pre-baked assets under `src/assets/`: `tools/export-models.mjs` writes the ship/debris/ammo meshes to `src/assets/models/*.json` (the authoring source for the primitive assembly `render/ships.ts` used to do at runtime), and `tools/export-earth-texture.mjs` writes `src/assets/earth.png` from `src/render/earth-color.ts`. Both tools pull the values they must agree with out of `src/` through the TypeScript compiler API rather than copying them — `export-earth-texture.mjs` takes the color functions from `render/earth-color.ts`, `export-models.mjs` takes `RCS_NOZZLES` from `render/rcs-nozzles.ts` and `RADIATOR_HINGE` from `render/radiator-hinge.ts` — so a baked asset and the code that reads it cannot diverge. Not part of `build` — run it only when the models, the nozzle table, or the surface-color functions change. `Object3D.toJSON()` mints fresh UUIDs on every run, so **all** of `src/assets/models/*.json` comes back dirty even when only one mesh changed; revert the files whose diff is UUIDs alone rather than committing the churn.

There is no other automated test suite wired into npm beyond `test:physics`.


### Architecture

Simulation state lives in ECI coordinates (Y axis = north pole), SI units (m, m/s), as plain `{x,y,z}` data (`physics/vec3.ts`'s `Vec3` is a **branded type** — build it with `v3()`/the helpers, never with a bare object literal).

`KinematicState` is a **timed** state vector — `{t, r, v}`, where `t` is the absolute simTime epoch — and it is the *single* type used for every "state at a moment": entity state, entity history, predicted-trajectory samples, and maneuver-plan nodes. There is deliberately no second type carrying the same information (the former `TrajectorySample`, `LineSample` and `PlannedNode.time` were folded into it), so a state and its time can never be passed as two arguments that disagree. `stepRK4` advances `t` along with `r`/`v`, which is why `toFrameState` takes no separate time argument.

**The base data types are immutable, with no exceptions**: `Vec3`, `KinematicState` (`physics/kinematic-state.ts`), `Quat`/`Attitude` (`physics/attitude.ts`) are all `readonly` and are advanced by *replacement*, never by in-place mutation — `stepRK4` returns a new `KinematicState`, `stepAttitude` returns a new `Attitude`, and every owner assigns the result (`entity.state = …`, `entity.att = …`). This is a correctness invariant, not a style preference: `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory` **pushes the outgoing state onto `history`** on every `step`/`reset`, and `game-entity/game-entity.ts`'s `GameEntity.orbitalElementsAround(center)` memoizes its result on `state`'s object identity plus `center.id` — both can only stay correct because nothing else can change the state behind its back. Consequences: defensive copying is unnecessary (`vec3.clone()` does not exist), and **everything under `physics/` is a pure function returning fresh values** — there are no `*Into`/`out`-parameter variants and no module-level scratch buffers. Those existed (軽量化計画 A-1) and were deliberately removed. The cost is real but small: in an isolated Node benchmark `stepRK4` went 1520 → 2190 ns/step and `stepAttitude` 218 → 414 ns/step, which at a typical ~400 live entities works out to roughly +0.35 ms per frame in the `update` phase — not distinguishable from run-to-run noise in the actual game (~5 ms update phase). The immutability that buys is what makes the `elements` memo correct, so the trade is intentional. If the entity budget grows enough for this to matter, the answer is a physics LOD (軽量化計画 B-1), not reintroducing mutable scratch into `physics/`. Rendering uses a **floating origin**: `game/floating-origin.ts`'s `FloatingOrigin` is rebuilt every `Game.sync` with its position (`r`) at the active camera's ECI position (`CameraSystem.activeCameraPos`, already resolved by that frame's `cameraSystem.update()`) and its velocity basis (`v`) at the player's velocity, and `RtoThreeV3`/`VtoThreeV3` are the *only* bridge from logical `Vec3` to `THREE.Vector3` — so f32 GPU precision never sees absolute magnitudes, at LEO or at the map view's astronomical-unit zoom-outs. Every `sync` takes the `fo` and must not use `player.state.r`/the camera position as the render origin directly (both are semantically distinct types from the `fo` they happen to agree with in combat view). Combat camera: near=`COMBAT_CAMERA_NEAR`=2m, far=`COMBAT_CAMERA_FAR`=6e7m — no logarithmic depth needed (see comment in `const.ts`).

Physics runs on the main thread (per-entity central-gravity two-body integration is cheap).

`src/game/` is an orchestrator (`game.ts`) plus subfolders grouped by concern: `player/` (player ship state), `game-entity/` (the entity base and derived classes, one file per class), `simulation/` (the array that holds live entities, plus the passive, non-AI/non-input systems that drive it each frame — stepping, prediction, collision, hit detection), `camera/`, `plan/` (maneuver planning and its future-trajectory display, independent of whether the map is open), `stages/` (per-stage behavior), `hud/` (DOM overlay), `marker/` (screen-projected markers), `vfx/`, and `input/`.

**`*Ctx` snapshot objects are a rejected pattern here** — don't add new ones and don't widen the survivors. Almost all of them have been expanded into explicit arguments or shared references; what remains is `Player.behave`'s parameter object.

- `src/main.ts` — entry point: WebGPU scene init (`initScene`, with a `showLoading` overlay while the WebGPU pipeline compiles), launch resolution (`resolveLaunchSelection`, below), an error overlay fallback, and the rAF loop (`startAnimationLoop`) driving `Game.update` → `Game.sync` → `Game.render` plus the optional `PerfMeter`. It also owns `Hud`/`Sfx`/`SettingsPanel` (`initHud`) — these are constructed before `Game` (and before the launch screen) because the title/select screen needs them too, so `Game` receives them as constructor arguments rather than constructing them itself. `main.ts` wires `SettingsPanel.onSettingsOpenChange` to `Game.pause()`/`Game.resume()` (the settings menu's open/close is the only driver of the pause state), `onBgmToggle` to `Sfx.setBgmEnabled`, and `onQuitToTitle` to reload with `?title=1`, which forces the title screen (see `resolveLaunchSelection` below). `resolveLaunchSelection(unlockManager, slots)` picks what launches without the title screen: `?title=1` forces the title screen outright; `?mode=creative`/`?stage=<id>` (checked next) resolve directly to that launch; otherwise it falls back to `SaveSlots.activeSlot()` — a `'creative'` slot resumes creative mode, a `'stage'` slot resumes `lastStageId` only if that stage is still a valid, unlocked, non-`hiddenFromSelect` `StageId` (`resumeFromActiveSlot`/`isResumableStage`) — and only shows the title screen (`selectLaunch`) when none of that resolves anything, e.g. no slot has been played yet.
- `src/game/game-mode.ts` — `GameMode` (`'stage' | 'creative'`) and `LaunchSelection` (`{mode:'stage', stage: StageId} | {mode:'creative'}`), the result type both the launch screen and the `?stage=`/`?mode=creative` URL shortcuts resolve to. No behavior — a shared type so `launch-select.ts`/`main.ts`/`game.ts` agree on the shape without importing each other's internals.
- `src/game/launch-select.ts` — `selectLaunch(unlockManager)`: the title screen, tabbed between ステージモード (built from `STAGE_DEFINITIONS`, gated per-stage by `UnlockManager.isUnlocked`, same as before) and クリエイティブモード (a single always-enabled button that resolves `{mode:'creative'}`). `main.ts`'s `resolveLaunchSelection` calls this only once `?title=1`, `?mode=creative`/`?stage=`, and the active save slot's resumable last launch have all been checked and none resolved — the URL shortcuts skip the screen entirely, mirroring each other so creative mode is as scriptable/shareable as a stage link. The corner debug-stage link and the per-stage select-screen keyboard shortcuts (`Stage.selectKeys`) live here too, both scoped to the ステージモード tab.
- `src/perf-meter.ts` — `PerfMeter`: `?perf=1` debug overlay (frame time split between update/sync+render, entity counts via `Game.perfCounts()`, JS heap). Owned by `main.ts`, not by `Game`.
- `src/game/game.ts` — orchestrator, and **only** that: it constructs and wires the subsystems and decides the per-frame call order. Owns `isPaused` (write-gated behind the public `pause()`/`resume()` methods — `main.ts` is the only caller, via the `SettingsPanel` wiring above), the frame's `FloatingOrigin`, and the top-level `update`/`sync`/`render` split (input edges → `ViewManager` → `Player.behave` → `Stage.update` → `Simulator.advance` → collision/board/loss/cleanup → `Predictor.update` → `MapPicker.refresh` → camera → plan editing; then sync: camera → environment → player → entities → VFX → orbit lines → markers → HUD panels). It does **not** own `simTime` (that's `Simulator`), the win/lose `phase` (that's `Stage`), and it does **not** construct `Hud`/`Sfx`/`SettingsPanel`/`UnlockManager` (all received from `main.ts`, see above). It constructs and drives `CameraSystem`, `MapPicker`, `EntityManager`, `Simulator`, `Predictor`, `EffectsSystem`, `Targeter`, `NavTarget`, `PlanEditor`/`DisplayTimeManager`/`PlanGuide`, `ViewManager`, `Docking`, `SimSpeedManager`, `Ephemeris`, `MarkerManager` (plus the two marker sets no single object can own — `GroupedMarkers`/`LeadMarkers`), `FrameControls` (exposed as the public readonly `frameControls`, since it's the sole entry point every other module's "move focus" action must call rather than reaching `OverviewCamera.setFocusTarget` directly — see `frame-controls.ts` below), and the active `Stage`. **It does not know which key does what**: the key↔action table is `input/key-mapping.ts`'s `KEY_MAPPING`, and `handleInput` just hands `Input` to each owner in priority order (`Docking` `pauseMenu`-while-docked → `SettingsPanel` `pauseMenu` → `Hud` `help` → `Stage` `restart` → `SimSpeedManager` `warpSlower`/`warpFaster`/`autoWarpToNode` → `ViewManager` `toggleMapMode` → `PlanEditor` `deleteNode`), and each consumes what it owns. Keys that only make sense while playing are taken by their owner's own per-frame call further down (`Player.behave` for `rcsDampToggle`/`progradeReset`/`fineAttitudeToggle`/`progradeHoldToggle`/`throttle*`/`reload`, `CameraSystem.update` for `followAttitudeToggle`/`gunsightZoom`/`camera*`, `MapPicker.handleRightClick`/`handleLeftClick`/`handleDoubleClick` → `PlanEditor.handleMapPointer` → `MapPicker.handleEmptySpaceRightClick` for map clicks, `Targeter.updateCombatTargeting` for combat right-clicks). `MapPicker.refresh()` is called once in each of `update`'s four paths (the paused early return, the no-ship early return, the stage-decided early return, and the normal path after `Simulator.advance`/`Predictor.update`) — all four go through the private `updateMapPresentation`, which opens by calling `EnvironmentScene.update(displayTime, overviewMode)` (the asteroid field's own per-frame position refresh — see `game/celestial/environment-scene.ts` above) before refreshing the candidate set immediately before that path's `cameraSystem.update()` call — building the candidate set post-integration keeps its coordinates in step with the post-integration state `sync` renders that same frame, instead of lagging a step behind under high time-warp. Many public fields (`cameraSystem`/`player`/`activeStage`/`entities`/`simulator`/`targeter`/...) exist because `hud/panel.ts`'s `HudPanels.sync(game, attractors)` reads the whole `Game` instance directly rather than a narrow ctx.
- `src/game/const.ts` — all gameplay tuning constants (thrust, sim speed levels, fire rate, hit radii, ammo/belt geometry, camera near/far, prediction throttles, stage tuning...).
- `src/game/floating-origin.ts` — `FloatingOrigin`: the render origin's motion state — `r` at the active camera's ECI position (kept near-zero relative to the camera itself, which is what keeps the WebGPU renderer's single-precision `modelViewMatrix` composition from losing precision to catastrophic cancellation at map-view distances), `v` at the player's velocity for relative-velocity-facing draws — and the sole `Vec3` → `THREE.Vector3` conversion (`RtoThreeV3` for positions, `VtoThreeV3` for relative-velocity-facing draws like bullets).

`src/game/player/` — the player ship, split by concern (all owned and composed by `Player`, which exposes read-only getters for `game.ts`/HUD):
- `player.ts` — `Player extends Ship`: initial LEO placement (`makeInitialState`/`progradeAttitude`, `INITIAL_ALT`=420 km / `INITIAL_INC_DEG`=97°; `progradeAttitude`'s `inertia` is asymmetric across all three axes — `PLAYER_INERTIA_PITCH/YAW/ROLL`, held as the shared `Player.INERTIA` so `restore` builds the same tensor rather than a placeholder — so the Dzhanibekov effect isn't debris-only, and manual angular velocity has no cap: holding an RCS key keeps accelerating the spin indefinitely), the per-frame `behave` (belt → own edge keys → torque → HP regen → fire → thrust) and `checkLoss`/`syncPlayer` entry points, damage/loss handling (`attacked` — which now also banks `thermal.addImpactHeat()` and, when `radiator.damageFromHit` reports a newly-destroyed panel, triggers a one-shot debris break effect at that panel's tip — and `checkLoss(dt, simTime, activeStage, playerPos, attractors)`, which checks hull heat/dynamic-pressure via `thermal` and, independently, whether the hull has reached any attractor's surface via `attractor.ts`'s `hitCelestialBody(state.r, attractors, C.PLAYER_MIN_ALT)` — the atmospheric-heating/dynamic-pressure thresholds stay Earth-specific (only Earth has an atmosphere here), but the structural-breakup threshold is body-agnostic), and composes `throttle`/`fire`/`belt`/`thermal`/`radiator`/`power`/`thrustEffects`/`rcsEffects`/`markers`. **Every `Player` owns its own `plan: Plan`** (not just the one flying in stage mode) — `PlanEditor.plan` is a getter forwarding to whichever ship `Game.setActivePlayer` last made active, so switching the flown ship switches which plan is being edited without either class needing to know about the other's swap. There is no creative-mode subclass: a placed ship is a plain `Player` constructed with an explicit `name`/`initialState` (the constructor's defaults are what a stage-mode ship uses instead), and `planExecution: PlanExecutionMode` (the type itself defined in `plan/plan-executor.ts`, not here — see below — and re-exported; `'off' | 'instant' | 'powered'`, read each frame by `CreativeStage.update`/`applySimulationEvents` to decide whether and how this ship's `plan` executes itself — `'instant'` is the historical teleport-on-node-time behavior, `'powered'` drives the `readonly planExecutor: PlanExecutor` field below) is on `Player` directly rather than something only some ships have. `behave` interrupts a `'powered'` ship back to `'off'` the moment the operator gives it manual translation (`this.thrust !== null`) or rotation (`throttle.hasManualRotationInput(input)`) input, the same way manual rotation cancels `progradeHold`. `syncPlayer(fo, camera, phasePlaying, paused, displayTime, isActive)` takes `isActive` because `EntityManager.players` can hold more than one ship (creative mode) — only the active one hides for the gunsight zoom and pushes `markers` (the orbital-direction/boresight/`▲` HUD markers, which describe *the* operator's frame and would be meaningless duplicated across ships). `thrustEffects`/`rcsEffects`/`reentryEffects` sync off `displayState(displayTime) ?? this.state` rather than `this.state` directly — the plume/RCS-puff/reentry-glow billboards have to sit at the same future-ghost position the hull mesh itself is drawn at, or the effect visibly detaches from the ship the moment the slider moves; when `displayState` is `null` (beyond this ship's prediction horizon) the effects are called with `alive` forced false instead of being skipped outright, so each one hides itself through its own existing dead/absent-state path rather than `syncPlayer` needing a second one. `handleEdgePress` claims the ship-side keys (`[T]`/`[F]`/`[V]`/`[C]`/`[1]`-`[3]`/`[R]`) via `input.takeKeys()`, which consumes them so no later module double-handles. `[R]` is only consumed when a manual reload actually starts. Right after `this.thrust` is set, `behave` calls `this.invalidatePrediction()` if it's non-null — the one place a `GameEntity` invalidates its own prediction *outside* `resyncPrediction`'s distance check, because the player's own burn needs to be reflected in the predicted trajectory immediately (a UX requirement `resyncPrediction`'s few-frame lag can't meet) and this is deliberately player-only, not a `thrust` setter, leaving room for `Enemy` thrust to someday *not* reset immediately.
- `player-throttle.ts` — `PlayerThrottle`: translation input → thrust accel (`buildThrust`, all 6 body-frame directions), manual rotation + RCS damping, and the prograde-hold autopilot (target forward = velocity direction, target up = radial-out) built on `physics/attitude.ts`'s `attitudeAlignTorque` — the PD control itself (quaternion error → body-frame axis-angle → `PROGRADE_HOLD_KP`/`_KD` torque) is a pure function there, shared with `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s burn-direction alignment rather than living only here. `updateTorque` only *returns* the body torque (`Player` writes it to `entity.torque`) and `updateThrustState` only returns the thrust vector — the actual attitude/orbit integration is `Simulator`'s. Each of the 6 translation keys is independently double-tap latchable (public `updateThrustLatches`, consuming `Input`'s press edges via `takeKey`; `isThrustHeld` ORs the physical hold state with the latch set), timed against `performance.now()` rather than a `dt`-accumulated clock so a double-tap's interval judgment doesn't depend on whether every frame in between actually called it. `updateThrustLatches` is `Player.behave`'s own call (skipped while `dvEditActive` — WASDQE are Δv-edit keys there —, while dead, or while `!simSpeed.canPlayerThrust` — `Player.behave` is the one asking `SimSpeedManager` per the `can*` convention, so a warp-level double-tap neither latches nor consumes the key edge, and dropping back below the threshold doesn't detonate a latch that silently accumulated during the warp), not folded into `updateThrustState`, since the latter runs unconditionally whenever real thrust is being computed and the two questions ("did a key just double-tap" vs "what thrust does the current hold/latch state produce") are separate. Holding the *opposite* direction key also holds that latch cleared for as long as the opposite key stays down (not just on its press edge) — otherwise a physical hold on the opposing key while one side is latched would only cancel it out in `buildThrust`'s per-axis sum, and releasing the opposing key would make the still-latched side reappear unannounced. `stopThrust()` (called from `updateThrustState`'s no-thrust branch, `clearTransientState`, and both of `Player.behave`'s early returns — `dvEditActive`, dead) only zeroes `thrustAccelVec` (the belt's own fictitious-force input, see `belt.ts` above) now — it does **not** touch SFX or the plume, since `thrust-effects.ts`'s `ThrustEffects.sync` reads `this.thrust` (the `GameEntity` field, whichever of `PlayerThrottle`/`plan/plan-executor.ts`'s `PlanExecutor` last wrote it) directly every frame and owns the engine-sound toggle itself; a manual stop reaches the plume/SFX only because `ship.thrust` itself goes `null` on the same path, not through a second explicit call. `latchedThrustKeys`/`lastThrustPressTime` are both cleared by `clearTransientState`. Holding any of the 3 opposite-key pairs together (`isThrustKillSwitchActive`) is an emergency all-stop: `buildThrust` returns `null` unconditionally (bypassing the per-axis sum entirely, so it also zeroes any *other* axis's held/latched thrust, not just the pressed pair's own) and `updateThrustLatches` clears every latch instead of running its per-key toggle logic that frame — a single pair stops every axis, not just its own.
- `player-fire.ts` — `PlayerFire`: gun state (mag/round/barrel counters, reload timer), `updateFireState`/`fireCycle`/`consume` (the ammo state machine, returning `ConsumeResult` = `empty`/`normal`/`mag-reload`/`barrel-reload`), `fireGun` (bullet spawn, recoil, casing eject, muzzle flash, `ScoreCounter.recordShot`), and `dropBarrel`/`spawnEjectedMagazineFrame` for reload debris. `tickMapMode` advances only the reload timer while the planner is open.
- `belt.ts` / `belt-physics.ts` — the ammo belt display split from its physics: `belt-physics.ts`'s `BeltPhysics` is a pure Vec3/Quat Verlet-chain solver (position-based dynamics; otherwise no THREE/mesh dependency — see `BeltSection` below for the one exception) for the belt's sag/twist under the ship's thrust+spin pseudo-forces; `belt.ts`'s `Belt` owns the link meshes and derives each link's transform from `BeltPhysics`'s solved state via parallel transport. `belt-physics.ts` also defines `BeltSection extends GameEntity` — the per-link contact proxy `game/simulation/contact.ts` resolves against (`BeltPhysics.collisionSections`/`applyCollisionSections` convert to/from it each frame). It's declared here rather than in `game-entity/` because nothing outside the belt uses it, and its constructor needs only a placeholder `THREE.Object3D` to satisfy `GameEntity`'s constructor — it's never added to a scene or stepped, so that placeholder is the only place this otherwise THREE-free module touches THREE.
- `thermal.ts` — `ThermalSystem`: **the single source of truth for the hull's heat budget** — aerodynamic heating, gun firing heat, bullet-impact heat and radiative cooling all land in `updateThermal`, plus dynamic pressure and the altitude-descent-warning EMA (`updateAltitudeAlarm`); returns a `ThermalLimit` verdict. `addGunHeat(rounds)`/`addImpactHeat()` both bank their energy in joules into the shared `pendingHeat` rather than writing `hullTemp` directly, because `updateThermal` runs once per substep and the deposited energy must not scale with how many times that happens — the actual destruction (`alive = false` + effects) happens in `Player.checkLoss`.
- `radiator.ts` — `RadiatorSystem`: the two deployable radiator panels (deploy fraction and hinge-angle `sync`). **It does not own the panels' damage** — `wear` (0=intact, 1=destroyed) is handed to `update(dt, wear)` every frame by `Player`, derived from the two radiator *parts*' remaining HP, so the thing the dock repairs and the thing that stops radiating are one and the same. Panels therefore never self-heal in flight; repair is the dock's job alone. It **does not know the hull temperature** — it answers only `radiatingArea()` [m²] and `solarLoad(sunlit, sunDir, att)` [W], which `Player` feeds to `ThermalSystem.setRadiatorLoad` each frame, so the Stefan-Boltzmann T⁴ law stays owned by `ThermalSystem` alone and no temperature round-trips between the two; `radiatingArea` multiplies `RADIATOR_PANEL_AREA` (the modeled dimensions) by the separate `RADIATOR_EFFICIENCY_MULT` performance multiplier, so tuning the panels' cooling power never has to fight the "model dimensions and physics stay one number" rule that `RADIATOR_PANEL_AREA` itself follows. `foldThetas(side)` is shared by `sync` and the panel-normal math so the drawn angle and the illuminated angle cannot diverge; because an accordion's even and odd folds face different ways, `solarLoad` evaluates both orientations and splits the area between them. A deployed panel is a real contact surface, not a hit-radius adjustment: `RadiatorFold extends GameEntity` is a one-fold-wide rigid collision proxy (`collides = true`, `attachedTo = owner`, so it never contacts the ship itself or the panel's own other folds/the belt's own nodes), and `collisionFolds(shipR, shipV, att, t)` re-derives each fold's world position/velocity from the ship's current state every frame — cheap enough to place fresh rather than track, since a fold's placement is a pure function of the ship's attitude and the panel's own deploy angle. Only sides deployed past `RADIATOR_CONTACT_DEPLOY` and not fully worn contribute proxies, so a stowed or destroyed panel can't be hit. `RadiatorFold.collideWith` forwards straight to `Player.collideAtRadiator(side, other, contact, activeStage)`, which special-cases a `Bullet` contact (routed to the same `attackedByBullet` a hull hit uses, tagged with `side` so the damage lands on that panel's part) and otherwise applies the contact's own impulse to the matching `radiatorParts[side]` — deploying a panel therefore both adds a real obstacle a bullet or another rigid body can strike and, once struck, funnels the damage to that specific part; `RadiatorSystem.tipWorldPosition` reads the destroyed panel's own fold mesh (post-`sync()`) for the one-shot break effect's spawn point, and `sync()` hides a fully-worn panel's fold meshes for good.
- `reentry-effects.ts` — `ReentryEffects`: the plasma glow ahead of the ship during reentry. **Stateless** — the intensity is derived from `ThermalSystem.qdyn` every frame (`REENTRY_GLOW_MIN_Q` → `REENTRY_GLOW_FULL_Q` mapped continuously onto scale and opacity), so there is nothing to advance in `update` and only a `sync`. It orients off the *absolute* ECI velocity rather than `fo.VtoThreeV3`, since the floating origin is the player itself and the relative velocity is therefore always zero here.
- `power.ts` — `PowerSystem`: the solar panels' charge, HUD-facing as `chargeRatio` (0..1). The panels are fixed (no deploy animation), so their normal is a constant body-frame `(0,1,0)` — `update(dt, sunlit, sunDir, att)` just rotates that one normal into world space, takes `max(0, dot(...))` (unlike `RadiatorSystem.solarLoad`'s `abs`, since a one-sided panel doesn't generate power off its back face), and banks the energy into `charge`, clamped to `POWER_CAPACITY`. Entirely numeric — no `sync()`, since there's no mesh state to push.
- `thrust-effects.ts` / `rcs-effects.ts` — billboard VFX (+ the shared engine/RCS loop SFX) for the main engine plume (positioned from `render/ships.ts`'s `MUZZLE_OFFSETS`) and for the attitude thrusters. Both read straight off the `GameEntity` fields the physics side already writes — `ThrustEffects.sync(fo, playerPos, thrust, maxAccel, alive, audible, camera)` takes `this.thrust` itself (not a `PlayerThrottle`-specific direction/throttle-index pair), so a burn commanded by manual input (`PlayerThrottle.updateThrustState`) and one commanded by `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s `PlanExecutor` (which writes `ship.thrust` directly, never touching `PlayerThrottle`) render identically without either module needing to know about the other; `maxAccel` (`ship.totalThrust / ship.mass`, computed by the caller) turns the thrust vector's magnitude into a 0..1 output ratio for the plume size, replacing the old fixed 4-step throttle-index scale so a continuously-varying autopilot output (full burn vs. trim) still reads proportionately. `RcsEffects` already followed this shape — it reads `this.torque` directly, not `PlayerThrottle`'s manual-rotation state, which is why `PlanExecutor`-driven attitude holds during a burn also light the correct nozzles for free. Both take an `audible`/`isActive`-style flag (`RcsEffects.sync`'s `audible`, `ThrustEffects.sync`'s `audible`) since every self-flown ship's plume renders but only the operated ship's engine/RCS loop should be heard — `RcsEffects` builds one plume per entry of `render/rcs-nozzles.ts`'s `RCS_NOZZLES`, and picks which ones fire by the torque each nozzle *produces*: `τ_k = r_k × (−exhaust_k)` is a per-nozzle constant derived once from the table, and a nozzle fires when `dot(τ_k, torque) > 0`. Because the nozzle's own direction is fixed hardware, the plume can only ever appear at one place per nozzle — a torque-dependent exhaust direction would slide the plume continuously around its block as the commanded torque turns.
- `player-markers.ts` — `PlayerMarkers`: the HUD markers that follow from the ship's own position/attitude — the six orbital-frame direction markers (prograde/retrograde/normal/antinormal/radial in-out, the Navball stand-in), the `┼` boresight, and the map-mode `▲` self marker. `sync(currentState, displayState, att, alive, overviewMode, isActive, project, scale, displayName, ...)` takes two states rather than one: the combat-view markers (direction/boresight) always read `currentState`, since they represent the ship's real orbital frame regardless of the future-ghost slider, while `▲` reads `displayState` (`null` beyond the player's own prediction horizon, in which case `▲` just hides) — the two states coincide whenever the slider is at rest, which combat view always is. `▲` is rotated to face `displayState.v` via `MarkerManager.headingRotationDeg(displayState.r, displayState.v, project, scale)`, so a circular orbit still shows which way the ship is actually moving; combat view needs no such hint (the mesh's own attitude is visible there) and leaves the direction markers unrotated. `Player.syncPlayer` drives it (only when that ship is the active one — see `player.ts` above), so callers never see a separate marker entry point.

`src/game/game-entity/` — the entity base and derived classes, one file per class:
- `game-entity.ts` — `GameEntity` (base: mesh/HP/alive/attitude/AI-facing state, plus the fields that let an instance stand in for `physics/attractor.ts`'s `Attractor` with no conversion: `readonly id: string` (explicit constructor argument, or self-assigned by the base `EntityIdAllocator` when omitted), `radius = 0` (physical radius [m]; 0 = point, the same value `Attractor.radius` reads), `collides = false` (rigid-contact participation — independent of `radius`, since a bullet or debris fragment has a physical size but must not bounce off the hull), `mu = 0` (gravity constant GM; 0 = no gravity — a body is a gravity source purely by `mu !== 0`, decided nowhere else), `degree2: Degree2Gravity | null = null` and `isStar = false` — both plain mutable fields (not `readonly`) precisely so a derived class can build its own field at construction, the way `Asteroid` does. `protected setGravitatingMass(mass)` is the one place a mass becomes a `mu` (`mu = GRAVITATIONAL_CONSTANT * mass`, also recording `mass` itself for rigid-contact use), so a subclass never derives `mu` by hand. `EntityManager.attractors()` filters on `mu !== 0 && alive` alone — `radius`/`collides` don't gate it, since a body's shape and its collision participation are separate questions from whether it pulls. Delegates the actual "time-stamped state + its sample history + advance itself" job to `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory`, held as the public `actualTrajectory` field. `state`/`prevState`/`history` are thin forwarding getters to `actualTrajectory` — the ~20 call sites across the codebase that only ever read `.state` are unaffected by this split. The `state` **setter** forwards to `actualTrajectory.reset(...)`, the sole entry point for a discontinuous external overwrite (rigid contact, recoil); `stepActual(dt, attractors)` never goes through it — it self-gates on `alive` and hands `actualTrajectory.step(...)` the caller-supplied gravity-source array plus the entity's own `bcInv`/`thrust`/`sampleInterval(attractors)`/`historyDuration` (integration itself is `actualTrajectory`'s job, not this class's; `Simulator.substep` decides `attractors` for this step — see `simulation/attractors.ts` below — so `stepActual`/`stepPredicted` share the same argument shape). `orbitalElementsOf(center)` is the only way to get osculating elements out of an entity — there is no bare `elements` getter, since which body an ellipse is centered on is a per-call choice (typically `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor`), not a fixed property of the entity; it memoizes on `state`'s object identity and `center.id`, both of which only change together with an actual new orbit. `sampleInterval(attractors)` derives the history's push interval from `attractor.ts`'s `localOrbitPeriod(state.r, attractors)` (the period around whichever body pulls hardest right now, divided by `TRAJECTORY_SAMPLES_PER_REV`, falling back to a `SHIP_HISTORY_DURATION`-based value when no finite period exists, e.g. hyperbolic orbits) and is skipped entirely while `historyDuration === 0` (bullets/casings/debris never need it), `torque`/`thrust` slots the owning system writes each frame, and a default `checkLoss(_dt, _simTime, _activeStage, _playerPos, attractors)` that calls `attractor.ts`'s `hitCelestialBody(state.r, attractors, C.DEBRIS_REENTRY_ALT)` — surface contact with *any* attractor, not just Earth, since a stray debris piece near the moon shouldn't pass through it; this `attractors` argument is always `Ephemeris.attractorsAt`'s analytic-body-only window, as `EntityManager.cleanup` receives from `Simulator`, never the gravity-bearing-`GameEntity`-merged one, so approaching another gravity-bearing entity is never itself mistaken for reentry).

- `src/game/display-time-manager.ts` / `display-time-panel.ts` — `DisplayTimeManager`: "when to look" — the display duration (`durationKey` → `durationSec(referencePeriod)`; presets `'orbit'` (default) /`'90min'`/`'day'`/`'week'`/`'month'` plus `'manual'`), the future-ghost slider position `sliderT`, `resolveDisplayTime(simTime, referencePeriod)` for the "show the sky at a future time" query every future-aware `sync` uses, and `forceCurrent` (a get/set accessor, starting `true`, flipped by `ViewManager`; while set, `resolveDisplayTime` always returns the present, and the setter also zeroes `sliderT` — leaving combat view always resets the slider to the present rather than resuming wherever it was left, and `panel.onDurationSelect` zeroes it the same way, since switching how far the slider can reach mid-drag would otherwise leave it pointing at a now-meaningless fraction of the new span). `durationSec` holds no orbital period of its own: for `'90min'`/`'day'`/`'week'`/`'month'` it looks the constant up in a `Record<key, seconds>` table (a compile-time-exhaustive alternative to an if-chain — adding a preset without a table entry is a type error rather than a silent fallthrough), `'manual'` reads `manualDurationSec` instead, and only `'orbit'` returns `referencePeriod` as-is (falling back to `C.APERIODIC_ARC_DURATION` when it isn't finite/positive) — every caller resolves its own notion of "one orbit" and passes it in, so this class never has to decide whose orbit or around which body. `Game.currentOrbitPeriod()` supplies it for the game-wide display time (the active ship's osculating period around whichever body currently pulls hardest, `NaN` with no ship); `plan.ts`'s `segmentDurationFrom` supplies it separately for the plan's trailing segment and `Plan.nodeTimeRange`, from that segment's own start state — the two contexts can disagree about "one orbit" without either owning the other. Owned by `Game` (not `PlanEditor` — nothing here is plan-specific; `EnvironmentScene`/`CameraSystem`/`FocusMarkers` all resolve a display time too, and `PlanDisplay`'s ghost is just one more consumer), and additionally shared by reference from `Game` through `PlanEditor`/`PlanDisplay` down to `plan-path.ts`'s `PlanPath`, which is what lets the plan's own trailing segment track the PREDICT panel's selection (see `plan-path.ts` below). `'manual'` reads `manualDurationSec` (a value+unit input in the panel, hour/day/week/year, clamped into `[DISPLAY_DURATION_MIN, DISPLAY_DURATION_MAX]` = 1 hour to 1 year — the lower bound matters because the duration doubles as the predicted list's retention window, and a zero-length window keeps no samples at all) instead of one of the fixed presets, so the slider can span durations no preset covers; `sync(simTime, referencePeriod)` also pushes the tick marks `hud/tick-scale.ts`'s `buildTicks(lastDurationSec, TICK_MAX_COUNT)` returns (positions at round multiples of a chosen interval rather than equal divisions of the span, so the panel shows scale regardless of which duration is selected) — each tick is narrow, so the absolute date is the slider label's job alone (`futureTimeLabel`, `hud/utils.ts`'s `fmtDateTime` off that module's `SIM_EPOCH_SEC`, the same epoch `hud/panel.ts`'s MET row anchors to), plus `setSliderValue(sliderT)`/`setSliderSteps(...)`/`setManualRange(DISPLAY_DURATION_MIN, DISPLAY_DURATION_MAX)` every frame so the slider DOM element and the manual-range clamp stay driven by `DisplayTimeManager`'s own state rather than only reacting to the panel's own input events — `DurationValueInput.emit` (`display-time-panel.ts`) clamps the typed value into the range its owner set and writes the clamped value back into the input field itself, so an over-range entry visibly snaps to the limit instead of silently being capped somewhere downstream; the same class also keeps the input's `min`/`max` attributes in sync with whichever unit is currently selected, so a 0-looking entry doesn't silently snap to the 1-hour floor once converted. `sliderSteps()` sizes the slider's step count from `lastDurationSec` (cached from the most recent `sync`, since the DOM `input` event that needs it fires outside the frame flow) at roughly `SLIDER_TARGET_STEP_SEC` per step, clamped to `[SLIDER_MIN_STEPS, SLIDER_MAX_STEPS]` — the `setSliderValue`/`onSliderChange` interface stays 0..1 regardless, so no caller needs to know the current step count. A T+-time jump input (also a `DurationValueInput`) resolves against the same cached `lastDurationSec` to set `sliderT` directly, and a "current" button resets `sliderT` to 0 the same way `forceCurrent`'s setter does. `DisplayTimePanel` is its DOM (duration `SegmentedControl` + manual value/unit inputs + jump value/unit inputs, both instances of the shared `DurationValueInput` + slider + reset button + tick labels + a self-sufficient hint label — the ghost marker's own label carries the richer altitude readout, since that needs `PlanDisplay`'s trajectory sample and `DisplayTimeManager` must not depend on it). The constructor wraps the panel in its own `#hud-displaytime-wrap` alongside a `hud/dom.ts`'s `buildCollapseToggle` button (`PREDICT_TOGGLE_LABELS`) — the same collapse mechanism `hud/dom.ts`'s left/right dock toggles use — so the PREDICT bar itself can be folded away independently of `setVisible`: `setVisible` is view-driven (hidden outright in combat view) while the collapse toggle's `.collapsed` class is the player's own choice within map view, and the two compose rather than overriding each other. The duration scales the future-ghost slider and, through `segmentDurationFrom`, the length of the plan's trailing segment and the range a node may be dragged across.
- `ammo.ts` — `Ammo`: the orbital resupply pickup `stage-utils/logistics.ts` spawns and the player absorbs on approach (`bcInv = SMALL_DEBRIS_BCINV`, `predictsFuture = true`, `radius = AMMO_PHYS_RADIUS`, `collides = true`). `id` is always set — the constructor takes an optional override (`CreativeStage` passes its own placement id, `Ammo.restore` passes the saved id) and otherwise self-generates one through `entity-id.ts`'s shared `EntityIdAllocator('ammo-')` when omitted, so every spawn — including `Logistics`' auto-resupply, which never passes one — has an id a caller can key a `MapPickable`/window on without inventing one at the call site.
- `debris-piece.ts` — `DebrisPiece`/`DebrisKind` (`bcInv = SMALL_DEBRIS_BCINV`): the one class backing fragments/ejected barrels/magazine frames/casings — `DebrisKind` picks which `render/ships.ts` builder runs and which extra `checkLoss` rule applies (only `casing` pieces expire on `CASING_LIFETIME`; the rest only via the shared any-attractor surface-contact check). `fragment` pieces get `collides = false` (pass through collision untouched, though they may still carry a nonzero `radius`); the others take the caller-supplied `radius` and `collides = true`.
- `bullet.ts` — `Bullet extends GameEntity`: player/enemy bullets and enemy plasma bolts (`Shooter`/`BulletType`), `bcInv = BULLET_BCINV`, its own loss check (**distance from the player first — `BULLET_MAX_DIST` — with the lifetime only as a backstop**, since every live bullet is its own draw call), and a velocity-facing (not attitude-facing) `sync` override that uses `fo.VtoThreeV3`. `predictsFuture` stays at the `GameEntity` default of `false`, so `displayState(displayTime)` is `null` for the entire time the future-ghost slider is active — bullets simply disappear rather than showing a nonsensical projected flight path, which is fine since their lifetime is a few seconds.
- `enemy.ts` — `Enemy extends Ship`: enemy AI (`behave` — burst-fire plasma at the player using `physics/intercept.ts`'s `solveLeadTime`; takes the `EntityManager` reference to read `enemies` for the per-group attacker cap and to `addBullet`; per-group attacker cap and burst state live here), damage/destruction (`attacked`, `checkLoss`), its own `OrbitLine`, `inertiaForEnemyKind(enemyKind)` (the one source of each `EnemyKind`'s inertia tensor, shared by `enemy-generator.ts`'s spawners and by `restore`), and its HUD presentation (`accentColor`, and `markerItem(role, viewerPos, pos, vel, overviewMode)` returning the `GroupedMarkerItem` the screen-marker set is built from — the enemy states what it looks like, `GroupedMarkers` decides how the set is laid out; `pos`/`vel` are caller-supplied arguments rather than `this.state`/`.v` because `Game.sync` resolves them from `displayState(displayTime)` — the marker has to sit at the same future-ghost position as the enemy's own mesh, or the two visibly disagree the moment the slider moves; `overviewMode` picks `Ship.hpMarkerSvg()` in combat view or `Ship.headingHpMarkerSvg()` on the map, and `vel` is what `GroupedMarkers.sync` turns into the map-view marker's heading rotation).
- `base.ts` — `Base extends GameEntity`: a docking station placed on an orbit like any other entity — its mesh (`render/ships.ts`'s `buildBaseModel`) is built and `sync`'d through the same `GameEntity`/`EntityManager` path every other entity uses, so it is a real object in both map and combat view (`EntityManager.sync` runs unconditionally, not gated on `overviewMode`) rather than a map-only marker. Its `baseState` (`money`/`inventory: AnyPart[]`/`dockedShips: DockedShipEntry[]`) is the sole store for what the base holds — a `DockedShipEntry` snapshots a docked ship's `id`/`name`/`hp`/`maxHp` for the ship-list tab plus the `player: Player` reference `Docking`/`DockView` use to act on the real ship (`parts` is not a snapshot — it's the same array as `player.parts`, so `hud/dock-view.ts` repairs land on the ship directly and only the `hp`/`maxHp` totals need writing back). Owns its own `orbitLine: OrbitLine` (`COLOR_BASE_ORBIT_LINE`, same construction/`dispose` pattern as `Enemy`'s), synced via `syncOrbitLine(show, fo, bodies)` — `Game.sync` calls it once per `entities.bases` entry with `show = overviewMode`, map-only like `Enemy.syncBackgroundOrbitLine` since a base's full orbit is a wide-context reference not needed at combat-view zoom.
- `asteroid.ts` — `Asteroid extends GameEntity`: the one class that both exerts and receives gravity through the ordinary integration path — no new orbit classification, it rides `stepActual`/`DynamicTrajectory` exactly like every other entity. The constructor derives `radius`/`collides = true` (from the caller-supplied physical radius) and, via `GameEntity.setGravitatingMass(mass)`, `mu = GRAVITATIONAL_CONSTANT * mass`, so the pull it exerts and the weight it hits with can never disagree. Optional `j2`/`c22` (both default 0, i.e. a point mass) build a `degree2` field once at construction — pole = its own `att.q`-rotated model +Y, tesseral long axis = model +Z rotated the same way, the same convention `body-orientation.ts`'s `spinOrientation` uses — rather than a getter, since `Asteroid` sits outside `Simulator.stepAttitudes`' target arrays (players/enemies/casings/debris/ammos) and so never rotates after construction; a getter would rebuild the pole vector every RK4 stage for every entity for no reason. `readonly predictsFuture = true` — an asteroid grows its own future-ghost through `Predictor` exactly like a ship, so a gravity-bearing entity's predicted trajectory is available for `predictedAttractorsAt` (`simulation/attractors.ts` below) to read from rather than needing a frozen stand-in. `bcInv`/`srpCoeff` stay at the `GameEntity` default of 0 (no drag/radiation-pressure model for a bare rock). Distinct from `game/celestial/point-field.ts`/`point-field-view.ts`'s point cloud, which is display-only and neither a `GameEntity` nor a gravity source — see that module's own note.

`src/game/simulation/` — the array that holds live entities, plus the systems that actually drive it each frame; split from `game-entity/` because these are updaters *over* the arrays, not entity classes themselves:
- `entity-id.ts` — `EntityIdAllocator`: the one implementation of "mint `${prefix}${counter}` unless a caller supplies an id to restore, in which case adopt it and advance the counter past it" — the pattern `Ammo`/`Base`/`Asteroid` all need (a restored save must keep its old id, but the counter must never hand out that id again to a fresh spawn). Each class holds its own instance keyed by its own prefix; the allocator has no cross-class state.
- `entity-manager.ts` — `EntityManager`: owns the `players`/`enemies`/`bullets`/`casings`/`debris`/`ammos`/`asteroids` arrays (capped, oldest evicted on overflow via `addXxx`/`addCapped` — `players` is uncapped instead, gated by `CREATIVE_MAX_SHIPS` at the `CreativeStage.placeShip` call site since removal there is deliberate, not eviction), `findPlayer(id)`/`findEnemy(id)` (the id lookups ship-menu actions, `NavTarget`'s plane resolution, and the equatorial-node source list use to turn a `MapPickable.id`/target id back into a live `Player`/`Enemy`), lifetime cleanup (`cleanup(dt, simTime, activeStage, playerPos, attractors)`/`prune`, which takes `simTime`/the player position (because `Bullet.checkLoss` culls by distance) and the frame's `Attractor[]` (because every `checkLoss` now checks surface contact against all of them) — `cleanup` runs `checkLoss` over `all()` uniformly but only `prune`s the five non-player arrays, since a lost ship stays around as the destroyed-effect/chase-camera anchor rather than being removed), and the per-entity render sync (`sync(fo, displayTime)`, forwarding `displayTime` to each non-player `GameEntity.sync` — see `game-entity.ts`'s `displayState` above; `players` is excluded because `Player.syncPlayer` does its own richer sync, driven by `Game.sync` looping `entities.players`). `all()` is every non-player array plus `players`; the private `otherEntities()` both `all()` and `sync()` build on top of is the one place "which arrays don't include players" is answered. `attractors()` filters `all()` down to the living, gravity-bearing (`mu !== 0`) entities and returns them structurally as `physics/attractor.ts`'s `Attractor[]` with no conversion step — `game/simulation/attractors.ts` is its sole consumer. It runs no simulation itself — `all()`/the arrays are handed out by reference to `Simulator`/`ContactPhysics` (below), and to every module that only ever reads or adds entities (`Targeter`, `NavTarget`, `Enemy.behave`, `Player.behave`→`PlayerFire`, `Stage`/`stages/`, `Logistics`, `EffectsSystem`, `NanWatchdog`). Owned by `Game` and exposed as the public `entities` field — the one window every other module shares by reference, which is also why `Stage.setup` now hands `Logistics` the manager directly and `EffectsSystem`'s constructor takes it directly instead of an injected `addDebris` callback (no per-owner closures).

  **The active ship is just `Game.player`'s current pointer into `players`**, not a separately-tracked identity — `players` holds every self-flown ship (stage mode's lone one included) on equal footing, and `Game.setActivePlayer` only ever reassigns which element `player` points at. Nothing filters the active ship out of `players` before processing it: `Simulator.substep`/`stepAttitudes` step every element of `players` uniformly, and `stepAttitudes` in particular integrates every array (players/enemies/casings/debris/ammos alike) at the same per-substep `subDt` — there is no separate, coarser attitude step for non-player entities, `EntityManager.cleanup`'s `checkLoss` pass covers `players` the same as any other array via `all()`, and `ContactPhysics.resolveSubstep` takes `entities.all()` (`players` included) as its participant list outright. The two places that still single the active ship out do so through the explicit `player` argument rather than by excluding it from an array: `Predictor.update` spends its first budget slice on `player` before round-robining the *whole* `entities.all()` (`player` included again) — the active ship can draw budget twice in one frame, deliberately, rather than being tracked and excluded from the round-robin by identity. `EntityManager.sync` is the one place `players` is genuinely skipped wholesale, since every ship (active or not) syncs itself via `Player.syncPlayer` instead (see `player.ts` above).
- `simulator.ts` — `Simulator`: the actual simulation step. Owns **`simTime`/`lastSimDt`**, `ContactPhysics`, and takes the `EntityManager` reference in its constructor rather than holding any array itself — splitting "which entities exist" (`EntityManager`) from "how they're stepped" (`Simulator`) keeps each to one responsibility. `advance` splits the frame's `simDt` into substeps (count = `ceil(simDt / SUBSTEP_MAX_DT)` — depends only on the simulated time this frame, never on the real frame rate, and is deliberately uncapped so the step size the constant names actually holds at every warp level); each substep (`substep`) first calls `simulation/attractors.ts`'s `attractorsAt(ephemeris, entities, t)` **once**, at the substep's own midpoint (`simTime + dt/2`) — merging `Ephemeris.attractorsAt`'s `mu !== 0` subset with any living gravity-bearing `GameEntity` (`entities.attractors()`) into a single array — then `classifyAttractors` splits that array once into the `GRAVITY_ALWAYS_COUNT` heaviest bodies (`always`) and a `SpatialGrid` the rest are binned into (`physics/spatial-grid.ts`, cell size derived from the split — see `attractors.ts` below), and finally every live entity's own `stepActual(dt, attractorsNear(entity.state.r, classified))` reads only its own 27-neighbor cell block plus `always` (`game-entity/game-entity.ts`). Assembling the classified structure once and handing every entity a lookup against the same one (rather than each entity re-deriving its own) is a correctness requirement, not an optimization: two gravity-bearing entities integrated against structures built at different moments would see each other in slightly different positions, breaking the symmetry a mutual interaction must have. `Simulator` only iterates the arrays; the orbit integration itself is the entity's responsibility. It also owns the attitude integration for *all* entities including every self-flown ship (`stepAttitudes`). AI/input/spawn decisions live in `game.ts`/`stages/`/`Enemy.behave`, which add entities back in via `entities.addXxx`.
- `predictor.ts` — `Predictor`: a third updater over `EntityManager`'s arrays, symmetric with `Simulator` (①holds the arrays / ②steps them / ③predicts their future — `Predictor` is ③) rather than nested inside `Simulator`, so it doesn't inherit that class's `simTime` concerns or vice versa. Owned directly by `Game` and driven from `Game.update` right after `entities.cleanup(...)` (dead entities shouldn't get predicted, and it needs the post-integration real state to resync against) — with **no branch on viewpoint or mode**, since prediction runs whether or not anything is currently displaying it. The only state it holds is a round-robin `cursor` into `EntityManager.all()` plus the `tracked`/`complete`/`discarded` counters `?perf=1` reads via `Game.perfCounts()`. `update(simTime, player, horizon)` takes `horizon` from `DisplayTimeManager.durationSec(referencePeriod)` (how far the future-ghost slider can currently reach). **It is not gated on time warp at all**: the budget outruns real time at every warp level — `PREDICT_MIN_STEP_DT` floors the step at `SUBSTEP_MAX_DT`, so a `PREDICT_STEP_BUDGET` slice advances the active ship 10,000 sim-s per frame (600,000 sim-s per real second at 60fps) against the 131,072 the top warp level asks for. A gate here would stop growth while `resyncPrediction` kept discarding, leaving the list permanently absent for that warp level and silently falling back to `OrbitLine`'s analytic ellipse, whose radius disagrees with the integrated trajectory under J2/third-body/drag. It first calls `resyncPrediction` on every entity in `all()` (the active ship included, once, since `all()` doesn't repeat it) and tallies the counters — cheap (one binary search each). Budget spending then spends a `PREDICT_STEP_BUDGET` step budget on `player` explicitly first, then one pass through the *whole* `all()` (the active ship included again) starting at `cursor`, saving wherever it stopped as next frame's `cursor` — nothing filters the active ship out of the round-robin, so it can draw budget twice in the same frame. The private `advanceBudget(entity, budgetSteps, simTime, horizon)` returns 0 immediately for an entity with `predictsFuture === false`, so bullets/casings/debris never touch either attractor lookup here at all. For a predicting entity each iteration reads the entity's current predicted-tip state (`entity.predictedTrajectory?.state ?? entity.state`) and re-derives that tip's own gravity sources from scratch — `simulation/attractors.ts`'s `predictedAttractorsAt(ephemeris, entities, tipState.t)`, which evaluates every other gravity-bearing `GameEntity`'s own `displayState(tipState.t)` at that same tip time rather than freezing it at its current real state (a body whose prediction hasn't reached that time yet — `displayState` returns `null` — is simply dropped from that iteration's array, not substituted with a stale position), then `classifyAttractors`/`attractorsNear` the same way `Simulator.substep` does — and sizes `dt` as `max(PREDICT_MIN_STEP_DT, localOrbitPeriod(tip.r, attractors) / PREDICT_STEPS_PER_REV, horizon / PREDICT_MAX_STEPS)` (`attractor.ts`'s `localOrbitPeriod`, `PREDICT_STEPS_PER_REV` = 600 — the period around whichever body pulls hardest at the tip, divided into a fixed step count per revolution, the same scheme `plan-arc.ts`'s `stepDt` uses with its own `PLAN_ARC_STEPS_PER_REV`; the `horizon / PREDICT_MAX_STEPS` term only bites once `horizon` grows large), and calls `GameEntity.stepPredicted(attractors, simTime, dt, horizon)` — looping until either the budget runs out or a call returns `false` (horizon reached, truncated, or thrusting). Both the step-count loop and the per-step `dt`/`attractors` sizing are `Predictor`'s own, not `GameEntity`'s, matching how `Simulator.advance` decides `subDt` and owns the substep-count loop around `GameEntity.stepActual` rather than leaving either to the entity. It never asks *why* a list is short (freshly spawned vs. just invalidated) — that indifference is what keeps discards from ever spiking frame time, since a discarded list just competes for the same budget as a new one next frame.
  **The active ship is just `Game.player`'s current pointer into `players`**, not a separately-tracked identity — `players` holds every self-flown ship (stage mode's lone one included) on equal footing, and `Game.setActivePlayer` only ever reassigns which element `player` points at. Nothing filters the active ship out of `players` before processing it: `Simulator.substep`/`stepAttitudes` step every element of `players` uniformly, and `stepAttitudes` in particular integrates every array (players/enemies/casings/debris/ammos alike) at the same per-substep `subDt` — there is no separate, coarser attitude step for non-player entities, `EntityManager.cleanup`'s `checkLoss` pass covers `players` the same as any other array via `all()`, and `ContactPhysics.resolveSubstep` takes `entities.all()` (`players` included) as its participant list outright. The two places that still single the active ship out do so through the explicit `player` argument rather than by excluding it from an array: `Predictor.update` spends its first budget slice on `player` before round-robining the *whole* `entities.all()` (`player` included again) — the active ship can draw budget twice in one frame, deliberately, rather than being tracked and excluded from the round-robin by identity. `EntityManager.sync` is the one place `players` is genuinely skipped wholesale, since every ship (active or not) syncs itself via `Player.syncPlayer` instead (see `player.ts` above).
- `contact.ts` — `ContactPhysics`: rigid-sphere contact enumeration and resolution for every `collides` `GameEntity` plus the infinite-mass celestial bodies. `resolveSubstep(simTime, entities, attractors, activeStage)` runs once per substep (the warp gate `SimSpeedManager.canResolvePhysicalCollisions` is checked by `Simulator.advance` before calling, not here); `resolveBelt(dt, ...)` is separate because the belt is a ship-local simulation solved at the real frame `dt`, so it is resolved once per frame outside the substep loop. Candidate pairs are narrowed through one `physics/spatial-grid.ts` `SpatialGrid` per call — cell size = twice the largest radius plus the largest per-substep movement among that call's participants, so no pair separated by more than a cell diagonal can overlap or have swept past each other — the same 27-neighbor pattern `simulation/attractors.ts` uses for gravity, applied to contact instead. Within one substep the contacts are resolved in ascending TOI order, each resolution re-deriving the remaining candidates' TOI, and any participant whose position/velocity/radius/mass is non-finite (or whose mass is not positive) is dropped before it can corrupt a grid cell index. **It resolves the impulse and hands both parties a `Contact`, and nothing else** — damage, sound, destruction and effects are each `GameEntity` subclass's own `collideWith`, so a bullet's damage and a hull's reaction are written on their own sides rather than branched on here. `Contact` is stated from the receiver's point of view (`self`/`other` swapped and `normal` flipped per side), so one resolution yields both parties' descriptions. `BeltSection` (the belt's contact proxy) is defined in `player/belt-physics.ts` and `RadiatorFold` in `player/radiator.ts`, not here. An `Asteroid` is not special-cased — it takes part on exactly the same footing as an `Enemy` or a pickup, and whether an object also pulls gravitationally (`mu`) plays no role in whether or how it bounces.
- `attractors.ts` — the gravity source array for one simulation/prediction/plan step, and the spatial classification that keeps summing it cheap. `mergeAttractors(bodies, dynamic)` is the one array-spread primitive (never mutating the `Ephemeris` ring-cache array it read from) the rest of the module and `plan-arc.ts` build on. `gravityBodiesAt(ephemeris, t)` narrows `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)` (the full analytic-body window) down to `mu !== 0` — a body's gravity-source status is decided by this one filter, nowhere else. `attractorsAt(ephemeris, entities, t)` merges that with `EntityManager.attractors()` (the living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`s, most notably `Asteroid`); `Simulator.substep` calls it once per substep, at the substep's own midpoint, and hands the one resulting array to every entity in that substep, so two gravity-bearing entities integrated together see each other at the same instant — computing it separately per entity would let their mutual pull disagree by whichever one moved first. `predictedAttractorsAt(ephemeris, entities, t)` is `Predictor`'s own sibling: it re-derives each dynamic entity's state at `t` via `displayState(t)` rather than reusing its current real state, dropping any entity whose prediction hasn't reached `t` yet — evaluating at the tip's own moment is the correctness requirement here, since a prediction budget pass has no shared "this substep" instant to synchronize dynamic bodies against the way `Simulator` does. `PlanArc` (`plan-arc.ts`) takes a third path: its `dynamicAttractors` argument is `entities.attractors()` frozen once at the moment `Game`/`PlanEditor` call `update`, then reused for the segment's whole (up to year-long) integration — a planned trajectory doesn't track a nearby `Asteroid`'s own future motion (a documented limit, `DEVELOP/SPEC.md`).

  `classifyAttractors(attractors)` sorts one such array into `{always, grid}`: the `GRAVITY_ALWAYS_COUNT` heaviest bodies by `μ` go into `always`, the rest are inserted into a `physics/spatial-grid.ts` `SpatialGrid` keyed on their own position — a value comparison made once per body per step, not once per (body, query point) pair. `attractorsNear(pos, classified)` is what every entity actually integrates against: `always` plus whatever `SpatialGrid.neighbors(pos)` returns for `pos`'s own cell and its 26 neighbors — a body sorted into the grid contributes only to query points within roughly one cell of it, which is safe because a query point farther than that has an even smaller ECI origin-correction term to lose (see `spatial-grid.ts`'s own comment).

  **Both the `μ` cut and the cell size are derived from the array itself each call** (module-private `alwaysThresholdMu`/`gridCellSize`), so the two tuning constants are a *count* and an *accuracy*, not two lengths: `GRAVITY_ALWAYS_COUNT` fixes how many bodies every entity sums unconditionally, and the cell size follows as `sqrt(heaviest gridded μ / GRAVITY_NEGLIGIBLE_ACCEL)` — the distance at which that body's pull has fallen to the tolerance. The approximation rests on mass in a star system being Zipf-distributed: almost all of it sits in the star and a handful of bodies, so a cut a few ranks deep leaves a remainder small enough that its reach is short. The threshold is a `μ` **value** rather than a rank so equal-`μ` bodies (a field of identical rocks) can never be split arbitrarily by sort order; the count may therefore exceed the target on ties. Because `μ` is constant per body, the `always` set is stable frame to frame.

  Two constraints on the count. **It must cover the perturbations the game depends on** — under the default registry the Moon is only the 14th heaviest body (the Galilean moons and Titan all outweigh it), so a count below that drops the lunar term for ships outside cislunar space; at 15 there is one rank of margin. And **the `μ` order is not the order of importance**: a body's own contribution splits into a direct pull `μ/d²` and the ECI origin correction `μ/D²` (`D` = its distance from the origin body), the latter being *independent of the query point* — the Moon's is 3.8e-5 m/s², the largest non-central term in the system, which is why it must never be gridded. Any criterion keyed on the **entity's** distance from the origin is forbidden however tempting the pruning: the simulation must behave the same wherever the origin body sits, floating-point error aside. Every gravity-integration call site (`Simulator.substep`, `Predictor.advanceBudget`, `PlanArc.integrate`'s sizing and stepping evaluations) runs its own `classifyAttractors` → `attractorsNear` pair rather than sharing a `ClassifiedAttractors`, since each call site's array (and often its evaluation time) differs.

`src/game/camera/` — every camera holds a `viewpoint: Viewpoint` field (`physics/projection.ts` — `position`/`lookTarget`/`up`/`fovDeg`/`aspect`) and an `update` that computes the viewpoint in absolute ECI (THREE-free) and writes it into `viewpoint` — no camera returns its `Viewpoint` as a value, since a function that both mutates state and hands back a derived product is the anti-pattern CLAUDE.md's TypeScript rules ban. Pushing `viewpoint` into a `THREE.PerspectiveCamera` through the floating origin (`sync`) and building the `ProjectFn` the rest of the game reads screen positions through (independent of both the THREE matrices and `fo`) are **`CameraSystem`'s job alone** — no sub-camera has its own `sync`/`projection`. Each sub-camera also exposes a `near: number` getter (a plain derived-value read, not a THREE write, so it doesn't cross that boundary) — `CameraSystem.sync` reads the active one and is the only place it reaches a `THREE.PerspectiveCamera.near`.
- `camera-system.ts` — `CameraSystem`: owns `CombatCameraSystem`/`OverviewCamera` and exposes the active one (`activeCamera`/`activeCameraPos`/`activeCameraProjection`); owns `overviewMode` (`zoomActive` is a forwarding getter — `!overviewMode && combatCamera.zoomActive` — since only `CombatCameraSystem` holds and updates that state). Module-private `syncCameraToViewpoint(camera, view, near, fo)` and `projectionFromViewpoint(view)` are the sole place any sub-camera's `viewpoint` becomes a `THREE.PerspectiveCamera` update or a `ProjectFn` — `sync(fo)` calls the former on whichever of `combatCamera`/`overviewCamera` is active, and `activeCameraProjection` calls the latter. `ScaleFn` (`(worldPos: Vec3) => number`, metres per screen pixel at that point) is the exact sibling of `ProjectFn` — module-private `scaleFromViewpoint(view)` binds `physics/projection.ts`'s `metersPerPixel` the same way, and `activeCameraScale` picks the active camera's viewpoint the same way, so a consumer that needs to size something in screen units receives a bound function as an argument rather than reaching for the camera. `update(player, simTime, input, dt, mapPickables)` also reads the roll keys (`K.cameraRollLeft`/`cameraRollRight`, mapped to the numeric-keypad `Numpad0`/`Numpad1` — distinct from the ship radiator toggles on the top-row `Digit9`/`Digit0`) and forwards `keyRoll` alongside `keyYaw`/`keyPitch` to whichever sub-camera is active. `mapPickables` (built fresh each frame by `MapPicker.refresh()`) is threaded through to `OverviewCamera.update` so its focus resolves against the same candidate set `MapPicker.handleRightClick` picks against — `CameraSystem` itself holds none of that set. Also owns the map focus concern (= "where the overview camera looks"): the `focusMarkers` label set — resolving a right-click against the shared candidate set is `MapPicker.handleRightClick`'s job via `map-pick.ts`'s `pickNearest` directly, so this class no longer wraps that lookup itself — and the **MAP VIEW panel** (`camera/overview-camera-panel.ts` — the per-class `BodyClassToggles` icon/label/orbit-line buttons plus the ammo-pickup marker toggle; no camera-frame or focus control of its own — origin/rotation for both the camera and the planned trajectory are `src/game/frame-controls.ts`'s job, and focus is chosen via `MapPicker`'s always-shown object list, a double-click, or a property window's "move focus" item). Every control on that panel writes `overviewCamera`/`Hud.settings`/`bodyClassToggles` state and nothing else, so the whole HUD wiring stays inside this class; `sync(fo)` pushes the panel's visibility and highlights each frame. `bodyClassToggles`' initial value is likewise read from `localStorage` (`tepui.bodyClassToggles`, `loadBodyClassToggles`) and written back on every toggle (`saveBodyClassToggles`), so it too survives a page reload alongside `Navball.gridVisibility`. The class-toggle section is one row per category (惑星/衛星/準惑星/小天体/ラグランジュ点, `BODY_CLASS_ROWS`), each holding `hud/buttons.ts`'s `IconToggleButton`(s) for that category's icon(●)/label(Aa)/orbit-line(⌒) `BodyClassToggles` fields — orbit-line is omitted for 衛星 (no such toggle exists, see `body-visibility.ts` below) and for ラグランジュ点 (no orbit concept at all), so those two rows show only two buttons. `serialize()`/`restore(d)` forward to `CombatCameraSystem`/`OverviewCamera`'s own `serialize()`/`restore(d)`, so a snapshot carries both sub-cameras' viewpoints regardless of which is active — `game.ts`'s `restore` calls this after `ViewManager.restoreView` has already switched to the saved view.
- `combat-camera-system.ts` — `CombatCameraSystem`: the combat view, holding its own `camera: THREE.PerspectiveCamera` and `viewpoint: Viewpoint` (the same `Viewpoint` shape `OverviewCamera` holds, so there's no second "camera view state" shape in the codebase). Owns the `[Z]` gunsight switch (`zoomActive`, read from `Input` each `update` — the key is only ever consulted while combat view is active, since `CameraSystem` doesn't call this class's `update` in map mode) and is the sole place FOV animates: each frame it picks `chase-camera.ts`'s `ChaseCamera` or `gunsight-camera.ts`'s `GunsightCamera` by `zoomActive`, calls that sub-camera's `update` (which writes its own `viewpoint`), then module-private `lerpViewpointFov` folds the picked `viewpoint` into this class's own — position/lookTarget/up/aspect snap to the target immediately, only `fovDeg` eases exponentially — so a sub-camera never animates its own FOV and a future third view only needs to expose the same `viewpoint: Viewpoint` field. `toggleFollowAttitude`/`camFollowAttitude` are thin forwards to `ChaseCamera` (the `[G]` key's receiver, same pattern as `reset()`) — the reference-frame decision itself lives there.
- `chase-camera.ts` — `ChaseCamera`: the third-person orbit view, owning only a unit quaternion `rot` and `dist` — no Euler yaw/pitch/up-vector state, so a long drag session can't gimbal-lock or drift into an inconsistent up vector. It holds its own `player: Player` reference (constructor-injected — `game.ts` constructs `Player` before `CameraSystem`/`CombatCameraSystem`/`ChaseCamera` specifically so this reference can be a direct one rather than a lazily-resolved thunk) and reads `player.att.q`/`player.alive`/`player.state.r` directly, so `update` takes no `player` argument. `setPlayer(player)` re-points this reference — `Game.setActivePlayer` calls it through `CameraSystem.setActivePlayer` → `CombatCameraSystem.setActivePlayer`, the same forwarding chain every layer between `Game` and this field uses — without touching `rot`/`dist`, so switching the flown ship keeps the camera's current offset instead of snapping to a default view. The togglable reference frame (`camFollowAttitude`, a get/set accessor) decides what `rot` *means*: `true` — the default — makes it an offset relative to the ship's own attitude (`player.att.q`); `false` makes it the camera's own absolute orientation in ECI, fixed in inertial space until dragged.
