The setter reinterprets `rot` through `player.att.q` (multiply/divide) so the rendered view never jumps at the moment of the toggle. `update(mouse, keyYaw, keyPitch, keyRoll, dt)` folds `player.att.q` into a working quaternion only while `camFollowAttitude && player.alive` — once the ship is destroyed the stored `rot` is used as-is (an absolute orientation), so the camera stops chasing the ship's post-destruction tumble — then applies key/drag rotations to that working quaternion before writing it back. Mouse drag rotates around `axis = normalize(cross(dragVector, viewVector))` (`viewVector` = the camera's current look direction, `dragVector` = the drag's screen delta expressed in the camera's own current right/up axes): the axis is always ⟂ the view direction by construction, so drag can never introduce roll around the view axis, and using the *current* basis rather than a fixed reference keeps the control gimbal-lock-free at any pitch. Arrow-key yaw/pitch rotate around the same current up/right axes; `keyRoll` (the numpad-0/1 keys) rotates around `view` itself (the camera's own look direction), the one axis arrow-key yaw/pitch never touch. `serialize()`/`restore(d)` write `rot`/`dist`/`panEci`/`camFollowAttitude` straight into a `ChaseCameraSaveData` and back — `restore` assigns `rot`/`_camFollowAttitude` directly rather than through the `camFollowAttitude` setter, since the setter's read-normalize is for a *live* frame switch and would reinterpret a value that is already correct for the saved frame.
- `gunsight-camera.ts` — `GunsightCamera`: the `[Z]` zoom view — stateless apart from `viewpoint`, just the ship's nose direction and position turned into a `Viewpoint` each `update`.
- `overview-camera.ts` — `OverviewCamera`: the map Earth-centered orbit camera (near=`dist / OVERVIEW_CAMERA_NEAR_RATIO` clamped so it never grows past the star/celestial-grid shell at extreme zoom-out, so the near plane tightens as the camera zooms in rather than clipping a close focus at a fixed distance. The clamp is not a flat fraction of `CELESTIAL_SHELL_RADIUS`: a shell point at view-axis angle θ from screen center clips at view-space depth `R·cosθ`, so the cap has to be `R·cos(θ_diag)·OVERVIEW_CAMERA_NEAR_SHELL_MARGIN`, θ_diag being the screen's diagonal half-FOV derived from `fov`/`window.innerWidth/innerHeight` each call — a flat `R`-fraction cap only protects the narrow region near screen center and leaves the wide-aspect edges/corners clipped/far=`dist × OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_RATIO` clamped to `[OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_MIN, OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_MAX]`, so the far plane also tracks zoom instead of sitting at a fixed distance), exposing `camera`/`viewpoint` on the same footing as `CombatCameraSystem`. Each sub-camera (this one and `CombatCameraSystem`) exposes its own `far` getter — the combat camera's is the fixed `COMBAT_CAMERA_FAR` — and `CameraSystem.sync` writes the active one's `far` into the `THREE.PerspectiveCamera` every frame, the same way it already does for `near`. Its source of truth is three **`cameraFrame`-relative** vectors (`offset_r` = focus→camera, `pan_r` = focus→look-target, `up_r` = the camera's up direction), so switching to a rotating frame makes the viewpoint co-rotate for free; the ECI ⇄ frame round trip is delegated to `physics/frame.ts` and the class never brands values itself. `cameraFrame` is a read-only getter for **the frame the viewpoint is fixed to** — the frame the planned trajectory is *drawn* in is `PlanDisplay.planFrame`, a separate value selected independently — and `setCameraRotation(rotatingWith: OrbitingId | null)` is the sole way to change it: it always resolves to `ephemeris.frameOf(ephemeris.originId, rotatingWith)`, keeping the frame's *center* pinned to `ephemeris.originId` regardless of `rotatingWith` (the three basis vectors are directions, not points, so only the rotation ever affects the viewpoint) and re-projects the current view into the new frame so the switch never jumps the visible camera. `update` rotates the viewpoint around the camera's *current* up/right axes (the same construction `ChaseCamera` uses) rather than around world Y, so a rolled map still yaws and pitches the way the drag looks on screen, and it re-orthogonalizes `up_r` against the just-changed view direction before applying `keyRoll` around it. Focus is a `camera/focus-target.ts` `FocusTarget` — either `{kind:'object', id}`, re-resolved every frame against the `candidates: readonly MapPickable[]` argument `update` receives (built by `MapPicker.refresh()` — Earth/Moon/Sun/Lagrange-point labels plus every living ship plus the navigation-target AN/DN icons), falling back to Earth once the id is missing for two consecutive frames; or `{kind:'point', frame, point}`, a `physics/frame.ts` `FramePoint` baked into a chosen `ReferenceFrame` and resolved back to ECI every frame via `frameTransformAt` — a rotating `frame` makes the fixed point ride that frame's own rotation instead of sitting still in ECI. `setFocusTarget(target, resetPan=true)` is the sole entry point for changing focus (replacing separate id/position setters), and `resolvedFocus` exposes the ECI position `resolveFocus` last settled on — `frame-controls.ts`'s "固定を解除" action reads it to bake a fresh `'point'` target in the star-centered inertial frame (or `ephemeris.inertialFrame` when the registry has no star) at the instant of release, so the camera keeps looking exactly where it was rather than jumping to Earth. `clearFocusIf(id)` only matches an `'object'` target, since a `'point'` target carries no id an object's disappearance could invalidate. The class holds no reference to `FocusMarkers` or any other pickable source itself. The future-ghost slider belongs to `DisplayTimeManager`. `serialize()`/`restore(d)` write `offset_r`/`pan_r`/`up_r`, `cameraFrame.rotatingWith`, and `focus` into/from an `OverviewCameraSaveData` — a `'point'` focus is saved as its baking `ReferenceFrame`'s `center`/`rotatingWith` plus the frame-relative point, and `restore` resolves that frame (and `cameraFrame` itself) through `ephemeris.frameOf` rather than a literal, per `frame.ts`'s reference-identity rule.
- `focus-markers.ts` — `FocusMarkers`: the focusable-target labels, each exposed as a `MapPickable` (`kind: 'body'`). The full label set is built once in the constructor from the active registry, **in hierarchical order** (a body, then its Lagrange labels, then its children — each carrying a `depth` so a consumer can indent without re-deriving the tree; `ObjectListPanel` uses exactly this) — one label per body, plus Lagrange-point labels for the bodies whose points are **dynamically meaningful**: `Ephemeris.hasUsableCollinearPoints` (L1/L2/L3, gated on the L1 clearance from the secondary's own surface exceeding `LAGRANGE_MIN_CLEARANCE_RATIO`) and `Ephemeris.hasStableTriangularPoints` (L4/L5, Routh's linear-stability mass ratio) are asked per body, so a system can legitimately get L4/L5 but not L1/L2/L3 — except for `body-class.ts`'s `'smallBody'`/`'dwarf'` classes, which cap out at L1/L2 regardless (too numerous for L3/L4/L5 to stay legible, and neither dynamically as interesting for these low-mass bodies). Names come out as `${primary name}-${id name} L${n}`, so registering a new body needs no change here. Split across the two phases like `NavTarget` is: `update(t, focusId, toggles)` (`focusId: AttractorId | undefined` — `undefined` when the camera is fixed on a `'point'` `FocusTarget` rather than a body, in which case `visibleBodyIds` falls back to its focus-independent rules) re-derives positions **only for the bodies `celestial/body-visibility.ts`'s `visibleBodyIds` currently admits** — an invisible body costs no `positionOf`/`lagrangeAt` at all, which is what keeps the per-frame cost bounded as the registry grows — and stamps each shown `FocusLabel`'s `showIcon`/`showLabel` from `alwaysFullyVisibleIds`/`bodyIconLabel` (a body inside `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` gets both true regardless of toggles, matching the class's independent icon/label toggle otherwise); `syncLabels(project, cameraPos)` places the markers off the toggle-gated subset `update()` just computed, passing `mk-poi`'s `sym`/`label` arguments as `''` for whichever of `showIcon`/`showLabel` is off (so the dot and the name can appear independently) and hiding a label the previous frame showed but this one dropped — `CameraSystem` calls the two back to back so the drawn markers are the same frame's coordinates. `allLabels` is exposed publicly too (the same array, id/isLagrange/name only, unconditional) — `MapPicker.sync()`'s parent-lookup construction reads it directly rather than the toggle-gated subset, since a row hidden on the map by a toggle must still nest correctly in the 軌道オブジェクト一覧's tree. `allBodyPickables(t)` is a separate, unconditional position lookup (every registered body plus every dynamically-meaningful Lagrange point, regardless of `toggles`/`focusId`) `MapPicker.refresh()` uses to seed its whole candidate list — so the map's own drawn labels stay toggle-gated (decluttered) while the 軌道オブジェクト一覧 and its own click/search/filter always sees every registered object, independent of what the map currently draws or occludes. An overview-camera-side concern, owned by `CameraSystem`.
- `focus-target.ts` — `FocusTarget`: `OverviewCamera`'s focus as a judged union, `{kind:'object', id}` (a body/ship/other `MapPickable`, re-resolved by id every frame) or `{kind:'point', frame, point}` (an ECI position baked once into a `physics/frame.ts` `FramePoint` within a chosen `ReferenceFrame`, re-resolved to ECI every frame via `frameTransformAt` so a rotating `frame` carries the point along). `focusTargetId(target)` is the sole way to ask "does this focus correspond to a body" — `undefined` for `'point'` — used everywhere a focus id feeds a body-keyed lookup (`visibleBodyIds`, `EnvironmentScene`'s reference-line focus, `ObjectListPanel`'s row highlight). `focusPoint(ephemeris, frame, pos, t)` builds a `'point'` target from a live ECI position at time `t`; `frame-controls.ts`'s "固定を解除" action is its only caller.

Screen projection itself is **not** in `camera/`: `physics/projection.ts` holds the pure math (`Viewpoint`/`projectToNdc`/`ndcToScreen`/`Projected`) and each camera exposes a bound `ProjectFn` (`(worldPos: Vec3) => Projected`) that consumers receive as an argument rather than importing.

- `src/game/map-pick.ts` — `MapPickable`/`MapPickKind` (`'body' | 'ship' | 'player' | 'apsis' | 'relnode'`) and `pickNearest<T extends { readonly pos: Vec3 }>`: the common shape for anything on the map (or in combat) that a click can hit, and the shared "project every candidate, take the screen-nearest one within a pixel-radius" search. The type parameter only requires a world position, not the full `MapPickable` shape, so a caller with its own payload (e.g. `Targeter.pickEnemyAt`'s `{ pos, enemy }`) can pass it straight through without faking the rest of `MapPickable`. `'body'` is `FocusMarkers`' celestial labels, `'ship'` is every living `Enemy`, `'player'` is every living self-flown ship in `entities.players` — split from `'ship'` because a self-flown ship's menu offers activate/follow-plan/delete on top of what an enemy's does — `'relnode'` is `NavTarget`'s relative AN/DN icons, and `'apsis'` is `PlanDisplay`'s periapsis/apoapsis icons — each kind drives a different `MapPicker.itemsFor` item set. Used by `MapPicker` (the map's whole right-click path) and `Targeter.pickEnemyAt` (enemies only), so target-selection and map-focus/context-menu picking share one implementation instead of several near-identical screen-distance loops.
  A second `DynamicTrajectory` — `predictedTrajectory` — is the entity's own future trajectory, driven incrementally by `game/simulation/`'s `Predictor` (below) rather than recomputed from scratch each frame: `stepPredicted(attractors, simTime, dt, horizon)` lazily creates it from `actualTrajectory.state` if absent, then advances its tip by exactly the caller-supplied `dt` (one RK4 step) against the caller-supplied `attractors` and returns whether it stepped. Sizing `dt`, looking up `attractors` for that tip, and deciding `horizon` are all `Predictor`'s job, not this method's — the same split as `Simulator` deciding `subDt` before calling `stepActual`. It refuses the step (returns `false`) once `predictedTrajectory.state.t` already exceeds `simTime + horizon` — so the tip always ends up one step *past* the horizon rather than short of it, which is what lets `at(t)` answer exactly `simTime + horizon` by interpolation instead of needing a clamped final step (clamping would make the prediction's resolution silently depend on whether the tip happened to have caught up to the horizon yet). It also refuses to grow (returns `false`) while `thrust !== null` (a thrusting free-flight prediction is meaningless and would just be invalidated next frame anyway) and permanently stops (sets `truncated`, cleared only when a fresh `predictedTrajectory` is created) if the integrated position sinks into any body's surface (`attractor.ts`'s `hitCelestialBody`, margin `REENTRY_ALT`) or goes non-finite — without that, a re-entering or NaN-corrupted entity would burn its budget forever without ever reaching the horizon. `resyncPrediction(simTime, attractors, horizon)` is the cheap per-frame correctness check, returning whether it discarded: if `predictedTrajectory.at(simTime)` is absent or has drifted from `actualTrajectory.state.r` by more than the tolerance, it calls `invalidatePrediction()` (drops `predictedTrajectory` — a zero-cost operation; the list simply regrows through the normal budget next frame, which is why discards never spike frame time). The tolerance is **not** a flat `PREDICT_RESET_DIST`: `resyncTolerance` widens it to whatever `at()`'s own cubic-Hermite interpolation error is at the list's current sample spacing (`PREDICT_SAMPLE_ERROR` scaled by the fourth power of how much `PREDICT_MAX_SAMPLES` has coarsened that spacing past `TRAJECTORY_SAMPLES_PER_REV`). Without that, a long display duration decimates the list far enough that a *correct* prediction reads as drifted — at the 28-day preset the interpolation alone is tens of km against a 500 m threshold — and every entity would discard and restart its list every single frame, so the prediction could never finish growing. `predictsFuture` (`false` by default; `Ship`/`Ammo` set it `true`) only decides *whether* an entity predicts at all — how far ahead is `horizon`, an argument threaded down from `DisplayTimeManager.durationSec(referencePeriod)` every frame, not a per-class constant, since the prediction horizon now tracks whatever span the future-ghost slider can reach. The payoff is `displayState(t)`: `t <= actualTrajectory.state.t ? actualTrajectory.at(t) : (predictedTrajectory?.at(t) ?? null)` — a two-way branch (past vs. future, nothing else) that `sync` (below) and every marker-position lookup route through, so "what does this entity look like at the currently-displayed time" has exactly one answer for meshes and markers alike.
- `src/physics/trajectory-features.ts` — pure functions that find orbital features from an already-integrated trajectory, rather than from the osculating `OrbitalElements` at some evaluation epoch. It exists because osculating elements oscillate with the epoch under J2 — tens of km of periapsis/apoapsis wobble per revolution at LEO — so an analytically-placed icon jumps every time the evaluation epoch moves, e.g. when a zero-Δv maneuver node is added. `apsisCrossing(center, prev, next)` finds a periapsis/apoapsis directly on the physical condition that defines one — the radial velocity relative to `center` (`dot(r - center.r, v - center.v)`) changing sign — checked on a single pair of *consecutive* integration steps rather than scanned out of a decimated sample array; a negative-to-positive crossing is a periapsis, positive-to-negative is an apoapsis, refined with a fixed 20-iteration golden-section search over `kinematic-state.ts`'s `hermiteInterpolate` between the two steps — the same interpolation `DynamicTrajectory.at`/`render/sampled-line.ts` already use, not a second one. Working on the raw step pair rather than a coarse sample array is what makes a genuine collision course (radial velocity stays negative all the way to impact, never crossing zero) correctly report no periapsis at all, instead of a coarse sample array spuriously reading an earlier, non-final close approach as the periapsis. `findEquatorCrossings(samples, center, pole)` finds the ascending/descending sign change of distance-from-center projected onto `pole` by scanning an already-integrated sample series (a bracketing triple by sign pattern), refined by a fixed 20-iteration bisection — a different feature with a different consumer (`game/marker/equator-node-markers.ts`), which has only a decimated sample series to work from rather than a live step pair. Both iteration counts are constants rather than convergence-driven, since a variable iteration count would let the result wobble frame to frame by itself. Pure functions, no game state.
- `src/physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts` — `DynamicTrajectory`: THREE/game-independent (`physics/`, alongside `StateQueue`/`Ephemeris` as "pure but stateful"), holding **one** `KinematicState` as `state` plus a decimated `StateQueue` of samples strictly older than it (`history`). `step(dt, bodies, bcInv, thrust, sampleInterval, keepDuration)` advances via `dynamics.ts`'s `stepDynamics` against the caller-supplied gravity-source array (`bodies` — the caller's own `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)` result; which timestamp to sample it at, e.g. the step's midpoint, is the caller's policy, not this class's) — then — only if `keepDuration > 0` and the about-to-be-overwritten `state` is at least `sampleInterval` seconds past the newest recorded sample — pushes that pre-step state into `history` and caps the window with `history.cleanup(keepDuration, 2)` (this is where the display-resolution decimation happens; `keepDuration = 0` never touches `history` at all, so casings/debris/ammo cost nothing). `prevState` (needed every step by `simulation/contact.ts`'s swept-sphere TOI test and `targeter.ts`'s board crossings, even for entities with `keepDuration = 0`) is tracked in its own field, separate from the throttled `history` — it's simply "the state one `step`/`reset` ago". `reset(state)` is the discontinuous-overwrite counterpart to `step`: it discards any `history` samples at-or-after the new state's time (`StateQueue.discardFrom`) to keep the "history is older than state" invariant, and still advances `prevState`. `at(t)` answers any time within the retained span — `t === state.t` returns `state` itself, newer than `history.newest` but older than `state` interpolates between the two via `kinematic-state.ts`'s `hermiteInterpolate`, and anything older delegates to `history.at(t)` — so callers never need to reconcile the queue and the live state themselves. `samplesOldestFirst()` flattens the retained span into the oldest-first `readonly KinematicState[]` that `render/sampled-line.ts` wants, joining `history` and `state` in the one place that owns the invariant between them, and memoizes the result — repeated calls between a `step`/`reset` pair return the same array reference, which is what lets `SampledLine.syncGeometry`'s reference-equality re-bake guard actually skip work; `step`/`reset` both invalidate the memo since both change what the array would contain. This class holds no orbital-elements memo of its own — a state's osculating elements around a chosen body are `game-entity/game-entity.ts`'s `GameEntity.orbitalElementsAround`, since which body to center on is a per-caller decision this class has no stake in. The class is deliberately direction-agnostic — the same step/at/record operations back a **past** span (`GameEntity.actualTrajectory`), a **future** one (`GameEntity.predictedTrajectory`, whose `history` spans "now → prediction horizon"), and a **planned** one (`plan/plan-arc.ts`'s segment). There is no second implementation of "advance a timed state while recording decimated samples" anywhere in the codebase, and adding one would be a mistake.
- `ship.ts` — `Ship` (abstract; adds name/hp on top of the base `GameEntity.radius` (the constructor's own `radius` argument, `PLAYER_HULL_RADIUS`/`ENEMY_RADIUS` — used for both bullet-hit and rigid-contact geometry, with no separate ship-specific hit radius), `bcInv = SHIP_BCINV`, `historyDuration = SHIP_HISTORY_DURATION` — the only class that keeps a history — and `predictsFuture = true`). Base for `Player`/`Enemy`. Also holds the two HP marker glyphs `markerItem` picks between: `hpMarkerSvg()` (combat view — a triangle outline with per-edge HP notches, meaningful only unrotated) and `headingHpMarkerSvg()` (map view — a triangle whose outline stays fixed while an inner fill rises from the base by HP ratio, so rotating it to face the heading direction never garbles the reading). **It also owns the parts model, and parts are the single source of truth for both health and performance.** `initDefaultParts()` splits the ship's nominal `maxHp` across a fixed `DEFAULT_PART_HP_RATIO` table (summing to 1) and then re-derives `hp`/`maxHp` from the parts it actually built, so the two can never disagree by a rounding step; radiators and solar panels come in **two parts each** because the ship physically has a left and a right panel (`radiatorParts`/`solarParts` return them in `RadiatorSide` order — `'up'` first). All damage funnels through `applyDamageToParts(amount, part?)`, which applies the best armor part's `damageReduction` and then either hits the caller-named part (when the hit location identifies one, e.g. a radiator panel) or a random surviving part; `updateOverallHp()` then sets `hp` to the sum of every part's remaining HP, overridden to 0 the moment the hull or the cockpit is gone. Performance is read the same way — `totalThrust`/`totalTorque`/`totalFuel`/`totalCoolingRate`/`totalPowerGeneration`/`totalFireRate`/`averageMuzzleVelocity` are what the throttle, thermal, power and gun systems actually consume, so swapping a part in the dock changes behaviour with no further wiring. The default thruster's `thrust` is defined as `PLAYER_MASS × max(THROTTLE_LEVELS)` precisely so a stock ship's real acceleration equals the number the HUD prints. `Bullet` carries the firing weapon's `damage`, so what a hit takes off is decided by the shooter's parts rather than a global constant. **`Part.weight` is still unread** — `mass` remains a per-class field (`Player` 1000 kg, `Enemy` 10000 kg), so a heavier part costs nothing yet; wiring it needs a decision on how an enemy's mass relates to its parts.

  The node-vs-context-menu split: in map mode `game.ts` calls `MapPicker.handleRightClick`/`handleLeftClick`/`handleDoubleClick` before `PlanEditor.handleMapPointer`, so a right-click that lands on a marker (celestial label/ship/apsis/AN-DN icon) is claimed by `MapPicker` first; only a right-click that misses every marker reaches the editor's own node hit test, and whatever that also misses falls through to `MapPicker.handleEmptySpaceRightClick` last — the priority is expressed purely as call order, and the gizmos never reference each other.

`src/game/plan/` — maneuver planning and its future-trajectory *display*, independent of whether the map is open. "When to look" (display duration, the future-ghost slider) is a separate, `game/`-root concern (`DisplayTimeManager` below) from "what a plan looks like when you look" (this folder):
- `src/game/docking.ts` — `Docking`: the base rendezvous/dock/launch cross-cut (proximity detection spans `Base`+every `Player`, the dock/launch transition spans `EntityManager`+`CameraSystem`+`Game`'s active-player pointer+the `DockView` UI — no single one of those owns it, so it gets its own module, the same shape as `MapPicker`/`ViewManager`). Owns `DockView` (`hud/dock-view.ts`) and wires its `onLaunchShip`/`onClose` callbacks. `selectBase(base)` marks a base as the one `canEnterDock()` (and thus the `ViewBadge`'s view selector) will accept without itself changing the current view — `MapPicker.handleLeftClick`'s base case and the property window's own "ドックビューを開く" item both funnel through it; `activate(base)` is `selectBase` plus an immediate `viewManager.setView('dock')`, kept as the one call that actually opens the dock. `checkProximity()` (called from `Game.update` once per frame, skipped while `dockView.visible`) walks `entities.bases` × `entities.players` and docks a ship once it's within `DOCK_CAPTURE_DIST` at under `DOCK_CAPTURE_REL_V` relative speed. Docking pushes a `DockedShipEntry` onto `base.baseState.dockedShips`, sets `ship.alive = false`, and calls the new `EntityManager.parkPlayer` (splices out of `players` without disposing — `removePlayer`'s `dispose()` would destroy the mesh/VFX permanently, which a ship that will fly again can't afford); because a parked ship's `syncPlayer` no longer runs to derive its own visibility from `alive`, `Docking` sets `ship.obj.visible = false` once at dock time as the one discontinuous mesh edit the transition needs (mirroring the scene-mutating lifecycle calls `EntityManager.removePlayer`/`dispose` already make from inside `Game.update`'s own entity-cleanup passes). The same "no more per-frame updates" gap used to leave the shared engine/RCS loop sounds stuck on if the docked ship was the active one and was thrusting/rotating at capture — `PlayerThrottle.updateThrustState`/`updateTorque` are the only things that ever silence `Sfx.setThrust`/`setRcs`, and those stop being called for a parked ship the instant it leaves `entities.players` (a differently-active ship would eventually re-silence them next frame, but the last-ship-docked case had nothing left to do it). `dock()` now calls `sfx.setThrust(false)`/`setRcs(false)` itself whenever `wasActive`, unconditionally, so this can't depend on what (if anything) becomes active next. Launching reverses it: a fresh `KinematicState` near the base, `alive = true`, `entities.addPlayer`, `Game.setActivePlayer` — no explicit `obj.visible = true` needed, since the ship is back in `entities.players` before this frame's `sync` runs and `syncPlayer` recomputes visibility from `alive` on its own. `buildShip` (wired to `DockView.onBuildShip`) is the same "docked but not flying" state reached from the opposite direction: it constructs a brand-new `Player` at the base's own state, immediately sets `alive = false`/`obj.visible = false`, and pushes a `DockedShipEntry` — no rendezvous needed because the ship never entered `entities.players` in the first place. `DockView` collects the price and calls back only once payment is settled, so `Docking` never has to know about money.
- `src/game/map-picker.ts` — `MapPicker`: everything about the map's selectable objects — the frame's single candidate set, the right-click hit test, the per-kind menu, and the dispatch of the chosen action to its owner. `refresh(simTime, displayTime)` re-derives `FocusMarkers`' celestial-label positions and `NavTarget`'s AN/DN (both are part of the set, so the pairing is kept inside rather than imposed on callers) and rebuilds `pickables`: `FocusMarkers`' celestial labels + every living self-flown ship (`'player'`) and enemy (`'ship'`), each at its `displayState(displayTime)` so a picked ship sits at the same future-ghost position as its own mesh, + `navTarget.mapPickables()` + `PlanDisplay.apsisMarkers` + `Game.equatorNodeMarkers.mapPickables()`. **The candidate set stays one list** — splitting it per kind guarantees a consumer that only reads one slice, which is how "the menu opens but focus jumps to Earth" bugs happen; `CameraSystem.update` (focus resolution) and `handleRightClick` (menu) both read this same `pickables`. `handleRightClick(input, simTime)` picks via `map-pick.ts`'s `pickNearest` and opens a `hud/property-window.ts` `PropertyWindow<MenuAction>` (below) built from `itemsFor`. Each open window's `entry.target` is refreshed every `sync()` from the current candidate set, and its `onSelect` reads through `entry.target` rather than the `target` captured at open time — a time-dependent action (warp/add-node) must run against the object's current position/crossing time, not a frame-old snapshot; `'empty-space'` is the one kind with nothing to show a property window about, so `handleEmptySpaceRightClick` keeps opening the plain `ContextMenu<MapPickable>` instead. `itemsFor`: `'body'`/`'ship'`/`'player'` all add the shared `navTargetItems` — set/unset nav-target, shown only when `NavTarget.canTarget` finds a resolvable orbital plane for that id, so an item is never offered and then silently refused; `'apsis'`/`'relnode'` add "add node here" (`PlanEditor.addNodeAt`) and, for `'relnode'`, "warp here" (`SimSpeedManager.startAutoWarpTo`); `'player'` additionally offers, at the top of its item list, a single 軌道計画の実行 toggle (creative mode only, cycling OFF→瞬間移動→自動操縦→OFF on each click, showing the current mode in its own label — `PlanExecutionMode` above) followed by activate/deactivate (`Game.setActivePlayer`/`setActivePlayerOrNull`, one label swapping for the other by whether this ship is already active) and delete, the last simply omitted for the active ship so the action is unreachable rather than rejected. `'player'`/`'ship'`/`'ammo'`/`'base'` also offer "duplicate" (`duplicateItems()`), but only in creative mode (`isCreativeMode()` — a single `(this.game.activeStage as any).stageId === 'creative'` check shared by every kind and by `'empty-space'`'s "place object" item, so the two never drift apart) — the item is never shown in a mode where there's nowhere to route it, matching the "don't offer what would be refused" rule the whole menu follows. Choosing it (`runDuplicate` → `duplicateSourceFor`, which maps `MapPickable.kind` to the matching `EntityManager` lookup and that entity's live `state`) hands the state to `CreativeStage.openShipPlacerForDuplicate`, which converts it to a preset `ElementsForm` via `creative/duplicate-form.ts`'s `elementsFormFromState` and opens `ShipPlacerPanel` pre-filled with it — a body/`'apsis'`/`'relnode'` target has no matching `duplicateSourceFor` case (nothing to duplicate), which is exactly why those kinds don't get the item in the first place. If the state can't convert (hyperbolic/parabolic) or the converted form's reference body fails `placement-validation.ts`'s `validateBaseReferenceFields` (a base whose current orbit isn't moon-centered), `openShipPlacerForDuplicate` doesn't pass the mismatched form through at all — it hints why and opens with only the object type carried over (`ShipPlacerPreset`'s `{kind: 'objectType'}`), since applying the values while silently rejecting just the reference body would leave the panel showing a different orbit than the one displayed. `handleRightClick` runs before `PlanEditor.handleMapPointer`, so a right-click on a marker is claimed here first; `PlanEditor.handleMapPointer` only sees the misses, consuming the ones that hit a node, so priority is expressed purely as call order and the two gizmos never reference each other. A right-click that hits neither falls through to `handleEmptySpaceRightClick`, which opens the `'empty-space'` menu in any mode (not just creative) — its "place object" item stays creative-only. `MapPicker` owns a permanent `ObjectListPanel` (`object-list-panel.ts`, docked top-right via `hudDock(root, 'right')`) shown for as long as `overviewMode` holds — no toggle, no open/close action — listing the current `pickables`, grouped into per-kind accordion sections (天体/自艦/敵/弾薬/基地; `apsis`/`relnode`/`eqnode`/`empty-space` are markers, not objects, and are left out), each section's header toggling its own body open/closed — within 天体, a body's satellites and own Lagrange points nest under that body's row as a collapsible child list rather than a flat indent, and a satellite's own Lagrange points nest one level deeper still under the satellite's own row, mirroring the physical hierarchy (`ObjectListPanel`'s recursive `RowNode`, built from a `parentOf: ReadonlyMap<id, id>` `MapPicker.sync` derives from `FocusMarkers.labels` — a Lagrange label's parent is `object-groups.ts`'s `lagrangeParentId`, an ordinary body's is `primaryOf`; a body with neither a parent nor children stays a flat row). Every such child list starts collapsed (`RowNode.expanded` defaults `false`) since the set nested under any one body is large and rarely needed at a glance, unlike the five top-level sections which start expanded. calling `game.frameControls.setFocus({kind:'object', id})` on a row click, and right-clicking a row (`onSelectRight`) opening the same `openPropertyWindow` a right-click on the object itself would — so the window is just another view onto the one candidate set, not a second source of what's on the map. `sync(overviewMode)` (called from `Game.sync`, alongside the other map-mode panel syncs) is the only place `pickables` reaches the DOM — and it must not do that by rewriting `innerHTML`: `ObjectListPanel.sync` keeps one persistent row element per item id (added/removed by diffing against the previous frame's id set) precisely because a full rebuild would drop each row's click listener the instant a click lands mid-frame, the same reason `stage.ts`'s radiator buttons stay off the `StageStatusPanel`'s rebuilt-every-frame center column.

`MapPicker` also owns the property-window lifecycle: `windows: Map<string, WindowEntry>` (`WindowEntry = {win: PropertyWindow<MenuAction>, target: MapPickable}`) keyed by `` `${kind}:${id}` `` (a `MapPickable.id` alone isn't unique across kinds) so an object holds at most one window, plus `tempWindowKey` for the single non-clipped one. `openPropertyWindow(clientX, clientY, target, simTime)` — called from `handleRightClick` for every kind but `'empty-space'` — moves and `bringToFront()`s an existing window for that key instead of opening a second one; otherwise it builds one via `buildContent` (`itemsFor`'s `type: 'header'` item becomes the window's title/subtitle; the action items themselves come from `windowItems`, not a one-time snapshot — see below) with `rows: []` (filled by the next `sync`, so there's no separate row-building path at open time) and wires `onSelect`/`onClose`/`onOutsideClick`/`onClipChange`. A freshly-opened window is never clipped, so it becomes the new `tempWindowKey`, closing whatever held that slot before; `onClipChange` keeps that invariant live afterward too — clipping hands the `tempWindowKey` slot back (`null` if this window held it), unclipping closes whatever currently holds the slot and takes it, so the "at most one temporary window" rule holds in both directions rather than only at open time. `onSelect` still calls `this.handlers[kind].run` exactly as the old `ContextMenu` wiring did, then closes the window unless it's clipped — except `'delete'`, which always closes it since the target is gone either way. `onOutsideClick` closes only when not clipped; the ✕ button always closes (that path runs through the widget's own `dispose()` before `onClose` fires, so `closeWindow`'s counterpart, `forgetWindow`, only drops the bookkeeping — it never double-disposes). `close()` (the same method `ViewManager` already called to fold the object-list panel on leaving the map view) now also closes every open window. `sync(overviewMode, simTime, bodies, player)` decides whether to close a window via `isTargetGone(target)` — for `'player'`/`'ship'`/`'ammo'`/`'base'` this reads the entity's own `alive` directly (via `EntityManager`'s finders) rather than the frame's `pickables`, since a living ship can legitimately be absent from `pickables` for a frame when its `displayState(displayTime)` comes back `null` (future-ghost slider past its prediction horizon, or time-warp outrunning prediction growth) — closing on that would be wrong, since the entity hasn't died. The remaining kinds (celestial body/apsis/AN-DN icons) have no `alive` of their own, so they fall back to "still in this frame's `pickables`". A window that survives has its stored `target` refreshed from the current `pickables` entry when one exists (kept as-is otherwise, since every kind's row/item derivation reads the live entity's own state rather than `target.pos`) before `syncRows`/`syncItems` re-derive that kind's `PropertyRow[]`/`PropertyWindowItem[]` and push them through — row derivation is display-only and reuses `hud/orbit-info.ts`'s `orbitInfo`/`relativeInfo` for every kind that shows orbital elements, never rederiving them, and action-item derivation (`windowItems`, shared by `buildContent` at open time and `sync` every frame after) re-runs `itemsFor` so a clipped window's items track state that changes after it opened (e.g. which ship is the active one). A row is omitted outright (not shown as `—`) wherever it depends on a live `player` that doesn't exist, e.g. Creative mode before any ship is placed. `buildContent` also passes `renameHandlerFor(target)` as `onRename` — non-`undefined` only for `'player'`/`'base'` (the two kinds with a mutable display name — `Player.displayName`, `Base.name`), and re-resolved through `target.id` inside the closure (not the `target` object itself) so a rename always lands on the live entity regardless of how many frames have passed since the window opened.
- `src/game/hud/dock-view.ts` — `DockView`: the full-screen docked-ship UI (格納艦/部品/ショップ tabs), entered as a view via `ViewManager.setView('dock')` once a base is active. Holds `currentBase`/`currentShip`/`currentTab` and rewrites its body's `innerHTML` per tab on every `open()`/tab switch/action (a one-shot modal, not a per-frame HUD panel, so the differential-DOM-update rule `ObjectListPanel`/`StageStatusPanel` follow for per-frame panels doesn't apply here — nothing here runs outside a user click). `SHOP_CATALOG` is its static part price list — **its numbers must stay in the same units and the same order of magnitude as `Ship.initDefaultParts`**, which is why the thruster entries derive their thrust/torque from `PLAYER_MASS`/`THROTTLE_LEVELS`/`MAX_ANG_ACCEL` rather than spelling out literals: a catalog value that drifts from the default scale turns a swap into a silent cliff (the thruster once read `thrust: 100` against a default of 100,000). Buying calls `game-entity/parts.ts`'s `createPart` and pushes into `base.baseState.inventory`; the 部品 tab shows 搭載部品 and 倉庫 side by side so the same-`type` comparison a swap needs is visible at once, and the 倉庫 column renders even with no docked ship because the inventory belongs to the *base*. Swapping `splice`s in place so `player.parts` keeps its array identity (the `Ship` performance getters read that array), then calls `Ship.refreshFromParts()` — the one place `maxHp`/`hp` are re-derived after a part changes. Repairing a part writes the ship's own `Part.hp` directly (docked `parts` is the live `player.parts` array, not a copy) and then re-derives the `DockedShipEntry`'s `hp`/`maxHp` totals through `Ship.refreshFromParts()`, since those are a display-only snapshot the ship-list tab reads without walking every part — routing through the ship is what keeps the "hull or cockpit gone ⇒ hp 0" rule from being bypassed. RCS tanks can also be refuelled here, which is the only way `RcsTankPart.fuel` is ever restored. The 格納艦 tab's 新造 button is `DockView`'s own affordability gate (`NEW_SHIP_COST`, priced off `SHOP_CATALOG`'s cheapest full loadout) — it debits `base.baseState.money` itself and only then calls `onBuildShip`, so `Docking` (which owns the actual `Player` construction) never touches currency.
- `src/game/nav-target.ts` — `NavTarget`: the map's *navigation* target — any `MapPickable`, not just an `Enemy`. It is deliberately a different class from `Targeter`'s combat target: the two differ both in what they can point at (`Enemy` only vs. any `MapPickable`) and in what the designation is *for* (gunnery vs. maneuver planning), so neither one's constraints — e.g. `Enemy`-specific fire-assist logic — get to narrow the other's target range. They may happen to point at the same enemy; that is a coincidence, not a shared id. Holds only a target id (`toggleTarget`/`id`); `update(player, entities, ephemeris, simTime)` re-derives the relative AN/DN (where the player's orbit crosses the target's orbital plane) from scratch each frame — `resolvePlaneNormal` reads the target's own orbit normal for a ship or base (`orbitalElementsOf(strongestAttractor(...)).hHat`, found by name in `entities.enemies` first, then `entities.players`, then `entities.bases` — a base has a real orbit via `GameEntity` like any other entity, so it resolves the same way), the lunar orbit normal for the moon (`Ephemeris.orbitNormalAt('moon', …)`), or the relevant rotating frame's ẑ for a Lagrange point (`orbitFrameRotationAt('moon'|'earth', …)`) — and stores nothing if the target's plane can't be resolved or is ~coincident with the player's own. `nodeAnomalies` returns the node true anomalies in the player's own center-relative basis, so `anPos`/`dnPos` are un-baked back to absolute ECI via `frameOfAttractor(playerCenter)` + `toInertialState` before being stored — `positionOnOrbit` alone returns a center-relative position, which only happens to read as ECI while the player orbits Earth (center at the ECI origin). `canTarget(id, entities, ephemeris, t)` runs the same `resolvePlaneNormal` just to answer whether a plane resolves at all, which is what lets `MapPicker.itemsFor` show the "set as nav-target" item on any `MapPickable.kind` whose id can actually work — including the moon and every Lagrange point, not just ships. `passTimeOf('nav-an'|'nav-dn')` answers the AN/DN crossing time (`tofBetween` from the player's current true anomaly), which `MapPicker` feeds to `SimSpeedManager.startAutoWarpTo`/`PlanEditor.addNodeAt`. `mapPickables()`/`sync(project, overviewMode, cameraPos)` expose/draw the ▲/▽ icons themselves — `MapPicker.refresh()` folds them into the shared candidate set alongside `FocusMarkers`' labels and every living ship. `sync` hides an icon `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body when `overviewMode`, off the `Attractor[]` `update` cached at `simTime`. Setting the nav-target itself is otherwise map-only (`MapPicker`'s right-click), with one exception: `updateCombatBasePicking(entities, input, project)` is `NavTarget`'s own combat-view right-click handler, holding a private `ContextMenu<Base, MenuAction>` (offering only `MenuCommon.navTarget`/`cancel`, since a base isn't a weapons target). `Game.update`'s combat branch calls it *before* `Targeter.updateCombatTargeting` — its callback returns `false` on a miss (`Input.takeRightClicks`' fall-through convention), so a click that doesn't land on a base is left for `Targeter` to try against enemies, exactly as if this handler weren't there. This is why the split stays clean: `Targeter` never learns about bases, and `NavTarget` never learns about enemies.
- `src/game/view-manager.ts` — `ViewManager`: **the source of truth for which of the three views is showing** (`ViewId` = `'combat' | 'map' | 'dock'`), and the sole place a transition happens (`setView`). **`setView` calls the private `applyChrome` unconditionally, even when `next` already equals `_current`** — so every call, transition or not, leaves the guarantee "the camera/plan/future-display flags and HUD chrome match the current view" holding, which is what lets `restoreView` (below) re-sync chrome through the same call rather than a second path. It co-switches the camera-side flag (`cameraSystem.overviewMode`), the plan-side editing flag (`editor.editMode`), and the future-display gate (`displayTimeManager.forceCurrent`) so they move together while staying logically independent. Combat and map are the two views that draw the 3D world; **dock is layered on top of whichever of those you came from** — `returnFromDock` remembers it, the private `worldView` getter reports it, and `applyChrome` drives the camera/plan flags off `worldView` rather than `current`. That split is what keeps map→dock from running `editor.onMapClosed()` (which prunes zero-Δv nodes) as a side effect of merely opening a dock. `handleInput` takes `[M]` (ignored entirely while in dock) and forces `'combat'` when the stage stops playing; `selectableViews(combatAvailable)` is what `ViewBadge` lists, filtering out both the current view and any view that can't be entered — `'dock'` needs `Docking.canEnterDock()`, `'combat'` needs a living ship. `Docking` registers itself after construction (`setDocking`, the same late-injection shape as `setTouchControls`) because it is built later. Rendering/viewpoint code checks `overviewMode`; input/behavior code checks `editMode`. This sits at the `game/` root because everything else about a view belongs to either the camera, the plan, or the dock concern. `applyChrome` also reparents a panel built statically elsewhere between combat and map placement — `hud/dom.ts`'s `syncNavballPlacement` (navball → `#hud-dock-left`), a no-op when the element is already at the target parent — and, leaving map, calls `hud/dom.ts`'s `resetHudDocks` to re-expand the left/right docks and the PREDICT bar's own collapse toggle, so leaving the map always hands back a fully-expanded HUD regardless of what was collapsed while inside it. `serializeView()`/`restoreView(view)` expose/restore `worldView` — the one piece of view state a snapshot saves, since dock is never itself a saved view (a docked ship is snapshotted as a `DockedShipEntry`, not a live view to return to).
- `src/game/frame-controls.ts` — `FrameControls`: the map's **座標系** panel, a cross-cut over two owners the same shape as `MapPicker`/`ViewManager` — `OverviewCamera` (camera focus/rotation) and `PlanDisplay.planFrame` (the planned polyline's origin/rotation) each keep their own state, and this class only writes into them from one panel so the two don't need separate UIs. Four zones, each a coordinate system's origin × rotation split into its own control: `カメラ`/`カメラ回転` (`AnchorZone`/`RotationZone` writing `overviewCamera`'s focus/rotation through this class's own `setFocus`/`setCameraRotation`) and `並進`/`計画軌道回転` (`AnchorZone`/`RotationZone` writing `planDisplay.planFrame` via `ephemeris.frameOf(center, rotatingWith)`, keeping whichever half the other zone chose). **`setFocus(target: FocusTarget)` is the sole entry point for moving the camera's focus, and every focus-changing UI in the game routes through it** — `Game.frameControls` is `public readonly`, and `MapPicker`'s object-list row click/double-click/property-window "move focus" item and `PlanEditor`'s node-focus action all call `game.frameControls.setFocus(...)` rather than reaching `overviewCamera.setFocusTarget` directly. It forwards to `overviewCamera.setFocusTarget` unconditionally, then — only when `target.kind === 'object'` and `target.id` is a body registered in `ephemeris.registry` — also re-centers `planDisplay.planFrame` on that same body, keeping `rotatingWith` as-is; an unregistered id (a self-flown ship, an enemy, a base, an ammo pickup) or a `'point'` focus leaves `planFrame` untouched, since `Ephemeris`'s frame resolution has no ECI origin to fall back to for a body it doesn't carry. This is a one-way coupling — moving the plan frame's own origin via the 並進 zone never moves the camera focus back. Only the camera zone offers "固定を解除" (`releaseLabel` — see `AnchorZone` below): releasing bakes the camera's current ECI position into a `camera/focus-target.ts` `FocusTarget` fixed in the star-centered inertial frame (`ephemeris.frameOf(starId, null)`, or `ephemeris.inertialFrame` with no star), via `focus-target.ts`'s `focusPoint`, using `overviewCamera.resolvedFocus` and the simTime `sync` last saw (`lastTime` — `onSelect` fires from a DOM event outside the frame flow, so it can't take simTime as an argument). The translation zone never offers a release: a drawn polyline is always baked into *some* coordinate system, so "fix to nothing" has no meaning there — fixing to solar-system space at all is what selecting the star itself in the pulldown already means. `Game` constructs it once and calls `sync(pickables, cameraPos, attractors, simTime, visible)` from `Game.sync`; it has no `update` of its own, since every zone's choices are re-derived fresh each `sync` from the current pickables/attractors rather than advanced over time.

  **Every way of editing Δv funnels through one method, `applyDv(axis, sign, amount)`**, which is the sole place `plan.applyNodeDv` is called from user input — so a drag, a key hold, a button hold, and a latched drag can't each compute their own rounding or sign convention, and a zero `amount` is a no-op (skipping the call, rather than letting `Plan.applyNodeDv`'s unconditional downstream-discard fire on a no-op edit). The three input paths differ only in how they arrive at `amount`: `applyAxisDrag` (short arm drags, below the latch threshold) scales pixel delta by the existing per-drag rate; `applyHeldDv` (WASDQE keys or a `dvButtons` press, checked together via `||` in `updateEditing`) accumulates a per-direction hold time in `dvHoldTime` (indexed `axis*2 + (sign<0?1:0)`) and looks it up through `rampedDvRate` — an exponential ramp from `DV_RATE_MIN` to `DV_RATE_MAX` over `DV_RATE_RAMP_SEC`, so a tap stays fine while holding sweeps up to coarse; a released direction resets its hold time to 0, so ramping always restarts from the beginning. The arm drag itself only free-drags (via `applyAxisDrag`) up to `NodeGizmo`'s own `DV_DRAG_LATCH_PX` — beyond that `NodeGizmo` stops reporting deltas and instead exposes `latch: AxisLatchState | null` (the axis/sign and the pixel excess past the threshold), which `updateEditing` reads every frame and feeds through `applyDv` at a rate proportional to the excess (capped at `DV_RATE_MAX`) — so a large, held drag keeps adding Δv even while the pointer stops moving, without the user needing to keep dragging further.
- `plan-display.ts` — `PlanDisplay`: the plan's trajectory display — `planFrame` (the coordinate frame the planned polyline is drawn in, independent of `OverviewCamera.cameraFrame` — both are selected separately via `src/game/frame-controls.ts`'s FrameControls panel, which is the sole writer of `planFrame`), the ⬡ `plannedPlayer` ghost marker showing where the plan puts the ship at `displayTime` (present or future alike, so it reads as "is the ship actually flying the plan" the instant the map closes — its label is `計画位置 高度 …` at or before `simTime`, `T+…h…m 高度 …` beyond it, altitude measured from whichever body pulls hardest at that position via `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor`, not fixed to Earth), the ◇ Pe/Ap periapsis/apoapsis icons, the ルーラー calendar-tick marks along the polyline (below), so it disappears once `displayTime` runs past the trailing arc's one-revolution end. The apsis icons describe **the orbit the ship is on after its last planned burn**, found directly on the actually-drawn integration — not the analytic ellipse — because osculating `OrbitalElements` oscillate with the evaluation epoch under J2 (tens of km of periapsis/apoapsis wobble per revolution at LEO), which would otherwise make the icons visibly jump the instant a zero-Δv node changed which epoch they were evaluated at. `apsisIconsOf` reads `path.finalPeriapsisPoint`/`path.finalApoapsisPoint` (`PlanPath`'s exposure of the trailing `PlanArc`'s `periapsisPoint()`/`apoapsisPoint()` — see `plan-arc.ts`/`plan-path.ts` above for how those are found by the radial-velocity sign crossing on the live integration step pair, not by scanning the drawn sample array) directly rather than deriving them itself, so a collision-course segment's `finalPeriapsisPoint === null` simply shows no periapsis icon rather than a spurious one. When both points exist, each resolves its own center independently via `strongestAttractor(point.r, ephemeris.attractorsAt(point.t))` (in practice the same body for both), and the circular guard compares `(apDist - peDist) / (apDist + peDist)` from those two precise center-distances against `APSIS_MIN_ECC`, hiding both icons below it since the apsis direction is then unreliable; a lone point (e.g. a hyperbolic orbit's periapsis with no apoapsis) is shown ungated, since the guard only makes sense once both are available to compare. `apsisTimeOf(id)` reads the found point's own `t` directly (no re-derivation), so `PlanEditor.addNodeAt` always places a node at a time that actually exists on the drawn line, and `apsisMarkers` exposes the currently-shown icons as `MapPickable`s (`kind: 'apsis'`) for that same right-click to hit. In the map view, `syncApsisMarkers` also hides an icon `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body from the camera's current position (`sync`'s `overviewMode`/`cameraPos` arguments) — combat view never gates on this, since a body can't occlude anything from the chase camera's close-in vantage the way it can on the map. The ruler-style calendar ticks are split the same way: `tickIconsOf()` (update) reads `PlanPath.timeRange()` and `hud/calendar-ticks.ts`'s `calendarBoundaries` to build the candidate set, tagging each with the coarsest calendar rank it satisfies (an ordinary midnight is a day tick, a month-start midnight is a month tick) rather than deciding the whole displayed span's unit at once — on an eccentric orbit the drawn samples bunch near periapsis and spread near apoapsis, so the screen-space density (and thus which unit reads as legible) varies along a single polyline, and a caller can't pick one interval for the whole thing without either flooding the dense end or leaving the sparse end blank. `syncTickMarkers` (sync) does the actual thinning, walking ranks coarsest-first and keeping a candidate only when it's at least `PLAN_TICK_MIN_PX` from the nearest already-kept tick on either side — a plain per-rank pass would let a fine-rank tick crowd out its own coarse-rank neighbor, so ties are broken toward keeping the coarser marks first. A tick's length is drawn relative to whichever rank is actually the finest surviving one this frame (`PLAN_TICK_LENGTH_PX`, indexed by that offset rather than by the absolute rank), so the average tick length stays constant as the unit switches along the line and only the switch point itself reads as a length step; its label only shows past the wider `PLAN_TICK_LABEL_MIN_PX` threshold, since a legible tick spacing is still too dense for a label at every one. Each tick is drawn as an inline SVG line (`tickSvg`, `mk-plantick`) rotated perpendicular to the trajectory via `MarkerManager.headingRotationDeg(...) + 90`, holding its previous rotation on the rare frame that function can't resolve one — a plain text glyph can't vary its length to carry the depth signal above. This is a distinct concern from `hud/tick-scale.ts`'s PREDICT-slider ticks: that module places ticks at even fractions of an *elapsed*-time span with a `T+` label, which has no notion of a UTC calendar boundary at all.
- `plan-path.ts` — `PlanPath`: splits the plan's corners into segments (anchor → node₁ → … → nodeₙ, plus a trailing one), owns one `PlanArc` per segment (grey before the first burn, white after it, orange beyond), and is the **single place display-frame transforms and screen picking are done** (`toDisplay`/`toDisplayDir`/`projectPoint`/`nearestSample`) so the drawn polyline and click hit-testing can't drift apart. The two transforms are split by what kind of quantity is moved, mirroring `physics/frame.ts`'s own point/direction split: `toDisplay` is affine (a sample position), `toDisplayDir` is rotation-only (an orbital basis vector, e.g. the Δv gizmo's axes) — putting a direction through the point path would offset it by the display frame's own origin the moment that frame is centered on a moving body. `update(plan, ephemeris, frame, currentTime)` rebuilds the segments and re-integrates each `PlanArc`; `sync(fo, project)` pushes the resulting sample series into the line meshes. The display context is split along the same line — the frame and un-bake time are settled in `update` (screen picking from a DOM pointer event has to work between frames), the `ProjectFn` arrives in `sync`; the `ProjectFn` is deliberately *not* captured at construction, since `CameraSystem.activeCameraProjection` returns a snapshot of whichever camera is active *now* (capturing it once would pin picking to the chase camera's startup viewpoint). `finalSegmentStart` is a **public field** holding the trailing segment's start state (`null` if every segment ends on a node, i.e. there's no segment beyond the last burn), alongside `finalSegmentSamples` — the same segment's already-integrated `PlanArc.samples` (also `null` together with it) — the two pieces of segment bookkeeping `Game.equatorNodeSources`' equatorial-node icons need, so it doesn't have to know how segments are split. `finalSegmentSamples` is the existing `PlanArc` array by reference, not a copy — re-exposing it under a new frame-local array would defeat `render/sampled-line.ts`'s reference-identity re-bake guard the moment a consumer draws it. `finalPeriapsisPoint`/`finalApoapsisPoint` are the same trailing segment's `PlanArc.periapsisPoint()`/`apoapsisPoint()` (`null` together with `finalSegmentStart` when there's no trailing segment), which is what `PlanDisplay`'s apsis icons read directly rather than deriving from `finalSegmentSamples` themselves — the two are computed by the trailing arc's own integration, at each segment's own start-state gravity snapshot (`strongestAttractor(seg.state0.r, ephemeris.attractorsAt(seg.state0.t))`) passed into `arc.update` as `apsisCenter`, rather than at the currently-displayed time, so the detected apsis stays tied to the segment's own physics regardless of where the future-ghost slider sits. Every other segment passes `apsisCenter: null`, since only the trailing segment is ever queried for Pe/Ap. Owned by `PlanDisplay`; `PlanEditor` shares the same instance (`planDisplay.path`) for its own screen picking. `sync` caches its caller-supplied camera position — `nearestSample` fires off a DOM pointer event, outside the frame flow — and drops any sample `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body before ranking candidates, so a node can't be placed on a stretch of the line that isn't actually visible. **How far ahead the plan is drawn is the PREDICT panel's call**: every segment ends at the next node's `t`, and the trailing one runs `plan.ts`'s `segmentDurationFrom(state0, attractors, displayTimeManager)` from its own start state — the constructor takes a `DisplayTimeManager` reference (shared from `Game` through `PlanEditor`/`PlanDisplay`, see `display-time-manager.ts` above) precisely so this length tracks the PREDICT panel's selection instead of a fixed span. The default `'orbit'` preset reproduces one orbital period around whichever body pulls hardest at that state (`attractor.ts`'s `orbitalElementsOf`'s `OrbitalElements.period` — not `localOrbitPeriod`'s circular approximation, which would return the short period of the periapsis radius for a node freshly placed on an eccentric orbit); picking a longer preset draws further, and `Plan.nodeTimeRange` (the range a node may be dragged across) grows the same way since it calls the same `segmentDurationFrom`. Beyond one revolution the polyline overlaps itself, so a click can no longer be disambiguated by screen distance alone — see `nearestSample` below. `nearestSample`'s optional `TimeRange` is how a caller restricts picking to part of the polyline; `PlanEditor` passes `Plan.nodeTimeRange` there so a node drag can only ever land on a legal time. Its tie-break is two-stage: first the screen-nearest candidate decides *which arc wins* — arc 0 (pre-burn) always has smaller `t` than the post-burn arcs, so comparing `t` across arcs at the point where two arcs visually cross would silently pick the wrong one — then, restricted to that arc's candidates within `NEAREST_SAMPLE_TIE_PX` of the screen-nearest distance, it picks whichever is closest to the caller-supplied `referenceT` (ties broken toward the earlier `t`). Both call sites in `plan-editor.ts` route through this one rule rather than branching: new-node placement (`handleMapClick`/`handleNodeRightClick`) passes `referenceT = -Infinity`, which — since every candidate is then equally "infinitely far" — degenerates to the earliest-`t` tie-break and reproduces "the first time the line reaches here"; a node drag (`dragNodeToNearestSample`) passes the node's own current time, so a click near where a later revolution's polyline overlaps an earlier one keeps the node on the revolution it was actually dragged from instead of snapping back to revolution 0. Every arc is drawn at the same `PLAN_ARC_OPACITY`, so the only thing distinguishing the pre-burn segment from the post-burn ones is `SEGMENT_COLORS`. `sync` also converts the dash pattern from screen pixels to world metres before handing it to each `PlanArc`: it takes the arc's middle sample, puts it through its own `toDisplay`, and asks the caller-supplied `ScaleFn` for the metres-per-pixel there, so the dashes keep the same on-screen spacing across the map's four orders of magnitude of zoom.
- `plan-arc.ts` — `PlanArc`: one segment of the plan, drawn as a polyline. It owns a `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts` `DynamicTrajectory` seeded at the segment's start state and RK4-stepped to its end (`update`; the mesh side is a separate `sync`) — so the plan reuses the same "advance a timed state while recording decimated samples" machinery every entity uses, rather than a second implementation of it. Its step size is `localOrbitPeriod(r, attractors) / PLAN_ARC_STEPS_PER_REV` (=100, `attractor.ts`'s `localOrbitPeriod` against that step's own `attractorsNear` result — see `simulation/attractors.ts` above; both the sizing lookup and the step itself re-derive `classifyAttractors(mergeAttractors(gravityBodiesAt(ephemeris, t), dynamicAttractors))` at their own evaluation time, `t` and `t + dt/2` respectively) off the current radius, so fixing the step count per revolution keeps accuracy uniform regardless of altitude, eccentricity, or which body the segment is currently orbiting; the step count is capped at `PLAN_ARC_MAX_STEPS` (20000) as a safety bound — a segment with a deep or fast periapsis pass shrinks that local-radius-derived step size and could otherwise take an unbounded number of steps to cover its full duration — and hitting the cap truncates the segment the same way reentry does. With `PLAN_ARC_STEPS_PER_REV` fixed, this cap converts directly into a maximum revolution count per segment (≈200) regardless of which PREDICT duration is selected — see `DEVELOP/SPEC.md` for the measured truncation distances. A lower step-size floor to force a long segment to finish drawing regardless was rejected: it lets RK4 diverge into a fabricated trajectory instead of an accurate but truncated one. Decimation is `DynamicTrajectory`'s own `sampleInterval` (segment length / `PLAN_ARC_MAX_SAMPLES`) and `keepDuration` (the whole segment), the polyline's points are `samplesOldestFirst()`, and `at(t)` is the queue's Hermite interpolation; the draw itself is delegated to `render/sampled-line.ts`, and the polyline is **always dashed** — a plan is a requested trajectory that has not happened yet, so it must not be mistakable for the solid predicted one. `sync` takes the dash length and gap as world metres (`PlanPath` converts them from pixels) and pushes them through `SampledLine.setDash` every frame, since the material's units are the same world metres the `lineDistance` attribute is baked in. `impactPoint()` is the state at which the integration reached a body's surface, which `plan-display.ts` turns into the `✕` marker; the arc itself simply ends there. The step that first lands inside the body is backsolved to the exact surface crossing via `physics/sphere-contact.ts`'s `sweptSphereToi` between that step's start and end position — a body's radius can be tens of km, and a single step near a fast, steep approach can otherwise land well past the surface, which would print an altitude reading deep in the negative rather than ~0. `update`'s `apsisCenter: Attractor | null` argument (`PlanPath` passes a body only for the trailing segment, `null` for every other) is what the arc's periapsis/apoapsis are measured against: right alongside the existing surface-impact check, each step pair (`trajectory.prevState`/`.state`) is handed to `physics/trajectory-features.ts`'s `apsisCrossing(apsisCenter, prev, next)`, and the first periapsis and first apoapsis found (independently — a segment can find one without the other) are kept as `periapsisState`/`apoapsisState`, read back through `periapsisPoint()`/`apoapsisPoint()`. Checking on the live step pair rather than the decimated `_samples` array is what makes a genuine collision-course segment (radial velocity never crosses zero on the way to impact) correctly report `periapsisPoint() === null` — there is no periapsis on that course. `apsisCenter === null` skips the check outright, so the segments nobody queries for Pe/Ap pay nothing for it. Drag is on at `SHIP_BCINV` — the planner only ever plans the player's own orbit, so it integrates with the player's own ballistic coefficient — and integration stops when the state sinks into any body's surface (`attractor.ts`'s `hitCelestialBody`, margin `REENTRY_ALT`) or goes non-finite, in which case `endState()` is `null`. Re-integration is throttled by `update`'s `tracksLiveAnchor` argument (`PlanPath` passes `true` only for the sole segment while the plan has no nodes — the only case where `state0` follows the live ship every frame): once a node is placed, `state0`/`end` only move on an actual edit, so plain identity/value equality decides whether to recompute, and an untouched arc costs nothing per frame. While `tracksLiveAnchor`, identity comparison alone is useless (`state0` is a fresh object every frame) and even the segment length isn't exactly repeatable under the `'orbit'` preset — the ship's osculating period drifts continuously under J2/drag — so both the length change and the anchor's time drift are compared against the same threshold, the current sample interval (segment length / `PLAN_ARC_MAX_SAMPLES`): below it the drawn polyline wouldn't visibly change, so the stale integration is kept; a real duration-preset change is orders of magnitude larger than that threshold and is always caught immediately. That threshold-based tolerance assumes the anchor is the *same* orbit merely ticking forward, which doesn't hold across a discontinuous re-anchor (`Game.setActivePlayer` to a different ship, a dock launch, a collision's `state` overwrite) — those can land at or before the same `t` the last recompute used, which the drift/length thresholds alone would read as "no change" and keep drawing the previous ship's orbit for up to a full sample interval. So `update` also recomputes immediately whenever `state0`'s identity changed *and* its `t` did not advance past the last recompute's — a swap that lands strictly later in time is left to the normal threshold, since by the time it would matter enough to matter visually, the drift check catches it anyway. A reusable per-segment unit — knows nothing about `Plan` — so `PlanPath` composes several of these rather than predicting the whole multi-node polyline in one shot.
- `node-gizmo.ts` — `NodeGizmo`: the maneuver-node DOM pointer-events layer — node handles + Δv axis arms + the node context menu (warp/delete). A plan concern; uses the shared `hud/context-menu.ts`. An axis-arm drag accumulates its total screen-space projection (`totalProj`) from pointerdown; once `|totalProj|` exceeds `DV_DRAG_LATCH_PX` the drag latches — `onAxisDrag` stops firing per-move deltas and the public `latch: AxisLatchState | null` field instead exposes the axis/sign and the pixel excess past the threshold, for `PlanEditor.updateEditing` to read and integrate every frame at its own rate (see `plan-editor.ts` above). `latch` is cleared and the latched flag reset on pointerup/pointercancel.
- `plan-gizmo-3d.ts` — `PlanGizmo3D`: `NodeGizmo`'s 3D counterpart — six world-space arrow meshes (PRO/RET blue, NRM/ANM green, OUT/IN red), one `THREE.Group` at `renderOrder = 999` so the gizmo always draws on top of the scene it's pointing into. It holds no pointer/drag logic of its own and never references `NodeGizmo`; the two stay decoupled the same way the DOM/3D split works everywhere else in `plan/` — `PlanEditor.syncGizmo` is what reads `NodeGizmo`'s `latch`/`activeAxis` state each frame and translates it into this class's own `setStretch(axis, sign, factor)` call, so a drag on the 2D arm and the matching 3D arrow's stretch are two independent pushes from one shared frame of truth rather than one gizmo driving the other. `setPositionAndRotation(pos, pro, nrm, radOut, scale)` places the whole group at the selected node and aligns its local axes (X=RAD, Y=PRO, Z=NRM) to the orbital basis at that node, with `scale` supplied by the caller from the current camera distance so the gizmo holds a constant apparent size regardless of zoom. `setVisible`/`setPositionAndRotation`/`setStretch` are plain setters pushing already-computed values into mesh transforms — sync-shaped by what they do, even though (unlike most of this codebase) they aren't named with the `sync` verb.

- `plan.ts` — `Plan`: the node list plus the `anchor` (the plan's starting state). **Both are plain `KinematicState`s: a node *is* its frozen post-burn absolute state, not a relative Δv** — the burn time is the state's own `t`, and Δv is the derived value (node `v` − arrival velocity). The node list is a single `KinematicState[]`, always kept in execution-time order by construction: `addNode(postState)` finds the insertion point (the count of existing nodes whose `t` is earlier), truncates from there, then pushes — so the added node always lands at the end and its returned index is stable. While the plan is empty, `trackAnchor` follows the live player state; placing the first node freezes the anchor, after which prediction is a pure function of anchor + nodes and never reads `player` state. `dropNodesBefore(t)` (called by `PlanGuide.update` with `simTime - C.NODE_EXPIRE_GRACE`) removes every node whose execution time is at or before `t`, re-freezes `anchor` to the last node it removed, and returns that node (`null` if nothing was dropped) — `CreativeStage.applySimulationEvents` uses the return value to snap an `'instant'` ship's `state` onto it directly, while `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s `PlanExecutor.finish` (for a `'powered'` ship) calls `dropNodesBefore` for the same node-consumption bookkeeping but then calls **`overwriteAnchor(ship.state)`** right after — `trackAnchor` would only take effect when the node list happens to be empty (the plan's own promise: "only follow the live state while there's nothing planned yet"), which a `'powered'` burn can't rely on if further nodes are still queued behind the one just consumed. `overwriteAnchor` replaces `anchor` unconditionally instead, so the segment that follows is always drawn from where the ship actually ended up — not the node's ideal, error-free value — regardless of how many nodes remain. Either way the remaining plan is still drawn from a valid start and the already-flown segment of `PlanPath` disappears on its own. Editing a node discards downstream nodes (`retimeNode`/`applyNodeDv`/`removeNode`), which is what keeps the module's one invariant true: **a node is never placed later than its predecessor's drawn segment reaches** (node *i*−1, or the anchor for node 0). `nodeTimeRange(idx, ephemeris, displayDuration)` is the single statement of that rule and `retimeNode`'s precondition — with it holding, node times are already ordered, so `retimeNode` neither sorts nor moves the node's index. `applyNodeDv` discards downstream nodes unconditionally, with no zero-Δv guard — a caller that wants to skip a no-op edit (`PlanEditor.applyDv`) checks for that itself before calling, rather than `Plan` special-casing a magnitude it has no reason to inspect. The module-level `orbitPeriodOf(state, attractors)` (`attractor.ts`'s `orbitalElementsOf`'s `OrbitalElements.period`, around whichever body pulls hardest at `state` — never a plan-level setting — falling back to `APERIODIC_ARC_DURATION` when no finite period exists, e.g. hyperbolic orbits) feeds `segmentDurationFrom(state0, attractors, displayDuration)` — the shared definition of "how long a segment starting at this state may run," which `nodeTimeRange` and `plan-path.ts`'s `buildSegments` both call rather than each deriving it separately, so the legal node range and the drawn polyline can never disagree. `displayDuration` is typed as the narrow `DisplayDurationSource` interface (`{ durationSec(referencePeriod): number }`) rather than the concrete `DisplayTimeManager`, so this module — and the no-DOM `tsconfig.test.json` that compiles it directly for `test:physics` — never has to resolve `DisplayTimeManager`'s DOM-heavy dependency chain. No prediction cache lives here. **Each `Player` owns its own `Plan`** (`readonly plan = new Plan()`), which is what lets creative-mode multi-ship plans exist independently of one another.
- `plan-executor.ts`/`plan-executor-math.ts` — `PlanExecutor`: drives a `'powered'` ship's maneuver node through a state machine (idle → slew → armed → burn → trim → idle), owned one-per-`Player` (`readonly planExecutor: PlanExecutor`) the same way `plan: Plan` is. It receives the ship/HUD/sim-speed it acts on as `PlanExecutorShip`/`PlanExecutorHud`/`PlanExecutorSimSpeed` — narrow structural types covering only the members it actually reads/writes, **not** `Player`/`Hud`/`SimSpeedManager` themselves (the same reason `plan.ts` has `DisplayDurationSource` instead of importing `DisplayTimeManager`): `Player` drags in `three/webgpu` and DOM types transitively, and importing it — even `import type` — would still force the whole reachable graph through `tsc -p tsconfig.test.json`'s DOM-less, `three`-less compiler options, breaking `test:physics`'s ability to compile this file at all. `Player` itself is passed at every real call site without a cast, since it's a structural superset of `PlanExecutorShip`. `PlanExecutionMode` (`'off' | 'instant' | 'powered'`) is defined here, not on `Player`, for the same reason — `player.ts` re-exports it (`export type { PlanExecutionMode }`) rather than defining its own copy. **Node identity is tracked by reference (`targetNode: KinematicState | null`), never by `node.t`**: `Plan.applyNodeDv`/`retimeNode` replace a node with a new `KinematicState` at the *same* `t` (Δv is applied to the existing node, not appended as a new one), so comparing `t` alone would read a mid-burn Δv edit as "still the same node" and let the burn keep going in its old, now-stale direction. Every entry point (`update`/`applyIgnitionAndCutoff`/`nextEventTime`) re-checks `node !== this.targetNode` — a stale reference stops `applyIgnitionAndCutoff`/`nextEventTime` outright (they no-op until `update` has re-synced `targetNode` to the new node next frame), and `update` itself routes an identity change through the same `reset()`/`clearState()` every other "stop what's in flight" path uses, so `ship.thrust`/`ship.torque` can never survive across a node swap into the new node's (unrelated) burn.

  **Attitude is held inertial-fixed, not chased**: while idle/slew/armed, the target forward is `norm(node.v − ship.state.v)`, re-derived every `update()` call so it tracks the moving target right up to ignition; at ignition it's frozen into `burnDirWorld` (and a matching `burnUpWorld`) and never recomputed for the rest of the burn — a real high-thrust/short-duration burn is closer to an impulsive shot than a continuously-corrected one. The up reference defaults to the ship's own radial-out `state.r`, but `plan-executor-math.ts`'s `burnUpReference` falls back to the orbit normal (`kinematic-state.ts`'s `orbitAxes(state).nrm`) whenever the burn direction is itself ~radial — `r` and the orbit normal are orthogonal by construction, so a burn direction can never be singular against both at once, which is what keeps a pure radial-in/out node from deadlocking `qFromForwardUp` (angle stuck at 180°, torque stuck at zero, `slew` never exits) the way using `r` alone would. `updateBurnOutput` (the `burn`/`trim` half of `update`) keeps torquing toward the *frozen* `burnDirWorld`/`burnUpWorld` for the whole burn — attitude authority is never silently dropped mid-burn — separately from whether thrust is currently flowing.

  **A ship is not considered "close enough" to a node just because its current velocity happens to be near the node's target velocity** — on a periodic orbit, that coincidence recurs every revolution, not only right before the actual burn. `update()` gates the whole idle/slew/armed/finish path behind an approach window computed off the *current* Δv and attitude error, `C.NODE_APPROACH_LEAD + burnDurationFor(dvMag, accel) + turnTimeFor(errRad, C.MAX_ANG_ACCEL)` (`plan-executor-math.ts`): outside it, `update()` zeroes `ship.torque` and returns without evaluating alignment or the "Δv already ~0" finish condition at all, so a return visit to the same phase a revolution early can never be mistaken for the node's own approach. `turnTimeFor` is a bang-bang-control time estimate (`2√(θ/α)`) for the current alignment error — without it, the window only ever covered `NODE_APPROACH_LEAD` (10s) plus the burn itself, so a small-Δv node that happens to need close to a full reversal of attitude wouldn't leave `slew` enough real time to finish turning before the computed ignition instant, arriving late and burning asymmetrically around the node time. `C.NODE_APPROACH_LEAD` itself is left untouched (it's shared with `plan/plan-guide.ts`'s combat-view approach hint) — the turn-time term is `PlanExecutor`'s own addition on top.

  `applyIgnitionAndCutoff` opens with an `!ship.alive` check that calls `reset` (the same teardown a `planExecution !== 'powered'` ship gets in `update`) and returns — a ship destroyed mid-burn is no longer simulated (`GameEntity.stepSim` self-gates on `alive`), so nothing this class writes reaches the integrator either way, but without the check the invariant "burn state only exists while genuinely burning" would be false for the rest of that frame's substeps.

  `update(ship, dt, simTime, simSpeed)` (wall-clock `dt`, called from `CreativeStage.update` for *every* `entities.players` entry, not just the active one, since `Player.behave` — which would otherwise drive this — only runs for the operated ship) is also the sole place that keeps `ship.thrust` correct across an entire frame for the *active* ship: `Player.behave` runs before `CreativeStage.update` each frame and unconditionally overwrites `this.thrust` (to `null`, absent manual input) via `PlayerThrottle.updateThrustState` — if only `applyIgnitionAndCutoff` (below) wrote `ship.thrust`, that write would land mid-`Simulator.advance`, survive only until the *next* frame's `behave()` call nulls it again, and the ship would integrate with `thrust = null` for the first substep of every single frame. `update()` (and `updateBurnOutput` within it) re-asserts `ship.thrust` unconditionally every frame after `behave()` has run and before `Simulator.advance` — gated behind `simSpeed.canPlayerThrust` itself (`thrustGateOpen`, cached from the last `update()` call and reused by `applyIgnitionAndCutoff`/`nextEventTime`, not asked of the caller), since at high time-warp a substep can be tens of seconds long and an ungated single-substep 400 m/s² burn would deliver an absurd, uncontrolled Δv in one step; while burning, it also re-derives the output stage (full thrust,
