or `THROTTLE_LEVELS[0]` "trim" once the projected residual drops under `C.PLAN_EXECUTOR_TRIM_DV`) and the fuel-limited `pendingAccel`. `applyIgnitionAndCutoff(ship, simTime)` — called every `Stage.applySimulationEvents` boundary (`Simulator`'s existing per-substep event contract — see `simulator.ts`/`stage.ts` below) — is the simTime-precise half and **also** writes `ship.thrust`/`ship.torque` at the instant it ignites (armed → burn, freezing `burnDirWorld`/`burnUpWorld`), so the remaining substeps left in the *current* frame (after the event boundary but before the next `update()` call) see thrust immediately rather than waiting a frame: it only actually ignites once `simTime` reaches the closed-form `ignitionTimeFor(node.t, dv, accel)` (placed symmetrically around the node's own time so the burn is centered on it), and only cuts off (`finish`: `ship.thrust = null`, `ship.torque = v3()`, `ship.plan.dropNodesBefore` then **`Plan.overwriteAnchor(ship.state)`** — unlike `trackAnchor`, this replaces the anchor unconditionally even with nodes still queued behind it, which is what lets the *actual* (imperfect) post-burn state — not the node's ideal one — seed the rest of the plan's display, then a one-shot `hud.hint` reporting the residual Δv) once `burnCutoffProjection(node.v, ship.state.v, burnDirWorld) <= 0` — the *signed projection* of the remaining Δv onto the frozen burn direction, not its raw magnitude, since a magnitude threshold would oscillate right at the crossing (the projection instead falls monotonically through zero and never comes back). `nextEventTime(ship, simTime)` mirrors the same two closed-form/live-projection computations (returning `null` outright while `!thrustGateOpen`, since neither ignition nor cutoff can actually happen then) so `Simulator.advance`'s substep sizing lands exactly on the predicted ignition or cutoff instant regardless of frame rate — `applyIgnitionAndCutoff` is still safe to call every substep besides (both its ignition and cutoff checks are live-state comparisons, not "was this the scheduled instant" checks), so an off-target substep boundary from an unrelated event just no-ops here. Every exit path (`finish`/fuel-exhausted/mode-off/node-swap) funnels through the private `clearState`, the one place `ship.thrust`/`ship.torque` are actually zeroed — so no path can leave a stale command behind. `plan-executor-math.ts` holds the closed-form/projection/up-reference functions with no `Player`/THREE dependency, which is what lets them (and `plan-executor.ts` itself) compile under `tsconfig.test.json` for `test:physics` the same way `plan.ts` does.
- `plan-guide.ts` — `PlanGuide`: the combat-view execution guide for the nearest node, split across the two frame phases. `update(plan, player, simTime, editMode, attractors)` (`Game.update`, immediately before `Plan.trackAnchor` so a node dropped this frame lets the anchor resume tracking the player the same frame) drops overdue nodes via `plan.dropNodesBefore(simTime - C.NODE_EXPIRE_GRACE)`, then — once the nearest remaining node enters its execution window (`simTime >= node.t - C.NODE_APPROACH_LEAD`) — notifies approach and, once the player's and the node's strongest attractor agree and the module-level `orbitalElementsClose` also holds between the player's own elements (`player.orbitalElementsAround(strongestAttractor(player.state.r, attractors))`) and the node's target elements (`orbitalElementsOf(node, strongestAttractor(node.r, attractors))`), achievement — a node placed near a different attractor than the one the player currently orbits can never read as achieved, since comparing elements taken around different centers is meaningless; node consumption is purely time-based (`dropNodesBefore`), so the center check and `orbitalElementsClose` only gate the achievement hint. Each hint fires once per node: private `approachNotified`/`achievedNotified` hold the last-notified node and are compared by **identity**, since editing a node always replaces it with a new `KinematicState`. `sync(plan, player, simTime, editMode, project)` (`Game.sync`) only places the ◆NODE/⬢BURN markers for that same nearest node; the planned polyline itself is `PlanDisplay`'s, drawn in combat view too. `PlanGuide` takes only `Hud`/`Sfx`/`MarkerManager` — auto-warp cancellation near the node is `SimSpeedManager`'s own concern.
- `plan-editor.ts` — `PlanEditor`: **`plan` is a getter forwarding to `ship.plan`** (`Plan` is owned per-`Player`, not by `PlanEditor` — see `player.ts` above), so editing always targets whichever ship is currently active; `setActivePlayer(ship)` (called from `Game.setActivePlayer`) swaps `activePlayer` and clears the now-stale `selectedNodeIdx`/open menu, since those refer to positions in the *previous* ship's node list. When `ship` is `null` (creative mode before any ship is placed), `plan` falls back to a throwaway `detachedPlan` whose anchor is never advanced (nothing calls `trackAnchor` on it) and stays frozen at `Plan`'s zero-state default — i.e. sitting exactly on Earth's ECI origin. The private `hasPlan` getter (`= ship !== null`) is what keeps that degenerate plan from ever being computed or edited: `handleMapPointer` returns immediately when `!hasPlan`, and `update`/`sync` both fold `hasPlan` into their "is there anything to show" condition, so a map click can't land on the origin-anchored trajectory (which would otherwise sit right under the Earth icon on screen) and silently create a node in a plan nobody flies. `PlanEditor` otherwise owns the `editMode` flag, `selectedNodeIdx` (a plain `number | null` into `plan.nodes` — `deleteNode` clears it when the deleted range covers it), the `NodeGizmo`, the 6 `HudHoldButton`s (`dvButtons`, one per prograde/retrograde/normal/antinormal/radial-out/radial-in), the MANEUVER PLAN HUD panel, and `planDisplay` (below) — editing and future-display are both "what this plan is/looks like", so one owner. `update(simTime, displayTime)` (`Game.update`, before `MapPicker.refresh` so the apsis icons it emits are this frame's) forwards to `planDisplay.update`, and `sync(mapDist, simTime, fo, project)` drives `planDisplay.sync(fo, project, scale, overviewMode, cameraPos)` whenever `hasPlan && (editMode || plan.nodes.length > 0)` — the polyline is drawn in combat view too, since that's where the plan is actually flown; separate `hasPlan && editMode`-only calls to `syncGizmo`/`syncPanel` keep the node gizmo and the MANEUVER PLAN panel restricted to editing; `planDisplay.hide()` covers the remaining case (not editing, no nodes, or no ship). `simTime`/`displayTime` arrive as arguments from `game.ts`, resolved via `DisplayTimeManager` — `PlanEditor` never reads "when to look" itself. `updateEditing(dt, input)` only accumulates Δv; every DOM write it used to do is now in `syncPanel`. **A node can be created two ways**: `handleMapClick` (left-click on the drawn polyline) calls `plan.addNode` directly on the clicked `planDisplay.path.nearestSample`, while `addNodeAt(t)` turns a *time* into a node by first resolving `planDisplay.path.sampleAt(t)` — hinting instead of doing nothing if the plan doesn't reach that time — and is what `Game`'s "add node here" context-menu action (against a periapsis/apoapsis or relative-AN/DN icon) calls. `handleMapClick` otherwise selects the nearest existing node on a hit, else clears the selection — a click that lands on neither node nor polyline always deselects. Drag-to-retime / node context menu / `[X]` delete (`clearPlanByKey`) all read `planDisplay.path`'s screen picking (`projectPoint`/`nearestSample`) rather than a per-frame argument, since the node drag / context-menu handlers fire on DOM pointer events, outside the frame flow. A node drag picks only among the samples inside `Plan.nodeTimeRange(idx)`, so the pointer snaps to the nearest *legal* point instead of freezing at the limit. Derived-only values are never stored: per-node Δv is `node.v` minus the arrival velocity, and the arrival states come from `planDisplay.path.arrivalStates()` — the same integration that drew the line, so the panel's Δv and the drawn trajectory can't disagree. `onMapClosed` hides the panel and the gizmo, prunes the trailing run of near-zero-Δv nodes, then clears the selection.

`src/game/stages/`:
- `stage.ts` — `Stage`: the abstract per-stage contract (`init`/`update`/`checkWin`/`onWin`/`hudSubStatus`/`isUnlocked`), **the win/lose `phase`** (private, exposed as `phase`/`isPlaying` — `Game` only reads it), the default kill-count win condition, the shared `behaveAllEnemies`/`addEnemy` helpers, and the shared `recordEnemyDeath`/`recordPlayerLost` result bookkeeping — `recordPlayerLost` opens with `if (!this.isPlaying) return;`, the same guard `recordEnemyDeath` already had, so a loss that lands after the stage has already resolved (e.g. the player's own ship decaying into reentry the instant the last enemy dies) can't overwrite a `'won'` phase with `'lost'`. `setup()` also stashes the `EntityManager`/`MarkerManager`/`Ephemeris` it's handed as protected `_entities`/`_markerManager`/`_ephemeris` fields, since `CreativeStage` needs the latter two beyond construction time. **Both per-frame entry points take `player: Player | null`** — a stage runs every frame and the presence of a flown ship is an argument, not a reason to skip the call, which is what lets `CreativeStage` keep its placement preview alive during the very state it exists for (creative mode before the first ship is placed). The campaign stages open their `update` with `if (!this.isPlaying || !player) return;`; the base `sync` hides the status panel and `Logistics`' ▣ markers (their labels are self-relative distances) when there is no ship. Its per-frame `sync(player, fo, project, displayTime, overviewMode)` — `fo` is carried for subclasses that draw their own world-space geometry, currently `CreativeStage`'s preview ellipse — the `StageStatusPanel` it owns (`stage-utils/stage-status-panel.ts`: a three-column layout — left (`hudSubStatus()` text + kills, plus a persistent widget slot any concrete `Stage` can drop a DOM element into via `Stage.addStatusPanelWidget` → `StageStatusPanel.appendLeftWidget` — kept out of the text's own `innerHTML` rebuild so a widget's listeners survive), center (装甲 / エンジン出力 / 温度, with the shutdown ceiling `MAX_HULL_TEMP` shown alongside it, / 電力 in `fmtEnergy` joules-scale units, not a percentage), right (the two radiator deploy buttons) — shown only for stages whose `hudSubStatus()` returns a string; it takes the `Player` reference directly rather than a list of individual readouts. The radiator buttons *are* the deploy control (click toggles `RadiatorSystem.toggle`) — each shows the side's key mapping (`ラジエーター左`/`右`, mapped 9/0 — `RadiatorSide.up`/`down` is +X/-X, i.e. port/starboard), deploy state, and `wearOf` as a percentage in its label, plus a `fill` bar behind the label whose *width* shrinks with wear (so the button visibly loses "meat" as the panel takes hits) and whose color turns hot past half wear, else `ACCENT` while deployed. The radiator buttons and both non-center columns are built once as persistent DOM and updated by direct style/`innerHTML` writes on their own containers, unlike the center column which rebuilds its own `innerHTML` wholesale — the split matters because a persistent radiator button keeps its click listener across frames, which a from-scratch rebuild would drop) plus `Logistics`' ▣ AMMO markers. Game-owned resources arrive through a one-time `setup()` injection (not per-frame ctx) because instances are constructed before those resources exist; the injected set includes the `Simulator`, which is what lets `CreativeStage` read `simTime` at ship-placement confirm time (a DOM event outside the frame flow) without keeping a copy of it. Each concrete stage owns its own runtime state as instance fields.
- `stage-dictionary.ts` — `STAGE_CLASSES`/`STAGE_DEFINITIONS` (label/unlock-only instances, never `setup()`), `initStage()` (constructs the instance actually played, runs `setup`/`init` — forwarding the same `Ephemeris` instance `setup` now takes — sets initial ammo, toasts the briefing), and the `isStageId` guard. `CreativeStage` is deliberately **not** in `STAGE_DEFINITIONS`/`STAGE_CLASSES` — it isn't picked by `StageId` or shown in the stage-select tab, and `game.ts` constructs it directly on `launch.mode === 'creative'` instead of going through `initStage()`. `EphemerisConfig` (`{registry, originId, epochOffsetSec}`) is a static data declaration a `StageClass` may optionally carry as `ephemerisConfig`, not a `*Ctx` argument object — `ephemerisConfigFor(launch)` looks up the matching class's declared value (`undefined` for every stage but `stage-debug-alt-system.ts`) and `Game`'s constructor passes the three fields to `new Ephemeris(...)` individually, so a stage that says nothing gets exactly today's defaults.
- `stage0.ts` / `stage00.ts` / `stage1.ts` / `stage2.ts` / `stage-debug.ts` — the concrete stages: training scoreattack (time-limited), infinite survival (wave-spawning), the two LEO/Molniya campaign stages, and the debug stage. `StageDebug` places a single enemy group, overrides `checkWin()` to always return `false` so testing is never interrupted by a result screen (which also keeps it out of `UnlockManager`'s clear counts), and drops a `HudToggle` for `Enemy.fireEnabled` into the status window's left column via `Stage.addStatusPanelWidget` rather than owning a floating panel of its own. It sets `hiddenFromSelect`, so `launch-select.ts` skips it in the ステージモード button column and reaches it through a small corner link instead.
- `stage-debug-alt-system.ts` — the same `hiddenFromSelect` debug-only shape as `stage-debug.ts`, but its `static readonly ephemerisConfig` points `Ephemeris` at a small invented registry/origin instead of `SOLAR_SYSTEM`/`'earth'`, and `init` overwrites the default LEO initial state (meaningless against an invented `mu`/`radius`) with an orbit around that registry's own body. Exists to demonstrate, and to keep exercising, that the GUI (celestial names, coordinate-system choices, camera focus, lighting/SRP) degrades safely when the active registry has no Earth, no Sun, or nothing `CELESTIAL_BODIES` has a hand-authored view for — the real solar system stays intact in ECI throughout, simply too far away to matter.
- `stage-debug-load.ts` — the same `hiddenFromSelect` debug-only shape as `stage-debug.ts`, existing to reproduce heavy load on demand rather than only when it happens to occur: `init` scatters `DEBUG_LOAD_ASTEROID_COUNT` gravity-bearing `Asteroid`s and `DEBUG_LOAD_DEBRIS_COUNT` non-gravitating `DebrisPiece`s around the player, both placed by a `physics/vec3.ts` `mulberry32(DEBUG_LOAD_RNG_SEED)` stream so the same layout (and thus the same load) reproduces run to run. **The asteroid field is placed at the real main belt's number density** (`DEBUG_LOAD_ASTEROID_DENSITY` = 3e-29 /m³, ~1.9 million bodies over 1 km across between 2.1 and 3.3 AU): the rocks are 1 km class (`DEBUG_LOAD_ASTEROID_MASS`/`_RADIUS` = 1e12 kg / 500 m), and `DEBUG_LOAD_ASTEROID_MAX_DIST` is *derived* from the count and that density rather than being a chosen length, so the field can't drift into a configuration no star system could hold — 300 bodies at that density fill a sphere of radius 1.3e10 m, i.e. a mean separation of 3.2e6 km. The `DEBUG_LOAD_DEBRIS_COUNT` fragments keep their own much tighter `DEBUG_LOAD_DEBRIS_MAX_DIST` (250 km), since a breakup cloud really is local. Both are sampled uniformly *by volume*. `checkWin()` is always `false`, same as `StageDebug`, so a play session can be held at that load indefinitely.
- `creative-stage.ts` — `CreativeStage extends Stage`: a stage like any other (`checkWin()` always `false`, same as `StageDebug`, and `recordPlayerLost` overridden to just `hud.hint(reason)` instead of the inherited result-screen/`UnlockManager` path — so no result screen or clear-count bookkeeping ever fires either way, win or lose), used for the sandbox mode rather than a second axis alongside `Game`. `hudSubStatus()` returns `''` rather than the base class's `null` — `Stage.syncStatusPanel` only hides `StageStatusPanel` on `null`, so this is what makes the combat-view HP/thrust/temp/power gauges appear in creative mode too, with no stage-specific message line (an empty string still shows the kill counter/gauges, just without the leading text a real stage like `stage0.ts`'s countdown would put there). `Stage` is already the one thing `Game` holds exactly one of (win/lose `phase`, per-frame `update`, status panel, `Logistics`), so riding the existing single `activeStage` path costs nothing, whereas a `Game`-level `mode` field would put a branch through the whole of `Game.update`/`sync` — `Game` never learns which `Stage` implementation is running. `setup` overrides the base `Stage.setup` injection to also build the `ShipPlacerPanel` (below), shown only while `overviewMode` (`sync` forwards that flag straight through) — the `markerManager`/`ephemeris` it needs (building a ship's meshes/markers, and un-baking a moon-centered orbit into ECI) come from `Stage`'s own `_markerManager`/`_ephemeris` fields (see `stage.ts` below) rather than a second injection call. **The placement preview is split across the two phases like everything else**: `update` re-derives `preview` (the form's current orbit as `OrbitalElements` + the ship's would-be position, `null` while the panel is closed, in lagrange mode, or when the numbers don't parse) and `sync` pushes it into the white preview ellipse and the ▷ PREVIEW marker. Re-deriving it every frame rather than on a form-change callback is what keeps it honest — the placement is defined at the current `simTime`, which keeps advancing (under warp, fast) while the panel is open, so the panel needs no `onChange` notification at all and the preview can never show an orbit for a moment that has passed. `placeObject` turns a confirmed `ShipPlacerForm` into an `KinematicState` (`buildElementsState` via `elements.ts`'s `stateFromOrbitalElements`/`semiMajorFromPeriod`, or `buildLagrangeState` via `physics/halo.ts`) and dispatches on `form.objectType` (`player`/`enemy`/`ammo`/`base`) — a ship placement (refusing past `CREATIVE_MAX_SHIPS`) constructs a plain `Player` and calls `entities.addPlayer` directly, the same two calls `Game`'s constructor makes for the stage-mode ship, so placing a creative-mode ship and spawning the flown one are exactly the same operation; a base placement constructs `game-entity/base.ts`'s `Base` and calls `entities.addBase`. Each branch's fallback name (used when the panel's name field is left blank) is type-specific (`Player-N`/`Enemy-N`/`Ammo-N`/`Base-N`) — the panel itself never invents a name, so the fallback always matches what was actually placed. A placed `Base`'s mesh (`render/ships.ts`'s `buildBaseModel`, tens of meters across) reads as a speck next to LEO distances, so `sync` (which takes `player: Player | null`) also calls `syncBaseMarkers`, which in combat view puts one `mk-poi` `●` marker per living `entities.bases` entry (same symbol/class `FocusMarkers` uses for celestial labels, plus a paired `setBearing` arrow mirroring `Logistics.syncMarkers`' ammo bearing for an off-screen one) so a placed base stays findable at any zoom, and on the map instead puts a `mk-base` `▲` rotated to face the base's own ECI velocity via `MarkerManager.headingRotationDeg` (`mk-poi` is not reused there, since `FocusMarkers`' celestial labels share that class and must not pick up a rotation) — the label carries the live distance from the player (`fmtMarkerDist`) whenever a player exists. Keys are array-index-based (`base0`/`base0-bearing`, `base1`/`base1-bearing`, ...) and the previous frame's count is tracked to hide markers for entries a shrunk array no longer covers — the same pattern `Logistics.syncMarkers` uses for ammo pickups. `update` also drives every `entities.players`' `PlanExecutor.update(ship, dt, simSpeed)` regardless of which ship is active — `Player.behave` only runs for the operated ship, so a non-active `'powered'` ship's attitude alignment/output-stage selection has nowhere else to happen. Node consumption itself is `nextSimulationEventTime`/`applySimulationEvents`' job (`Stage`'s existing per-substep event-boundary contract, see `stage.ts`/`simulator.ts` below): for an `'instant'` ship, `plan.dropNodesBefore(simTime)` at the node's own time drops every node whose time has passed and returns the last one dropped (already a post-burn absolute state, so no finite-burn simulation is needed), which becomes the ship's new `state` directly (teleport); for a `'powered'` ship, `PlanExecutor.applyIgnitionAndCutoff(ship, simTime)` does the same node consumption through its own `finish()` (see `plan/plan-executor.ts` below) — the state is *not* overwritten, so the achieved Δv's error against the plan stays visible. `Player.behave` (AI-free — a placed ship neither fires nor thrusts on its own) runs only for the active ship (`Game.update`'s single `this.player.behave(...)`), so an inactive placed ship costs only integration/attitude/render sync, never belt/thermal/radiator/power.
- `src/game/creative/ship-placer-panel.ts` — `ShipPlacerPanel`/`ShipPlacerForm`: the マップモード-only 艦艇配置 panel `CreativeStage` owns. Two `placementMode`s — `'elements'` (a reference body, an exclusive `sizeMode` tab over 近地点+遠地点/半長軸+離心率/周期+離心率, and 傾斜角/Ω/ω/ν) and `'lagrange'` (系・点・halo/lissajous、面内/面外振幅) — sit in separate DOM groups the panel shows/hides by `placementMode`, so the confirm handler only ever needs one branch's worth of fields. The panel does no physics itself: it collects the raw form values into `ShipPlacerForm` and hands them to `onConfirm` (plus `isOpen`, which `CreativeStage` polls each frame to decide whether to derive a preview — there is no change notification), leaving `stateFromOrbitalElements`/`semiMajorFromPeriod`/`haloState`/`lissajousState` (all in `physics/`) to turn them into an `KinematicState` — same "form owns input, caller owns conversion" split as `plan-editor.ts`'s Δv panel. Both the reference-body list and the Lagrange-system list are built from the same `ORBITING_IDS` — every orbiting body in the active registry (a star is excluded because a ship cannot orbit the thing the ellipse is centered on). `LAGRANGE_DEFAULT_AMPLITUDE_KM: Partial<Record<OrbitingId, {ax, az}>>` only has hand-picked defaults for a few systems; any other system falls back to deriving its default amplitude from that system's own primary-secondary distance times a ratio, so a newly registered gravity source needs no entry here to work.
- `src/game/creative/duplicate-form.ts` — `elementsFormFromState(state, bodies)`: the inverse of `buildElementsState` (above) — takes a live entity's current `KinematicState` and derives the `ElementsForm` that would reproduce its orbit, for the "duplicate" menu item. It's pure inversion of existing geometry, not new orbital math: the reference body is `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor` (falling back to Earth if that's a star, since the panel's reference-body control never offers one), the osculating elements are `attractor.ts`'s `orbitalElementsOf`, the apsis altitudes are `elements.ts`'s `apsisAltitudes`, and the true anomaly is `trueAnomalyAt` against the center-relative state (`frame.ts`'s `toFrameState`). The one piece with no existing counterpart is recovering Ω/ω from the elements' `hHat`/`pHat` (`raanArgpFromBasis`), since nothing else in the codebase needed to invert `elements.ts`'s `orbitPlaneBasis`; an equatorial orbit's undefined node falls back to Ω=0 by convention. Returns `null` for a hyperbolic/parabolic state (`e >= 1`), since neither an apoapsis altitude nor a meaningful reference body selection exists there — the caller opens the panel as a plain new placement instead. The duplicated true anomaly is **not offset** — the panel opens with the duplicate sitting exactly on top of the original, letting the person placing it re-aim ν (and everything else) before confirming, rather than guessing an offset that would just need to be edited away for the common case of wanting a near-identical clone.
- `stage-utils/logistics.ts` — `Logistics`: ammo-pickup spawn/pickup/despawn logic plus the ▣ AMMO markers that point at them (map view instead uses a `▲` rotated to face the pickup's own ECI velocity, via `MarkerManager.headingRotationDeg`), with a hollow △ screen-edge bearing arrow while a pickup is off-screen in combat view (`syncMarkers(player, project, scale, displayTime, overviewMode)`, driven from `Stage.sync`, hiding every marker while `player` is null since the labels are self-relative distances; reads each `Ammo.displayState(displayTime)` rather than `.state` so the marker tracks the same future-ghost position as the pickup’s own mesh, and drops the marker once that’s `null` beyond the pickup’s prediction horizon — marker keys are numbered over just the pickups that got one, so a dead or out-of-horizon pickup never leaves a gap), shared by all stages via `Stage.setup`. Automatic resupply is gated two ways, both checked in `updateLogistics(simTime, player, simSpeed, respawnOnDespawn)`: the public `resupplyEnabled` flag (creative mode's LOGISTICS panel toggle — see `creative-stage.ts` above) and `SimSpeedManager.canResupplyAmmo`. `simSpeed` arrives as a per-frame argument rather than a constructor reference because every caller is a `Stage.update` that already holds it, the same shape `Stage.behaveAllEnemies` uses. Absorption and despawn are deliberately *not* gated — those settle pickups that already exist, a separate question from whether new ones appear — and while resupply is blocked the interval timer is left un-advanced, so re-enabling it resupplies on the next frame instead of carrying the blocked stretch forward as dead time. The stage-scripted one-shot `spawnForPlayer` calls (stage0/stage00/stage-debug's wave and opening drops) bypass both gates: they are scripted events, not periodic resupply, and their wave machines count on the spawn actually happening.
- `stage-utils/score-counter.ts` — `ScoreCounter`: pure shot/hit/kill/loss tallies; result side effects (notifications, win/lose) live on `Stage`.
- `stage-utils/score-attack-timer.ts` — `ScoreAttackTimer`: stage0's countdown.
- `spawner/enemy-generator.ts` — builds individual `Enemy`s from orbital-element/phasing parameters (phased/coelliptic/crossing/elliptic/Molniya presets).
- `spawner/enemy-spawner.ts` — builds enemy *groups* by calling `enemy-generator.ts` repeatedly; only `generateCluster` (stage0's training cluster) remains here — stage00's wait-for-ammo → wave-approach → active-combat phase machine and its wave-formation generation live directly in `stage00.ts` (they're used nowhere else, so there's no shared abstraction to keep separate).

- `src/game/sim-speed-manager.ts` — `SimSpeedManager`: the sim-speed stage (`SIM_SPEED_LEVELS`, ×1 to ×131072; shown to the player and referenced in SFX as "warp"), the `[N]`-key auto-warp-to-node behavior (`toggleAutoWarpToFirstNode`/`startAutoWarpTo`/`cancelAutoWarp`/`update`), and the **`can*` predicates** (`canPlayerThrust`/`canPlayerFire`/`canEnemyFire`/`canResolvePhysicalCollisions`/`canResupplyAmmo`) — callers ask these instead of comparing `simSpeed` against `MAX_PHYS_SIM_SPEED` themselves. `canResupplyAmmo` (`Logistics`' gate) is the one predicate with a *different* threshold — `simSpeed < MAX_PHYS_SIM_SPEED`, i.e. ×1 only, one step stricter than the rest — because a resupply pickup exists to be flown to and collected, and collection is already dead at the warp levels the other predicates still allow; spawning at those levels would only pile up pickups nobody can reach. `estimatedRealSecondsToWarpEnd(simTime)` answers "how many real seconds until the current auto-warp stops" for the HUD: it can't just divide the remaining sim time by the current speed, since `update`'s own step-selection rule (the largest level at or below `remaining / AUTOWARP_MARGIN`) means the level keeps dropping as the remaining time shrinks — high-multiplier stretches are short and low-multiplier ones are long, so the estimate replays that same step-down rule and sums the real time spent at each level. Returns `null` when no auto-warp is active.
- `src/game/theme.ts` — the sole definition source for the dark-theme color constants (`ACCENT`, `SURFACE`, `EDGE`, `TEXT`, ...), shared by `hud/`, `main.ts`, `plan/node-gizmo.ts`, `input/touch.ts`.
- `src/game/vfx/effects-system.ts` / `vfx/flash-effect-manager.ts` — the flash/debris spawn API (`EffectsSystem`, the sole place `Player`/`Enemy`/`PlayerFire`/`Stage` reach to spawn VFX or add debris) and the flashes themselves (`FlashEffectManager`), split across the two phases: `update(dt, simDt)` ages each flash, advects it by its source velocity and drops the expired ones; `sync(fo, camera)` only pushes the survivors into their billboards. Because the ageing is on the update side, flashes freeze while the game is paused and keep animating after a win/lose (`Game.update`'s stage-decided path still calls `update`).
- `src/game/nan-watchdog.ts` — `NanWatchdog`: シミュレーション状態に非有限値(NaN/Infinity)が入った**最初のフェーズ**を捕まえてコンソールと HUD へ報告する。**Game が所有し、`update` の各フェーズ境界で呼ぶ。** 一度検出したら以後は何もしない。存在理由は、NaN が静かに伝播したあと別の顔で表面化するため — 自機が汚染されると `FloatingOrigin` 経由で全メッシュが消えて 3D だけが暗転し、敵が汚染されると `Enemy.checkLoss` の `hitCelestialBody` 判定が false になって「再突入により喪失」と誤表示され、非有限な座標は空間グリッドのセル添字自体を壊す。伝播を止めるガードは `simulation/contact.ts` 側にあり、検出と報告はこちらの責務。
- `src/game/debug-trajectory-line.ts` — `DebugTrajectoryLine`: `?debugLines=1` debug overlay (self-contained URL-param gate, same pattern as `perf-meter.ts`'s `?perf=1` — `enabled` is read once in the constructor, and `sync` is a no-op with zero `THREE` objects created when it's off) drawing each tracked `GameEntity`'s `actualTrajectory.history` (past) + `actualTrajectory.state` + `predictedTrajectory?.history` + `predictedTrajectory?.state` (future, if any) as one `SampledLine` per entity. Takes the target set as a `sync` argument every frame (default: player + the current target) rather than having `GameEntity` own a line itself — the "which entities get a line, drawn how" display spec isn't decided yet (`better_predict.md` §6), and owning it on the entity would silently lock in "every entity always has one" as that decision. Past/future share `sampleInterval`, so the two halves meet at the same density at the current-time point. Creating a line on demand and disposing it once an entity leaves the target set is `game/entity-line-set.ts`'s `EntityLineSet`, so switching targets can't leak `THREE` geometry. Concatenating the two halves would otherwise mint a fresh array every frame even when neither half changed — defeating `SampledLine.syncGeometry`'s reference-equality re-bake guard — so a per-entity `concatCache` remembers the last `(current, predicted)` reference pair alongside their concatenation and reuses it verbatim whenever both inputs are unchanged.
- `src/game/targeter.ts` — `Targeter`: the combat target — **`target`/`secondaryTarget` are the only state, both `Enemy | null`, and there is no auto-selection** (`aliveTarget`/`aliveSecondaryTarget` are the read side, hiding a dead individual rather than a live re-pick). Both are set only through a right-click context menu: `updateCombatTargeting(player, enemies, input, project)` takes a right click, and only if it lands within `TARGET_LOCK_PICK_PX_SQ` of a living `Enemy` (`pickEnemyAt`, wrapping each live `Enemy` as a `MapPickable` — `kind: 'ship'` — so the nearest-on-screen search is `map-pick.ts`'s shared `pickNearest`) opens a `ContextMenu` offering "set/clear target" and "set/clear secondary target" against that hit; a miss opens a second `ContextMenu<true, MenuAction>` (`emptySpaceMenu` — a target-less menu over the same `true`-stands-in-for-target shape `ViewBadge` uses) offering "open settings menu" (`SettingsPanel.toggle(true)`, injected by constructor since the callback fires from a DOM pointer event outside the frame flow), so a right click always opens *some* menu in combat view. Setting one target clears it from the other role (no double duty). The secondary target is **display-only**: it gets its own cyan `secondaryOrbitLine` (`ACCENT_SECONDARY`, `theme.ts`) and enemy-list/marker highlighting, but the target-board bullet-pass marks (`updateBoardMarks`/`syncBoardMarkers`) and the ◇/◆ target-bearing direction markers are primary-only — `Enemy.markerItem(role, ...)` is what the marker set reads the primary/secondary/none distinction from. The relative AN/DN markers live on `NavTarget`, not here (see `nav-target.ts` above for why the two designations are separate classes). All of it goes out through one public `sync`.
- `src/game/entity-line-set.ts` — `EntityLineSet`: the per-entity `SampledLine` bookkeeping shared by every module that draws one line per `GameEntity` — `lineFor(entity)` creates on demand and registers into the scene, `pruneTo(alive)` removes and disposes whatever is no longer in the set. It knows nothing about how a line looks: the colour/opacity/`renderOrder` come from the `factory` its owner passes in. Note `lineFor` *creates* — a caller that only wants to know whether a line exists must track that itself rather than calling in (see `PredictedTrajectoryLine.hasLineFor`).
- `src/game/predicted-trajectory-line.ts` — `PredictedTrajectoryLine`: draws each target's `predictedTrajectory` (the future the integrator has actually reached, no history) as a solid `SampledLine`, coloured `0xbfc9d4`/0.55 at `renderOrder` 1 — deliberately identical to `Player.orbitLine`, since it stands in for that analytic ellipse rather than adding a second line beside it. Owned by `Game`, which passes the target set every frame (today: the active ship alone, or nothing when there is no living ship) and bakes into `PlanDisplay.planFrame` so the predicted and planned lines share one coordinate frame and can be compared on screen. `hasLineFor(entity)` answers whether that entity's line is actually drawn, and is what `Game.sync` feeds to `Player.orbitLine.setSuppressed` — so the analytic ellipse only appears while the prediction is too short to draw (a freshly placed ship, or time-warp outrunning `Predictor`'s growth budget), which is the fallback that makes replacing it safe. It holds its own `Set` of the entities it drew this frame, because `EntityLineSet.lineFor` would otherwise mint a line just to be asked about one.
- `src/game/unlock-manager.ts` — `UnlockManager`: per-stage clear-count persistence (localStorage is the source of truth) and unlock-toast notification; `Stage.isUnlocked` stays a pure predicate over the counts.
- `src/game/save/` + `save-data.ts` — セーブは**セーブデータ(歴史線)⊃ ステージ履歴 ⊃ スナップショット**の三階層。**Every save-carrying class owns its own `serialize()`/`static restore(...)`, and a composite owner just calls its children's and assembles the result** — `save-data.ts` (`GameSaveData` and its per-class fields, e.g. `PlayerSaveData`/`EnemySaveData`/`BaseSaveData`/`StageSaveData`, plus `CameraSaveData`/`ChaseCameraSaveData`/`OverviewCameraSaveData`/`FocusTargetSaveData` for the camera viewpoint, and the index-side `SnapshotMeta`/`StageHistoryMeta`/`SaveSlotMeta`/`SaveIndex`/`SlotExport` and the `SAVE_VERSION`/`SLOT_EXPORT_*` constants both sides of a boundary must agree on) is pure type definitions, never logic. `save/` splits the work by what each part is allowed to know:
  - `save-store.ts` — `SaveStore` (interface) / `LocalStorageSaveStore`: JSON persistence only, `Game`-free. The index lives at `tepui.saveIndex`, each snapshot body at `tepui.snapshot.<id>` — **split so the browsing UI can list snapshots without reading a single body** (a body carries every ship/enemy/base/part and runs tens of KB). Reads swallow their errors and return `null`; **writes deliberately let the exception through**, since the caller is the one that can free space and retry.
  - `save-slots.ts` — `SaveSlots`: the `SaveIndex` is its own field, and it is the sole thing that edits it (slot create/rename/delete/duplicate, stage histories, snapshot metas, pruning). Snapshot bodies are always `SaveStore`'s. **Retention hangs on one axis, `SnapshotMeta.pinned`** — unpinned autos are a per-history ring buffer (`AUTO_SNAPSHOT_LIMIT` = 12, oldest dropped), pinned ones are never pruned and instead refuse past `PINNED_SNAPSHOT_LIMIT` (30) rather than silently evicting, since clipping *is* the declaration that a snapshot must not disappear. `SnapshotKind` (由来: how it was taken) and `pinned` (保持: whether it survives pruning) are separate fields on purpose: promoting an auto snapshot to `kind: 'manual'` on clip would erase which trigger produced it. `addSnapshot` retries a quota-failed write while dropping its oldest unpinned neighbour, and `duplicateSlot` routes through `importSlot` so the "copy bodies, re-mint ids, roll back what was written on failure" path exists once — `SaveBrowser`'s 「ここから分岐」 is exactly `duplicateSlot` with a cut-off snapshot.
  - `snapshot-service.ts` — `SnapshotService`: the `Game` ⇄ `GameSaveData` bridge. `capture` walks `Game`'s own arrays (`entities.players`/`.enemies`/`.ammos`/`.bases`, `activeStage`) calling each entity's `serialize()`, also calls `game.viewManager.serializeView()`/`game.cameraSystem.serialize()` for the `camera` field, derives the list-facing `SnapshotMeta` through `hud/orbit-info.ts`'s `orbitInfo` (never re-deriving centre/altitude/speed itself) and hands both to `SaveSlots`. `restore` is the mirror, and afterwards calls `SaveSlots.discardAfter` — everything unpinned later than the restored point is a future that did not happen, the same "editing invalidates downstream" rule `Plan`'s node edits follow. `GameSaveData.earthSpinPhase0` (optional, matching `bases`' own backward-compat pattern rather than a `SAVE_VERSION` bump) rides alongside `phaseOffsets` for the same reason — `EarthBody`'s random initial spin phase is a per-game phase exactly like `Ephemeris`'s, just owned outside `Ephemeris` itself (Earth's spin isn't part of the analytic ephemeris model); `Game.restore` only calls `environment.setEarthSpinPhase0` when the field is present, so an old snapshot leaves the session's already-randomized phase in place instead of forcing it to 0.
  - `autosave.ts` — `AutoSave`: **when** to capture, and nothing else. Currently the 直前枠 only (`AUTOSAVE_INTERVAL_REAL_SEC` = 60 real seconds, measured off `performance.now()` since it must not scale with time warp); the event/PNR triggers are `memos/mikanixonable/SAVE_SLOT_PLAN_2026-08-09.md` §6-3b/§6-3c.
  - `save-transfer.ts` — file I/O and the validation of foreign JSON (`exportSlotToFile`/`importSlotFromFile`/`pickAndImportSlot`). Separate from both of the above because neither the index nor the store should learn about `File`/`Blob`/`<a download>`. A `SlotExport` carries a `format` identifier so an unrelated JSON reads as "not a save file" rather than "corrupt save", validation narrows from `unknown` step by step, and a snapshot whose body is missing or whose `version` doesn't match is dropped from the metas rather than aborting the whole import. Import always lands as a **new** slot with freshly minted ids — re-reading the same file must not overwrite what is already there.
  - `legacy-save.ts` — one-shot adoption of the single-slot-era `tepui.save` key.
  `Game.restore` clears `entities`, reconstructs every entity via its class's `static restore(...)`, and delegates ownership-scoped state to its owner the same way (`Stage.restore` for score/phase/`Logistics`, `Ship.restoreOverallHp` for the total-HP-only entities that don't save a full `parts` array). A `Ship` subclass that saves its full `parts` array (`Player`) restores HP by replacing `parts` outright and calling `refreshFromParts()`; one that doesn't (`Enemy` — only a scalar `health` is saved) instead rebuilds default parts fresh and calls `Ship.restoreOverallHp(savedHp)`, which prorates each part's HP to reproduce the saved total — both routes converge on `parts` as hp's one source of truth, per the `Ship` architecture note above. `GamePhase` values other than `'playing'` restore as plain data (`Stage._phase = data.phase`) without replaying `showResultScreen`, which is why both `AutoSave` and the `[F5]` clip refuse to write at all once `!activeStage.isPlaying` — `Game.update` early-returns whenever the stage isn't playing, so a phase restored with no screen behind it would load into a frozen, unresponsive state. `ViewManager.restoreView` is called unconditionally near the end of `restore` — with `data.camera?.view` when a snapshot carries one, otherwise `viewManager.serializeView()` (the view already current), so a snapshot with no `camera` field still runs `setView`'s `applyChrome` and lands with the camera/plan/future-display flags and HUD chrome in sync with whatever view was already showing, rather than leaving `displayTimeManager.forceCurrent` and the rest at whatever they happened to be before the load. `CameraSystem.restore` runs right after, only when `data.camera` is present, applied after `setActivePlayer` has re-pointed `ChaseCamera`'s ship reference, since `ChaseCamera.restore` writes `rot`/`dist`/`panEci` directly rather than through the `camFollowAttitude` setter's read-normalize; a snapshot with no `camera` field leaves the camera at its default viewpoint.
- `src/game/hud/save-browser.ts` — `SaveBrowser`: the full-screen セーブデータ/スナップショット window, opened from the `[Esc]` menu's スナップショット item or `[F9]`. A **one-shot modal** like `DockView` (it rebuilds its own `innerHTML` on every action and has no per-frame `sync`, so the differential-DOM rule the per-frame panels follow doesn't apply), owned by `main.ts` and injected into `Game` via `setSaveBrowser` — it needs the `Game` that is constructed before it, the same late-injection shape as `ViewManager.setDocking`. Left pane = slots (rename/duplicate/export/delete/play), right pane = that slot's snapshots **split into クリップ済み and 自動 sections**, so "these will disappear on their own" is visible before anything is clicked rather than after. Every name it prints goes through an escape helper first, since slot and snapshot names come from `prompt()` and from imported files alike. Loading is a double-click on the snapshot card itself (there is no separate load button), offered only while the viewed slot is the active one (a different history line's state cannot be restored into the running game) — an ineligible card carries no `sb-snap-loadable` class/cursor and its double-click reports the reason via `setStatus` instead of restoring.
- `src/game/input/input.ts` / `input/touch.ts` — keyboard/mouse/pointer edge-trigger queue (`Input`: `update()` freezes one frame's snapshot of presses/clicks/double-clicks/right-clicks/mouse deltas; middle-drag pan and two-finger pinch are folded into the same `MouseDelta`). Double-clicks are the one edge queue sourced from a native browser event (`dblclick`, left button only) rather than this class's own pointerdown/up drag-threshold logic — double-click timing/movement judgment is left to the browser instead of reimplemented, and it queues independently of (and in addition to) the two single clicks `dblclick` implies, since `takeClicks` already consumes those through the ordinary click path. Edges are handed out **first-come-first-served**: `takeKey(code)`, `takeKeys(handler)`, `takeClicks(handler)`, `takeDoubleClicks(handler)`, `takeRightClicks(handler)` remove any event whose handler returns true, so a later module never sees it. `down(code)`/`mouse()` stay plain reads. The held-key set is dropped wholesale (`releaseAll`) on blur / `pagehide` / `visibilitychange`-to-hidden **and on the Command key's own `keyup`** — macOS withholds every other key's `keyup` while Command is held, so a key released during e.g. `Cmd+Shift+5` would otherwise stay held forever (thrust that never stops); which keys are still physically down is unknowable at that point, so all of them are released. This is what lets each module own its own keys — see `Game.handleInput` and the touch virtual-pad overlay (`TouchControls`, feeding the same `Input.setVirtualKey`; `syncModeButtons(rcsDamp, fineAttitude, progradeHold)` is its only per-frame entry point — it owns the code↔button mapping, the caller passes only the current values).
- `src/physics/atmosphere.ts` — piecewise-exponential density model (Vallado table, 0–1000 km), plus the co-rotating atmosphere built on it: `airspeed(r, v)` (v minus `EARTH_OMEGA`×r about the Y/polar axis) and `dragAccel(r, v, bcInv)` (Cd·A/m per entity class), both folded into `dynamics.ts`'s acceleration composition; player hull temp uses Sutton–Graves stagnation heating + Stefan-Boltzmann radiative cooling (see `updateThermal` in `player/thermal.ts`).
- `src/game/hud/` — the DOM-overlay HUD, split by concern: `hud.ts` (`Hud` — just the shell: `root`/`layers`/`svgOverlay` for others to attach to, toast/hint/help, and `panels`), `overlay-layer.ts` (`buildOverlayLayers`/`bringToFront` — **the single definition of the HUD's stacking order, and the only module allowed to assign a `z-index`.** Seven fixed `<div>`s are appended to `#hud` once, back to front: `marker` (world-space markers + the SVG overlay) / `panel` (the always-on docked/status/navball/plan/creative panels) / `window` (`PropertyWindow`) / `popup` (`ContextMenu`/`ObjectPicker` popups) / `view` (`DockView`, the one full-screen view that must cover the panels/windows beneath it) / `notify` (toast/hint/the view badge) / `system` (help/end-screen/settings-panel/save-browser) — `z-index: 10` through `16` in that order. Every other DOM owner picks which layer it's a child of (via a constructor argument threaded down from `Hud.layers`, e.g. `panelRoot`/`popupRoot`) and never sets a `z-index` of its own; ordering *within* a layer is plain DOM append order, and `bringToFront(el)` — re-`appendChild`ing `el` to its own parent — is the sole way to move something to the front of its layer. A popup that opens from inside another popup (an `ObjectPicker` inside a `PropertyWindow`, a `ContextMenu` inside a placement panel) calls `bringToFront` on itself at open time, so "opened from X draws above X" falls out of the layer split alone, with no per-widget z-index bookkeeping that could get the ordering wrong), `dom.ts` (`buildHudDom` — static DOM/style construction, including the CSS for panels other modules build; calls `buildOverlayLayers` once and is the one place that decides, per element it builds, which layer it becomes a child of. Anything painted at HUD level must be given a layer, because markers are created at runtime and would otherwise land on top of everything by DOM order. `input/touch.ts`'s virtual pad sits below `#hud` entirely for the same reason — it drives the game world, so it must not cover a system window. `buildCollapseToggle(root, id, className, target, labels)`/`syncCollapseToggle` are the one implementation of "a button that toggles `target`'s `.collapsed` class and keeps its own glyph/`aria-expanded`/`title` in sync with it," parameterized by a `CollapseToggleLabels` (glyph/title pairs) so a caller only supplies which labels it wants — `buildDockToggle` builds the left/right map-panel dock toggles this way, and `display-time-panel.ts`'s `DisplayTimePanel` constructor builds the PREDICT bar's own fold toggle the same way with the exported `PREDICT_TOGGLE_LABELS`. `resetHudDocks(root)` re-expands every such toggle at once (both docks plus the PREDICT bar) — `ViewManager.applyChrome` calls it on leaving map view, so nothing collapsed while inside the map stays collapsed once you're back in combat view), `panel.ts` (`HudPanels` — status/target/enemy-list panel sync; takes the `Game` instance directly since it cherry-picks from the whole game state rather than a narrow ctx, but is **display-only** — it writes its own four panels and touches no other module's state or DOM. `sync(game, bodies)` derives each row's values via `orbit-info.ts`'s `orbitInfo`/`relativeInfo` and only formats/writes them — altitude is measured from the resolved center's own surface, not Earth's, and the player/target relative-inclination row (`relIncDeg`) is `NaN` whenever the two centers disagree, since comparing `hHat` across different centers has no meaning. `sync` also hides `#hud-orbit`/`#hud-enemies` in map view — both are combat-view-only, since map view shows an object's orbital elements through its own `property-window.ts` window's `'軌道'` group instead), `orbit-info.ts` (`orbitInfo(entity, bodies)`/`relativeInfo(self, other, bodies)` — the DOM-free derivation of an entity's resolved center/altitude/speed/apsides/inclination/period, and of one entity's distance/closing-speed/relative-speed/relative-inclination against another; both route the center resolution through `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor` and the shape through `elements.ts`'s `apsisAltitudes`, so `HudPanels` and any other consumer needing the same numbers share one implementation rather than rederiving them), `buttons.ts` (`SegmentedControl` / `hudButton` / `HudToggle` / `HudHoldButton` — the exclusive-select button row (whose `setItems` rebuilds the choices in place, keeping the heading, for callers that narrow what is selectable rather than offering an option and then refusing it — a no-op when `items` is contentwise identical to what's already shown, so a caller recomputing the same choices every frame doesn't drop a button mid-click), the one-shot button, the sliding ON/OFF switch, and the press-and-hold button (`isHeld`, read every frame by the caller rather than driven by a `setInterval`, matching the per-frame-poll style every other continuous input in this codebase uses), DOM/event handling only; shared by the map-mode panels — `PlanEditor`'s MANEUVER PLAN panel uses `HudHoldButton` for its six Δv direction buttons. Anything here that takes a click needs `pointer-events: auto` in `dom.ts`'s STYLE, since `#hud` itself is `pointer-events: none`, **`pointer-events` is an inherited property**, and a click that falls through reaches the canvas and fires the gun. The same reset (`#hud, #hud * { margin: 0; padding: 0 }`) outranks bare class selectors by ID specificity, so any rule that sets margin/padding/width on a HUD child must be scoped by an id — see the `#dock-view …` rules), `frame-labels.ts` (`celestialBodyName(id)` — the `AttractorId` → display-name lookup, reading `celestial-registry.ts`'s `CELESTIAL_BODIES` for a real solar-system body and falling back to the bare id for one a custom registry invented, shared by `HudPanels`' ORBIT/TARGET center-body rows, `camera/focus-markers.ts`'s celestial labels, `hud/object-picker.ts`'s rows and `creative/ship-placer-panel.ts`'s reference-body control, so no caller spells out "地球"/"月"/"太陽" itself), `object-picker.ts` (`ObjectPicker<T>` — the trigger button + popup used wherever the choices number in the dozens: a filter input, then the rest grouped and laid out as a multi-column grid (`.op-grid`, `repeat(auto-fill, minmax(140px, 1fr))`) so a hundred-item candidate set doesn't need a page-length scroll. `setGroups` skips the rebuild when the incoming groups are contentwise identical to what's already shown (label + each item's `[value, label]` pair, value by reference) — callers such as `FramePicker`/`ShipPlacerPanel` pass the same groups every frame, and an unconditional rebuild would fight an open popup's own filter/hover state. **`SegmentedControl` stays for anything with a handful of fixed choices** — it lays its items out in one flex row with no wrap/scroll/truncate handling, which is exactly why a registry-sized list needs a different widget rather than a wider row. Closes on a capture-phase outside pointerdown, the same rule `ContextMenu` uses; the popup is a child of the `popupRoot` its constructor is given — always the `overlay-layer.ts` popup layer — and calls `bringToFront` on itself when opened), `object-groups.ts` (`groupPickables(registry, items)` — the pure `MapPickable[]` → ジャンル別 `ObjectPickerGroup<string>[]` classifier `AnchorZone`'s object-picker builds on: `'body'` items split into ラグランジュ点(id が `-l1`〜`-l5` で終わるもの、`LAGRANGE_ID`/`lagrangeParentId` もここに置く)か `body-class.ts`'s `bodyClassOf` の5分類、`'player'`/`'ship'`/`'base'`/`'ammo'` はそのままジャンル、マーカー由来の kind(`apsis`/`relnode`/`eqnode`/`empty-space`)は出さない — 空のグループは返さない), `anchor-zone.ts` (`AnchorZone` — マップの座標系UIのうち「何に固定/追随するか」を選ばせる1ゾーン: 上段は登録天体・自艦・敵・基地・弾薬まで含む全候補から選ぶ `ObjectPicker`、下段は呼び出し側(`FrameControls`)が `celestial/body-visibility.ts`'s `systemMembersAt` で絞った天体列を `setNearby(members, pickables)` で受け取って並べるだけのクイック `SegmentedControl` — 押せてから拒否されることのないよう、`pickables` に無い id はクイックボタンに出さない。コンストラクタの `releaseLabel` が `null` なら「固定を解除」の選択肢自体を出さない(並進ゾーンの用途)。値は `string | null` — `null` が解除で、`onSelect` はどちらのゾーンでも同じ値を受け取る)、`rotation-zone.ts` (`RotationZone` — 同じUIのうち「何の公転に合わせて回すか」を選ばせるゾーン。`setNearby(members)` で受け取った天体列のうち `primaryOf` が非 `null` を返すもの(恒星以外)だけを `OrbitingId | null` の `SegmentedControl` として並べる — 恒星の除外はこのクラス自身の判断で、渡ってくる `members` 自体は恒星を含む。原点込みの座標系そのものは呼び出し側が `Ephemeris.frameOf` で組む — このクラスは回転対象の id だけを返す), `settings-panel.ts` (`SettingsPanel` — the ⚙/`[Esc]` pause-menu; `main.ts` owns the instance and wires its `onSettingsOpenChange` to `Game.pause()`/`Game.resume()`), `context-menu.ts` (`ContextMenu<T>` — a generic screen-positioned popup that holds the target `T` it was opened against, so `onSelect(act, target)` always reports back which item was chosen for which target without the caller tracking that itself; shared by `MapPicker` (`ContextMenu<MapPickable>`, the `'empty-space'` right-click menu only — every other `MapPickable` kind opens a `property-window.ts` `PropertyWindow` instead, see `map-picker.ts` above), `Targeter` (`ContextMenu<Enemy>`, the target-lock menu), `NodeGizmo` (`ContextMenu<number>`, over a node index), and `ViewBadge` (`ContextMenu<true, ViewId>` — no real target, so `true` stands in for it; `ContextMenu` only fires `onSelect` when its target is non-`null`, which is the one thing a target-less menu has to route around). DOM/event handling only. It closes itself on any outside pointerdown via a **capture-phase** document listener, so none of the callers ever need to close each other), `property-window.ts` (`PropertyWindow<A>` — the draggable, clippable property/action window `MapPicker` opens per selected map object; unlike `ContextMenu` it is instantiated fresh per window rather than reused, since several can be on screen at once, and it is appended as a child of the `window` overlay layer (its root argument, from `overlay-layer.ts` via `Hud.layers.window`) rather than `document.body` — every one of its own CSS selectors is scoped with a leading `#hud` so it outranks the `#hud, #hud * { margin: 0; padding: 0 }` reset by ID specificity. Display-only: the constructor takes a `PropertyWindowContent` (title/subtitle/rows/items) and builds the header DOM once; `syncHeader(title, subtitle)`, `syncRows(rows)` and `syncItems(items)` are the per-frame entry points, each diffing (by row `key` for rows, by a serialized `act`/`label`/`shortcut` string for items) so an unchanged value never touches the DOM and an unchanged shape never rebuilds it (same reasoning as `ObjectListPanel`/`StageStatusPanel` — a full rebuild would drop the item-click/header-drag/clip-button listeners). A `PropertyRow`'s `group` sorts it into a named, collapsed-by-default section drawn first in the row list (`map-picker.ts`'s `orbitRows` puts every ship/base/ammo/celestial-body orbital-element row under `'軌道'`, which is how map view shows an object's orbital elements — the ORBIT panel is combat-view-only, see `panel.ts` above), and its `collapsible` flag instead sorts a group-less row under a single "▼ 詳細を表示" toggle at the end of the row list — the expand/collapse state of both is this class's own (`groupExpanded`/`collapsibleExpanded`), not passed in, since it's pure display state no caller needs to know about; `map-picker.ts`'s row builders leave the combat-relevant fields (HP, distance, temperature, power, ammo) always visible — `syncItems` exists because the action set itself is state-dependent (e.g. a ship's "操作対象にする"/"削除" presence, or a follow-toggle's label) and `MapPicker.sync` calls it every frame alongside `syncRows`, not just once at open time. Either sync reclamps the window into the viewport (`reclamp()`, a private wrapper around `moveTo` using the window's own current position) whenever its shape changed, since content growing after the window opened at a viewport edge would otherwise push it off-screen. Header-drag (pointer capture, `CLICK_MOVE_THRESHOLD` latch shared with `Input.pushIfClick` — no second threshold) and the 📌 clip toggle are the only interactive surface besides the `onSelect`/`onClose`/`onOutsideClick`/`onClipChange` callbacks; the class itself has no opinion on what clipping or an outside click should *do* (close/keep-open policy lives in the caller, `MapPicker`) — it only tracks `clipped`, toggles the button's look, and reports the event through `onClipChange` so the caller can move its one-temp-window bookkeeping in step. A window's own item shortcuts (`[F]`/`[T]`/`[W]`/`[N]`/`[ESC]`, same hint style as `ContextMenu`) only fire while `!clipped` — a clipped window neither shows nor accepts them, since several clipped windows could otherwise fight over the same key; because at most one window is ever unclipped, there's no ambiguity about which one a keypress reaches. `moveTo(clientX, clientY)` is public so a caller can re-home an existing window (`MapPicker`'s "already open, move to click position" path) as well as re-clamp on its own `resize` handling; `bringToFront()` just re-`appendChild`s to its root, since siblings at the same fixed `z-index` stack by DOM order — no per-window z-index counter. When `content.onRename` is supplied, an extra ✎ button appears beside 📌/✕ and swaps `titleMainEl` for an `<input>` (`startRename`); Enter or blur commits (empty/unchanged input is a no-op) and calls `onRename(name)`, Escape cancels — either way the input is swapped back for `titleMainEl` without waiting for the next `syncHeader`. The input's own `keydown` stops propagation, so it never reaches this class's own `[F]`/`[T]`/… item-shortcut listener while typing), `layout.ts` (`clampOverlayPosition`/`Point2` — the pure viewport-clamping arithmetic shared by `PropertyWindow`'s drag/resize and `ContextMenu`'s open-position clamp, so the two popups can't drift to different clamping rules), `shortcut-hint.ts` (`shortcutKeyLabel` — the pure `Escape`→`ESC`/`Delete`→`DEL`/else-uppercase conversion behind both popups' `[KEY]` item hints), `view-badge.ts` (`ViewBadge` — the top-right badge showing the game title/version, `Mode: <selectLabel, title-cased>`, and a `View: <Combat|Map|Dock> ▾` dropdown built on the shared `ContextMenu`; owned and `sync`'d by `Game` rather than by `Hud` itself, since it needs a live reference to `ViewManager` that `Hud` is constructed too early to hold — see `game.ts` above for why `Hud` predates `Game`. It holds **no view state of its own**: the label reads `ViewManager.current` and the menu is exactly `ViewManager.selectableViews(combatAvailable)`, which already excludes the current view and anything unreachable, so a listed item can never be refused after being picked), `result-screen.ts` (win/lose/score-attack end-screen assembly), `tick-scale.ts` (`chooseTickInterval`/`buildTicks` — the PREDICT スライダー's tick marks: pick the smallest interval from a fixed 10min→1year ladder whose tick count fits the panel, then place ticks at that interval's multiples; pure, DOM-free), `calendar-ticks.ts` (`calendarBoundaries`/`tickLabel` — the planned trajectory's ruler-style tick marks (`plan-display.ts` above): a fixed rank ladder of calendar units (1時間/3時間/6時間/12時間/1日/1月/1年), `calendarBoundaries(fromUnix, toUnix, maxCount)` picking the finest rank whose boundary count over that span still fits `maxCount` and enumerating that rank's boundaries, each tagged with the *coarsest* rank it actually satisfies (an ordinary midnight is a day boundary, but a month-start midnight is also a month boundary, so it's tagged the coarser way) — month/year boundaries step through `Date.UTC`'s own month/year index rather than a fixed second count, since neither unit is a fixed duration. `tickLabel(unix, rank)` formats the matching string per rank (`HH:00`/`M/D`/`M月`/year). This is a separate module from `tick-scale.ts` rather than a second mode of it: that one places ticks at even fractions of an *elapsed* span with a relative `T+` label, which has no notion of a calendar-absolute month or year boundary — the two modules answer genuinely different questions, not the same question for two callers), `utils.ts` (shared formatters `fmtDist`/`fmtSpeed`/`fmtTime`/`fmtDuration`/`fmtMarkerDist`/`fmtDateTime`, plus `SIM_EPOCH_SEC` — `fmtDateTime` renders a Unix timestamp as ISO 8601 UTC (`2026-08-08T14:16:00`) by reading the date's components rather than `toISOString()`, and `SIM_EPOCH_SEC` parses `const.ts`'s `SIM_EPOCH_UTC` with its own regex rather than `Date.parse`; both exist because the sim epoch sits in a far-future year whose 5-digit form `Date.parse` rejects outright and `toISOString()` renders in the signed extended-year notation. The pair turns `SIM_EPOCH_UTC` + `simTime` into the global status bar's absolute-date readout and `plan-display.ts`'s UTC day-boundary ticks; it is deliberately independent of `Ephemeris`'s phase offsets, since those are random per-game initial phases and `SIM_EPOCH_UTC` is a fixed display anchor with no bearing on where the sun/moon actually are) — **flat dark theme: monotone grays on near-black panels (1px light edge, no shadows) with a saturated orange primary accent** (`#ff6a00`); markers and orbit lines follow the same rule — monotone base (player orbit light gray, planned orbit white, prograde/boresight light gray) with orange reserved for attention targets (target marker+orbit, lead, maneuver node/BURN, ammo pickups). The one deliberate second color is cyan (`ACCENT_SECONDARY` = `#00c8ff`, `theme.ts`) for the secondary target only — chosen specifically to be unmistakable against orange, since the two can appear on screen together (secondary orbit line, enemy-list row, marker glyph).
- `src/game/marker/` — the screen-projected marker **mechanism**, plus the few marker sets that no single object can own. `marker-manager.ts` (`MarkerManager` — element pooling keyed by id, `set`/`setPosition`/`setDirection`/`setBearing`/`headingRotationDeg`/`hide`/`remove` (`hide` keeps the pooled element for keys drawn from a fixed set; `remove` drops the DOM node for keys that grow per target — enemy/LEAD markers, which would otherwise accumulate forever), label-collision relaxation and the SVG leader lines; `setBearing` is the shared "off-screen target → arrow on a ring at the screen edge" placement, used both by `GroupedMarkers` (enemies, filled ▲) and `Logistics` (ammo, hollow △) — the caller supplies an *up-pointing* glyph because the ring angle is applied as a rotation; **owned by `Game`, not `Hud`**, and shared by reference with every module that emits markers). `headingRotationDeg(worldPos, vel, project, scale)` is the sole place the "rotate an up-pointing glyph to face `vel`" assembly lives — it projects `worldPos` and a point `MARKER_HEADING_PROBE_PX` pixels ahead along `vel`, turns the atan2 angle between them into the +90°-corrected rotation `set`'s `rotationDeg` expects, and returns `undefined` — meaning "don't rotate" — when the projected difference falls under `MARKER_HEADING_DEGENERATE_PX`, i.e. the velocity is nearly along the view axis and the direction is numerically unreliable. `set` treats `rotationDeg === undefined` as "leave the glyph's current rotation alone" rather than resetting it to 0°, so a marker passing through that degenerate moment holds its last heading instead of visibly snapping to unrotated and back; a marker that never rotates (the combat-view direction markers, the boresight) simply never receives a defined `rotationDeg` and stays unrotated forever. **The rule is that each object syncs its own markers** — a module must not expose both `sync` and a separate `syncMarker`; the marker update belongs inside its `sync`. So the orbital-direction/boresight/`self` markers live in `player/player-markers.ts` (owned by `Player`, driven from `syncPlayer`), the target bearing + board-crossing marks in `targeter.ts`, the relative AN/DN markers in `nav-target.ts`, the ▣ AMMO markers in `stages/stage-utils/logistics.ts`, the celestial labels in `camera/focus-markers.ts`, NODE/BURN in `plan/plan-guide.ts`, and the periapsis/apoapsis icons + the planned-position ghost in `plan/plan-display.ts`. Three sets stay in `marker/` because no single object can decide them:
  - `grouped-markers.ts` (`GroupedMarkers`) [Game-owned] — takes a set of `GroupedMarkerItem`s (key/class/symbol/world position/velocity/priority/label parts/bearing color) and does the things that need the whole set: collapsing screen-adjacent markers into one representative (highest `priority` wins; the rest lose their label), in combat view only, replacing off-screen ones with a screen-edge bearing arrow (`sync`'s `overviewMode` argument hides it on the map, same as `Logistics`/`CreativeStage`'s own bearing arrows — a map-mode object is already visible in the overview, so a bearing hint is redundant there), and, in map view only, rotating each marker to face its own `vel` via `MarkerManager.headingRotationDeg` (`sync`'s `scale` argument — a `ScaleFn` — is what that conversion needs; combat view leaves `rotationDeg` unset, since there the mesh itself already shows attitude). Target-agnostic and reusable; `Enemy.markerItem()`/`Player.markerItem()` are what supply the set's content.
  - `lead-markers.ts` (`LeadMarkers`) [Game-owned] — the ✛ LEAD markers, which depend on the player *and* the enemy and so belong to neither. Owns the per-enemy last-locked time (rebuilt each frame from the live enemy list, so it can't leak) that keeps a LEAD marker up for `LEAD_HOLD_SEC` after the target moves on. Hidden entirely in map mode / when the player is dead.
  - `equator-node-markers.ts` (`EquatorNodeMarkers`) [Game-owned] — the ▲▽ EqAN/EqDN icons showing where a source's orbit crosses its center body's equatorial plane, for a caller-supplied list of `EqNodeSource`s (`{id, name, state, samples?}`) rather than a single owner's own state, since **which objects get one is a choice spanning several owners at once** (`Game.equatorNodeSources` builds the list each frame from the active ship — the plan's final segment start if a plan exists, else the ship's live state — plus whichever entity `NavTarget`/`Targeter` currently point at, plus every living `entities.bases` entry unconditionally (a base is a permanent orbital fixture and approaching/docking to it is itself a plane-matching maneuver, so it belongs in the set regardless of selection), deduped by id — celestial bodies and Lagrange points are never sources, since they aren't themselves an orbiting "object"). `update(sources, frame, displayTime)` resolves each source's own strongest attractor and skips it when that body has no `degree2.pole` (sun/jupiter — no modeled equator) or the orbit plane is ~coincident with it. **`samples` decides how a source's crossings are found, and the split is deliberate rather than uniform**: the optional field is set only for the active ship (`Game.equatorNodeSources` passes `PlanPath.finalSegmentSamples` alongside its `finalSegmentStart`) and only that source's crossings are found by scanning the integrated series (`physics/trajectory-features.ts`'s `findEquatorCrossings`) — the same reasoning `plan-display.ts`'s apsis icons follow, since the ship's planned orbit is drawn as `PlanArc`'s integrated polyline and an analytically-derived icon would drift off it under J2's epoch-dependent osculating elements. Every other source (enemy/base/nav-target) has no `samples` and keeps the analytic `nodeAnomalies(el, eqNormal)` path, because those orbits are drawn as `OrbitLine`'s analytic ellipse — an icon derived any other way would be the one not on the line. Positions are baked/un-baked into the display `frame` self-contained (no `plan-path.ts` dependency, since a source need not be on the plan at all). `mapPickables()` (`kind: 'eqnode'`) feeds `MapPicker`'s candidate set. `sync(project, show, cameraPos)` places the markers, keyed `eqan-${sourceId}`/`eqdn-${sourceId}` — since the source set's membership changes frame to frame, a key that drops out is `remove`d (not `hide`d), matching `stage-utils/logistics.ts`'s `syncMarkers`; a point `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body (off the `Attractor[]` `update` cached at `displayTime`) is `hide`d instead, since the pair itself hasn't disappeared.

  `markerManager.resolveCollisions()` is the one call that must see every marker, so `Game.sync` calls it last.
- `src/game/navball/` — the 表示 panel, owned by `Game`. `navball.ts`'s `Navball` holds `gridVisibility`; `navball-panel.ts`'s `NavballPanel` is its DOM (a `Hud.root` panel with the six celestial-grid `HudToggle`s), split on the usual build/sync line.

  **`gridVisibility: CelestialGridVisibility` is the source of truth for the six celestial-grid toggles** — `Navball` holds it (flipped by its own panel's `HudToggle`s) and `Game.sync` reads it once, passing it as `EnvironmentScene.sync`'s own `gridVisibility` argument for `render/celestial-grid.ts`'s `CelestialGrid.sync` to consume; `CelestialGrid` itself holds no visibility state of its own (see `game/celestial/environment-scene.ts` below). Grouping the grid toggles inside the 表示 panel rather than a separate window is a spatial choice only. Its initial value is read from `localStorage` (`tepui.gridVisibility`, `loadGridVisibility`) and written back on every toggle (`saveGridVisibility`), so it survives a page reload.

`src/game/celestial/` — the meshes/lights/labels for each individual celestial body (THREE-dependent), as opposed to `physics/ephemeris.ts`'s "where is it" (THREE-free). Moved here from `render/` because it already depended on `game/camera/`, `game/floating-origin` and `game/const` — a `render/` regulation violation this move corrects.
- `celestial-body.ts` — the abstract `CelestialBody`: `id: AttractorId`, `build(scene)` (one-time mesh construction), `sync(fo, displayTime, cameraSystem, ephemeris)` (per-frame placement). Holds no position/velocity of its own — `Ephemeris` is the only source of truth, read fresh every `sync`.
- `src/game/celestial/point-field.ts` — **THREE-free** generation and evaluation of the solar system's diffuse point clouds (main belt, Jupiter's L4/L5 Trojans, the Hilda group, the Kuiper belt's cold/hot populations, the scattered disk) as plain orbital elements, so it's a `physics/`-style pure module covered by `test:physics` (`tests/physics/point-field.test.ts`) despite living under `game/celestial/` (display-only — these points are never a gravity source, pick target, or focus target, so they don't belong in `physics/attractor.ts`'s `AttractorId` registry). Each group is one `PointFieldDef` in the `POINT_FIELD_DEFS` array — a size distribution (`'semiMajor'` with a range plus optional Kirkwood-style Gaussian-rejected gaps, or `'perihelion'` for the scattered disk, whose q clusters at 30–40 AU rather than its a) crossed with an eccentricity/inclination range, plus an optional `ResonanceDistribution` for a group locked to a Jupiter mean-motion resonance. `generatePointField(seed = ASTEROID_SEED)` is deterministic (`mulberry32`, not `Math.random`, precisely so a save/replay reproduces the same field) and returns one `PointFieldGroup` per def; `allPoints` flattens them. A resonant group's `l0`/`lonPeri`/`meanMotion` are solved from the resonant angle σ = p·λ_J − q·λ_H − (p−q)·ϖ_H staying near one of `librationCentersDeg`: the 1:1 Trojans (p=q) have no ϖ_H term, so σ fixes `l0` directly and `meanMotion` is Jupiter's own `lRate`; the 3:2 Hilda group (p=3, q=2) instead draws `l0` freely and solves for `lonPeri`, with three libration centers (0°/120°/−120°) reproducing the real Hilda group's three-lobed clustering relative to Jupiter — a single center only reproduces one lobe, since nothing else about the model forces the other two to be occupied. `PointElements` carries a precomputed `meanMotion` alongside the usual elements specifically because `pointPositionAt(el, t)` runs across the whole field every frame update and a `sqrt` per point per call would be wasted work. `pointPositionAt` returns a **sun-centered** position (via `elements.ts`'s Kepler solve + `Q_ECLY_TO_ECI`) — ECI-izing by adding the sun's own position is the caller's job, same split `elements.ts` itself follows elsewhere.
- `src/game/celestial/point-field-view.ts` — `PointFieldView`: the `THREE.InstancedMesh` draw side, one `InstancedMesh` **per group** (a `GROUP_VIEW` table of draw radius/color keyed by `PointFieldDef.id`, since the Kuiper belt/scattered disk sit an order of magnitude farther out than the main belt/Trojans/Hildas and need a larger draw radius to stay visible at that zoom — this table is this module's own, since `point-field.ts` stays THREE-free), owned by `environment-scene.ts`'s `EnvironmentScene`. Geometry is `TetrahedronGeometry`, not a flat billboard/plane — a flat shape has a normal, and at some camera angle every instance would go edge-on and the whole field would flicker out at once; a tetrahedron always presents some visible face. `update(t, overviewMode, ephemeris)` is a no-op while `!overviewMode` (never drawn in combat view, so there's nothing to evaluate) and otherwise re-evaluates only **1/8 of the points per frame** (`UPDATE_FRACTION`, round-robin via a persistent `cursor` per group) — justified because the fastest possible orbital period in the field is several years, so a few frames of position lag is sub-pixel at any map zoom. Positions are cached sun-centered (`positions: Vec3[]`, mirroring `pointPositionAt`'s own convention) because the sun's own ECI position moves every frame regardless of whether a given point's orbital position was just refreshed. `sync(fo, overviewMode)` rewrites every instance's `instanceMatrix` unconditionally each frame (not just the ones `update` refreshed) — the floating origin itself moves every frame, so even an unrefreshed point's on-screen position must be re-baked.
- `earth-body.ts` — `EarthBody`: wraps `render/earth.ts`'s `Earth` (self-rotation angle, sun direction, aurora/atmosphere `tick`). `phase0` (the spin's random initial phase) is mutable and exposed via `spinPhase0()`/`setSpinPhase0()` so it can round-trip through a save the same way `Ephemeris`'s own phase offsets do — see `GameSaveData.earthSpinPhase0` below.
- `sun-body.ts` — `SunBody`: the sun's two views (`render/stars.ts`'s `Sun`, holding both). Combat view keeps the camera-relative glow billboard (a flat sprite reads fine at that scale); the overview camera instead places the real `Sun.mesh` — a self-lit `MeshBasicMaterial` sphere, since the sun is a light source and must not shade under the scene's own lighting — at its true ECI position and `R_SUN` radius, the same "real position/radius on the map" rule `SphereBody` follows for the moon/Jupiter; whichever mode isn't active hides its own visual. It owns no light: the scene's one `DirectionalLight` illuminates only what is drawn near the render origin, so it belongs to `EnvironmentScene` alongside the ambient light rather than to the star's own view.
- `sphere-body.ts` — `SphereBody`: the shared "textured sphere at a compressed display distance" view for any orbiting body that needs nothing more (the moon and Jupiter). The one place the `visDist * radius / trueDist` scaling formula lives, so every body drawn this way keeps its true angular size — a shaped body scales all 3 axes by the same factor, so the property holds unchanged. The constructor resolves `solar-system.ts`'s `shapeAxes(radius, shape)` once into a local `THREE.Vector3 axes` (`shape` an optional `ShapeDef` argument, `undefined` = a sphere of all 3 axes = `radius`); `sync` writes `mesh.scale` from `axes` (map view) or `axes` scaled by `visDist/dist` (combat view) instead of `setScalar`. Because THREE composes a mesh's matrix from position/quaternion/scale independent of assignment order, `axes` is always interpreted in the body's own (pre-spin) local frame regardless of which of `mesh.quaternion`/`mesh.scale` is written first — a shape is a fact about the body, not about ECI, so it must not rotate with the spin write. Orientation is not `Object3D.lookAt` — `sync` reads `ephemeris.poleAt(this.id, displayTime)` and, when non-`null`, sets the mesh's quaternion straight from `body-orientation.ts`'s `spinOrientation(axis, spinAngle)`; a body with no `pole` model simply keeps whatever orientation it already had. The Moon's tidal lock therefore falls out of its synchronous spin phase rather than a per-frame "face the parent" override (verified over a full sidereal-month sweep: the modeled meridian tracks the true Earth direction to within ~10.1°, the size of lunar libration). Optional constructor arguments `rings?: RingSystemDef`/`ringTextures?` build a `game/celestial/ring-view.ts` `RingView` in `build()`, added to the scene **as a sibling of the body mesh, not a child** — see `ring-view.ts` below for why. `sync` scales the mesh by `axes × (scaleFactor/radius)` (3 axes, so a shaped body's flattening survives the map/combat distance switch) but hands the ring only the uniform `scaleFactor` itself (a ring is a circle in the equatorial plane, so it takes no flattening of its own even when the body it orbits does), and passes the resolved pole axis (`null` for a body with no `pole` model, in which case the ring is left unrotated by this class — `RingView.sync` itself no-ops on a `null` axis).
- `body-class.ts` — `BodyClass` (`'star' | 'planet' | 'dwarf' | 'satellite' | 'smallBody'`) and `BODY_CLASSES: Record<SolarSystemId, BodyClass>`. **A display-importance judgment, deliberately separate from `solar-system.ts`'s `kind`** — `kind` says what a body orbits (a dynamical fact), this says whether the map shows it by default, which is what splits dwarf planets and comet nuclei out of the `kind: 'planet'` crowd. Kept THREE-free (unlike `celestial-registry.ts`) so the visibility rules below can be evaluated — and tested — with no renderer. `bodyClassOf(registry, id)` falls back to the dynamical `kind` for an id a custom registry invented.
- `body-visibility.ts` — `BodyClassToggles`/`DEFAULT_BODY_CLASS_TOGGLES`/`visibleBodyIds(registry, focusId, toggles)`/`alwaysFullyVisibleIds(registry, focusId)`/`bodyIconLabel(registry, toggles, id)`: **the single answer to "which bodies is the map currently about", shared by the map labels, the orbit-object list, and the placement panel's reference-body control.** `focusId: AttractorId | undefined` — `undefined` is the camera fixed on a `FocusTarget` with no corresponding body (a `'point'` target baked into a coordinate system rather than following a specific object), in which case `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` falls back to the star alone.
