Three separate filters would let "it's on the map but not in the list" happen, which is exactly what makes a 100-body registry unnavigable. `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` is the toggle-independent part: every star, plus (once `focusId` is defined) the focused body's ancestor, child, or **planetary-system sibling** (sibling only below the star, since every heliocentric body is a sibling of every other and the star already covers that layer) — so focusing Jupiter surfaces the Galilean moons and leaving it puts them away again. `visibleBodyIds` adds every body whose class has its **icon or label** toggle on — a body with both off is not just undrawn but out of the candidate set entirely (unpickable, absent from the object list), which is why the admission test is icon-or-label rather than a single label flag. **Icon (the marker dot) and Label (the name) are independent per class** (`planetIcon`/`planetLabel`/`dwarfIcon`/`dwarfLabel`/`satelliteIcon`/`satelliteLabel`/`smallBodyIcon`/`smallBodyLabel`/`lagrangeIcon`/`lagrangeLabel`) — wanting a body's position without its name (or the reverse) is a real request, and `bodyIconLabel(registry, toggles, id)` is the per-id answer a caller already inside `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` must not call (that case is always icon:true/label:true, decided by the caller before falling back to this function). `planetOrbit`/`dwarfOrbit`/`smallBodyOrbit` are a third, orbit-line axis independent of both — `visibleBodyIds`/`bodyIconLabel` never read them; `environment-scene.ts`'s `showsReferenceLine` does. `satellite`/`lagrange` have no orbit flag at all (a satellite's reference line is decided by focus proximity instead, and a Lagrange point has no orbit to draw). Defaults: everything off except `planetOrbit`/`planetIcon`/`planetLabel` (the planets are the solar system's skeleton, so hiding them by default would make the map unreadable), `smallBodyIcon`/`smallBodyLabel` (small bodies are numerous enough to flood the map, but the ones registered today are hand-picked and worth seeing by default) and `lagrangeIcon`/`lagrangeLabel` (`FocusMarkers` already filters lagrange points down to the dynamically-meaningful ones via `Ephemeris.hasUsableCollinearPoints`/`hasStableTriangularPoints`, so the "too many, would flood" concern doesn't apply). **Proximity is deliberately not an axis**: camera distance changes continuously, so a distance threshold would make labels and list rows flicker mid-zoom; the focus parent/child relation is the discrete stand-in that only changes when the player asks it to. `systemChainAt(registry, cameraPos, attractors)` is the one exception, and deliberately so: it walks from whichever body most strongly pulls at `cameraPos` (`attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor`) up through its ancestors — a continuous quantity, camera position, driving a discrete result — because it answers "what system is the camera physically in" rather than gating what's shown; a body not in the active registry (a living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`) returns a single-element chain rather than an ancestor walk, since it has none. `systemMembersAt(registry, cameraPos, attractors)` builds on it for the UI's actual need: the chain plus each chain body's own children (skipping a star's children, or a Jupiter-chain list would pull in every other planet) — a fixed near-to-far, body-then-its-children order so the caller's choices don't reorder frame to frame. `frame-controls.ts`'s `AnchorZone`/`RotationZone` use it to build their quick-pick/rotation-target lists for "the system I'm currently near."
- `celestial-registry.ts` — `CELESTIAL_BODIES: Record<SolarSystemId, { name: string; create(): CelestialBody }>` — keyed by `solar-system.ts`'s `SolarSystemId` (not the general `AttractorId`), so this table stays exhaustive over `SOLAR_SYSTEM`'s full body set (101 as of this writing — 1 star, 49 `kind: 'planet'` bodies, 51 `kind: 'satellite'` bodies) exactly as before the id type opened up: registering another body in `SOLAR_SYSTEM` still makes this table fail to compile until it too gets an entry. **The single definition of each real solar-system body's display name** — `hud/frame-labels.ts`'s `celestialBodyName`, `focus-markers.ts`, and `creative/ship-placer-panel.ts` all read it rather than spelling the name out again. Japanese display strings never appear under `physics/`. `fallbackCelestialView(registry, id)` covers the complementary case — a body a caller's active registry actually has but this table doesn't (a custom-registry stage's invented body) — returning a `SunBody`-equivalent for `registry[id].isStar`(-classified bodies) and a plain solid sphere otherwise, sized from the registry's own `radius`; it's a runtime fallback, not a second `Record`, so it carries no exhaustiveness guarantee and none is needed — an id a caller reaches this way is by definition outside `SOLAR_SYSTEM`. Every planet other than Earth is a real-photography textured sphere: `CelestialSurface.textured(textureUrl)` (`render/celestial-surface.ts`) loads one of the `src/assets/2k_*.jpg` textures (Solar System Scope, CC BY 4.0 — credited in `README.md`; Venus's is its cloud-deck atmosphere texture, since the surface itself is never visible), built via the shared `planetEntry(id, name, textureUrl, pointBrightness?)` registry helper — it reads `rings: RingSystemDef | undefined` straight off `bodyDef(SOLAR_SYSTEM, id)` (only present when `def.kind === 'planet'`) and forwards it, plus the module-level `RING_TEXTURES: Record<RingTextureId, string>`, to whichever of `SphereBody`/`PointBody` it constructs — `RING_TEXTURES` is the single place a `RingBandDef.texture` identifier resolves to a real asset URL (today just `saturn` → `saturnRingTextureUrl`), since `solar-system.ts` may not hold a URL itself (THREE/アセット非依存の規則). **`pointBrightness` (`undefined` by default) is what switches a planet's `create()` from `SphereBody` to `point-body.ts`'s `PointBody`** — venus/jupiter=`'bright'`, mercury/mars/saturn=`'medium'`, uranus=`'faint'`; neptune and every dwarf-planet/large-asteroid/comet-nucleus body stay `SphereBody`, since past +6 apparent magnitude a combat-view point sprite would be dishonest about what's actually visible to the eye. Every moon (this file's satellites) stays a flat-shaded, single-colored sphere (`CelestialSurface.solid(color)`, no texture, via `satelliteEntry(id, name, color)`, displayed at the same `MOON_VIS_DIST` compressed distance the Moon uses) — their look is intentionally unfinished, since registering them exists to prove a body can be added, not to art-direct each one. The 41 non-planet heliocentric bodies — the original 9 (ケレス/ベスタ/パラス/冥王星/ハウメア/マケマケ/エリス/ハレー彗星/エンケ彗星) plus 32 further dwarf planets/large asteroids/comet nuclei/NEOs registered later — are likewise flat-shaded solid spheres, via the sibling helper `solidPlanetEntry(id, name, color)` — same look, but displayed at `PLANET_VIS_DIST` (the textured-planet distance) rather than `MOON_VIS_DIST`, since they're `PlanetId`s (heliocentric) rather than satellites. Ring data itself is registered per-body in `solar-system.ts`'s `SOLAR_SYSTEM` (jupiter/saturn/uranus/neptune/quaoar/chariklo — see that file's entry below), not here; this file only resolves a band's `RingTextureId` to an actual asset (today only Saturn's carries one — Quaoar's and Chariklo's rings are untextured `RingOpticsDef`-only bands). 衛星は `texturedSatelliteEntry`(実写の全球モザイクが手に入った6体 — フォボス/イオ/エウロパ/ガニメデ/カリスト/タイタン、いずれも USGS Astrogeology のパブリックドメイン画像)と、それ以外の全衛星(トリトン/ダイモスに加え、木星以遠の不規則衛星や準惑星・小天体の衛星まで)が使う `satelliteEntry`(単色)に分かれる。**実写が無い天体に想像図を貼らない**ので、この分岐は「作り込みの手間」ではなく「実データの有無」で決まる。
- `point-body.ts` — `PointBody`: the combat-view stand-in for a planet too small on screen for `SphereBody`'s visual-distance compression to read as anything but a sub-pixel speck. It is a **wholesale model swap by view, not a mode branch inside `SphereBody`**: map view (`cameraSystem.overviewMode`) draws the exact same real-position/real-radius(-or-shape)/self-rotating mesh `SphereBody` would (spin via `Ephemeris.poleAt`; the constructor resolves `shapeAxes(radius, shape)` the same way `SphereBody` does, so a shaped body's dwarf-planet-scale point still reads flattened once zoomed into map view; an optional `RingView`, built and scaled the same sibling-not-child way `SphereBody` builds one, is shown here and hidden in combat view — a point sprite has no room to also show a ring); combat view instead hides that mesh (and the ring group, if any) and places a `render/billboard.ts` `Billboard` at `camera.position + direction-to-body × STAR_SHELL_RADIUS` — the same camera-following shell the star field sits on, so the point never drifts relative to the stars as the camera moves. `PointBrightness` (`'bright' | 'medium' | 'faint'`) is a fixed 3-step table of billboard scale/opacity fed in at construction by `celestial-registry.ts`'s `pointBrightness` argument — this module holds no magnitude physics of its own, just the three preset levels.
- `environment-scene.ts` — `EnvironmentScene` (moved from `render/`, see above): owns the star shell, `CelestialGrid`, ambient light, the `CelestialBody[]` built from `CELESTIAL_BODIES` (one per registry entry, in declaration order), and `point-field-view.ts`'s `PointFieldView` (the main-belt/Trojan/Hilda/Kuiper-belt/scattered-disk point clouds — a map-only display built from the ephemeris, so it belongs here alongside the other celestial views rather than under `physics/`). The map-only reference `OrbitLine`s are **data-driven, not one field per body**: a `ReadonlyMap<OrbitingId, OrbitLine>` is built once, one entry per orbiting body registered in `SOLAR_SYSTEM` (a satellite's line colored the old moon-line gray `0xaab3c0`, a planet's white), and the shared `orbitElementsFor(id, simTime)` resolves each one's center — a satellite's own planet, a planet the sun (a fresh sun `Attractor` from `ephemeris.stateOf('sun', t)` each call, since the sun itself moves in ECI) — so registering a body needs no new field or branch here. The one exception is the geostationary reference line (`geoLine`), which isn't a celestial body at all and stays its own field. `syncReferenceLines(simTime, fo, overviewMode, focusId, toggles)` decides *which* reference lines actually draw, via the private `showsReferenceLine(id, focusId, toggles)`, which routes through `body-class.ts`'s `bodyClassOf` so a body's reference line follows the same class as its `FocusMarkers` label: a star's line always shows (while `overviewMode`); a planet's, dwarf-planet's or small-body's line follows that class's **orbit** flag in `body-visibility.ts`'s `BodyClassToggles` (`toggles.planetOrbit`/`toggles.dwarfOrbit`/`toggles.smallBodyOrbit`, from `cameraSystem.bodyClassToggles`, forwarded in by `sync`) — independent of the icon/label flags the same body's map marker reads, so a body can show its icon and/or name without its ellipse or the reverse; a satellite's line shows only while `overviewCamera.focus` sits within that satellite's own planetary system (`focusSystemOf(focusId)`, which strips a `-l1..5` Lagrange-point suffix and resolves a planet to itself / a satellite to its parent) — drawing all eight non-Moon satellite lines at once would bury the inner solar system in overlapping ellipses at any zoom that shows more than one planet. Earth's own system is the deliberate exception and always shows the Moon's line regardless of focus, since the Moon is contextually relevant everywhere in a game centered on Earth orbit. `syncReferenceLines` also passes `excludeNearBodyFor(id, el, rel)` — the body's own current eccentric anomaly (`trueAnomalyAt(el, rel)` converted from true to eccentric anomaly, `rel` being the body's ECI position relative to `el.center`) and radius (`bodyDef(...).radius`) — since every reference line is centered on the body's own orbit and therefore always passes through wherever that body currently is, which would otherwise z-fight against the body's own opaque mesh. `densifyNear` is picked to serve whichever of the two concerns is the sharper constraint for that orbit: for `e > 0.5` (the dwarf-planet/comet-nucleus bodies especially — Halley's is 0.968) it goes to the periapsis (`positionOnOrbit(el, 0)`), where the curvature is sharp enough to read as a coarse polygon otherwise; for every other body it goes to `rel`, the body's own position, because the fade needs vertices inside the 1–2 radius band to have any gradient steps at all — under uniform sampling not one vertex lands there, and the line degenerates to the straddle rule's hard cut. **`update(t, overviewMode)`** re-derives the point field's positions at the display time (a no-op while `!overviewMode`, since the field is never drawn in combat view) — called from `Game.updateMapPresentation`, ahead of everything else that path updates, so the field's coordinates are as fresh as the candidate set/camera built from the same frame (see `game.ts` below). `sync(playerPos, floatingOrigin, displayTime, cameraSystem, gridVisibility)` computes the sunlit fraction once, feeds it to the `SunBody` instance it picks out of the array, forwards `cameraSystem.overviewCamera.focus` into `syncReferenceLines`, syncs `pointFieldView` (transform-only — it holds no per-frame physics of its own), then just loops `for (const body of this.bodies) body.sync(...)` — adding a body to the registry needs no change here.
- `src/audio/sfx.ts` / `bgm-tracks.ts` — WebAudio synth SFX (CIWS-motif: motor-whine spin-up, heavy discrete gun reports) + Steve-Reich-style ambient minimal BGM (modal quartal pitch set, two pulse patterns of coprime lengths (16 vs 12 steps) phasing against each other, sine/triangle only; starts on first user gesture, stops on win/lose).
- `src/physics/kinematic-state.ts` — the `KinematicState` type (`{t, r, v}`, built only via `kinematicState(t, r, v)`) plus the geometry derivable from a state vector alone: the orbital basis `orbitAxes(state)` → `{pro, nrm, radOut}` plus `fromOrbitAxes(state, x)`, the one place that basis is built (the Δv edit keys, the Δv arm screen directions, the PROGRADE/NORMAL/RADIAL markers and every `norm(cross(r, v))` spelling all route through it — note `radOut` is the in-plane direction perpendicular to the velocity, which equals r̂ only when r⊥v; the name follows the HUD's RADIAL OUT/IN labels, not the true radial), and `hermiteInterpolate` (cubic Hermite between two `KinematicState`s, using their velocities as tangents; refuses a `t` outside the two epochs unless `allowExtrapolation` is passed, since the cubic diverges fast outside the span). Surface contact with *any* attractor is `attractor.ts`'s `hitCelestialBody`, which is what every `checkLoss` uses; altitude above Earth specifically (for the atmosphere-only callers — `atmosphere.ts`'s density table, `thermal.ts`'s drag/heating, the descent-warning EMA) is `solar-system.ts`'s `earthAltitudeOf`. Pure functions, no Earth constants — those live in `solar-system.ts` alongside the registry that names Earth.
- `src/physics/elements.ts` — the classical orbital `OrbitalElements` type and the state↔elements conversions. **`OrbitalElements.center: Attractor` replaces a bare `mu`**: an orbit's shape alone doesn't say what it's an orbit *around*, and comparing/displaying elements needs that answer (which body's radius the apsis altitudes are measured from, which body's name a HUD readout shows) — so the element set carries its own center rather than leaving the caller to remember it separately. `orbitalElementsFromState(rel, center)` takes a **center-relative** state (not absolute ECI — the argument name and its own comment say so) and is called from exactly one place; the sole entry point every other module uses is `attractor.ts`'s `orbitalElementsOf(s, center)`, which converts the absolute ECI state itself before calling in. `keplerPeriod(a, mu)`/`semiMajorFromPeriod(T, mu)` keep a plain `mu` argument (they only ever need the number, not a full `Attractor`, and are used before any `Attractor` exists yet — e.g. `CreativeStage`'s size-from-period input mode); `keplerPeriod` is the sole place a semi-major axis becomes an orbital period, and `OrbitalElements.period` routes through it. `OrbitalElements` carries no altitude of its own — periapsis/apoapsis depend on a body radius — so `apsisAltitudes(el)` reads it off `el.center.radius`. Kepler helpers for maneuver planning (`trueAnomalyAt`, `tofBetween`, `positionOnOrbit`/`velocityOnOrbit`, `stateFromOrbitalElements` — all round-trip-verified to machine precision; `stateFromOrbitalElements` returns a center-relative state too, absolute ECI conversion is the caller's job). `nodeAnomalies(el, planeNormal)` is the single implementation of "where does this orbit cross a given plane" — the true anomalies of the ascending/descending nodes (`planeNormal × el.hHat` for the line of nodes, so it points at the ascending one), `null` when the two planes nearly coincide; `plan/plan-display.ts`'s equatorial-node icons and `nav-target.ts`'s relative AN/DN both call it rather than each rederiving the line-of-nodes cross product. Pure functions, no Earth constants. `eccentricAnomalyFromMean(m, e)`'s Newton-method initial guess switches at `e > 0.8`: below that it starts from `E = M`, but at high eccentricity `M`'s own near-0 region makes that starting point converge slowly or oscillate, so it instead starts from `±π` on the same side as `M`.
- `src/physics/attractor.ts` — the shape every gravity source shares. `AttractorId`/`OrbitingId` are plain `string` (not a closed union) because the set of registered bodies is a runtime choice (`solar-system.ts`'s `CelestialRegistry`, injected per `Ephemeris` instance) — the exhaustiveness that keeps a body's display name from being forgotten lives one level down, in each concrete registry's own `keyof` (`solar-system.ts`'s `SolarSystemId`), not here. `Attractor` (`{id, mu, radius, state: KinematicState, degree2: Degree2Gravity | null, isStar}`) holds position/velocity as one timed `KinematicState` rather than separate `r`/`v` fields — so a position and a velocity from different instants can never be paired by mistake; Earth pinned at the origin under the default registry. `Degree2Gravity` (`{j2, refRadius, pole, tesseral}`) is the body's own degree-2 gravity field, resolved to concrete ECI vectors at the lookup time; `null` means "treat as a point mass". `tesseral` (`{c22, longAxis}`) is likewise `null` for an axisymmetric field. **`refRadius` is the radius the coefficients were defined against and is deliberately separate from `radius`** (the Moon's is 1738.0 km against a 1737.4 km surface radius), so updating the surface radius can never silently rescale the gravity field. `isStar` is what `dynamics.ts` sums solar radiation pressure over (every star, not "the" one), so a registry with zero or several stars degrades safely instead of hard-coding `id === 'sun'`. The pure functions built on `Attractor`: `attractorAccel(r, attractor)` (one body's own contribution to the ECI equation of motion — `μ_b[(r_b−r)/|r_b−r|³ − r_b/|r_b|³]`, the second term correcting for ECI itself free-falling toward that body, and each term skipped at zero distance so a body coincident with the query point — or the origin body itself — can't blow up); `strongestAttractor(r, attractors)` (the body with the largest `attractorAccel` magnitude — deliberately not the largest raw μ/d², which hands the sun a false win ~2.6×10⁵ km from Earth by ignoring that the ECI origin is comoving with it); `localOrbitPeriod(r, attractors)` (the circular period around whichever body that is); `frameOfAttractor(center)` (the identity-rotation `FrameTransform` centered on an already-looked-up `Attractor`, so `orbitalElementsOf` doesn't need to re-query the ephemeris for a snapshot it already has); `orbitalElementsOf(s, center)` (osculating `OrbitalElements` around a caller-chosen body, via `frame.ts`'s `toFrameState`); and `hitAttractor(r, attractors, margin)`/`hitCelestialBody(r, attractors, margin)` (the body a position has sunk into, or just whether one exists — every integration-truncation check and every entity's `checkLoss` routes through this, not just Earth's). This module holds no position-dependent narrowing of its own — **the only thing that ever reduces which bodies enter a gravity sum is `game/simulation/attractors.ts`'s spatial-grid classification** (`classifyAttractors`/`attractorsNear`, over `physics/spatial-grid.ts`'s `SpatialGrid`), which is what actually lowers the summation's order; a position-dependent filter evaluating `attractorAccel` itself (as a straight distance/magnitude cutoff once did) costs the same O(N·M) the sum it's supposed to cheapen already costs, so it was removed rather than kept as a second, redundant narrowing. `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)` is the sole place all bodies' current `r`/`v` are assembled into an `attractors` array, in registry declaration order — used both for gravity (after `game/simulation/attractors.ts`'s `gravityBodiesAt` narrows it to `mu !== 0` and merges in any living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`) and for every other "which bodies are there" question (occlusion, surface contact, center-body resolution, integration/sampling-interval step sizing), since there is exactly one window, not two.

  This module intentionally joins only two things — summing gravity, and handing out a body to center an ellipse on — and nothing more. Five once-conflated concerns stay structurally separate, with no umbrella concept binding them: **which gravity sources the integrator sums** (always all three, decided nowhere — `attractorAccel`/`dynamics.ts`); **which body an analytic ellipse (`OrbitalElements`) is centered on** (chosen per call site, typically `strongestAttractor` → `orbitalElementsOf`, and re-derived every frame as a point-in-time display rather than cached as if it belonged to the entity); **which frame a *sampled* trajectory is baked into for display** (the existing `Frame`/`PlanDisplay.planFrame` machinery, untouched by this module — a sampled polyline must stay in one frame for its whole length, so it is never derived from the per-frame ellipse-center choice); **where the overview camera looks and which frame it's fixed to** (`OverviewCamera.focus`/`cameraFrame`, likewise untouched); and **the time scale sizing integration/sampling steps** (`localOrbitPeriod`, independent of the ellipse-center choice). No function here derives one of these from another, and there is no GUI to pick an ellipse's center — it is always resolved via `strongestAttractor`.
- `src/physics/ecliptic.ts` — the ecliptic basis and its relation to ECI (Y = north): `Q_ECL_TO_ECI` (Z-up ecliptic → ECI, used by rotation-basis code), `Q_ECLY_TO_ECI` (Y-up ecliptic → ECI, used wherever a Kepler element set built in ecliptic coordinates is turned into an ECI state via `stateFromOrbitalElements`, which assumes Y = pole), `ECL_POLE`/`ECL_POLE_ECI`/`ECL_VERNAL`, `eclToEci`/`eciToEcl`, plus `ECI_POLE` (the ECI polar axis, Y — the one constant every "is this body's spin axis the one that defines ECI" check reads) and the exported `stdToEci(xs, ys, zs)`/`raDecToEci(raDeg, decDeg)` (standard equatorial X=vernal-equinox/Z=north-pole coordinates, and IAU right-ascension/declination pairs, both converted straight to ECI — `body-orientation.ts`'s `spinPhaseRef` reads `ECI_POLE` and `ephemeris.ts`'s `'iau'` pole resolution reads `raDecToEci`, so a body's ECI-frame pole direction is never derived a second way). The obliquity (23.439291°) is a fixed J2000-equivalent constant — equinox precession (25,772-year cycle, ~5.6° of equatorial drift per 1000 years) is out of scope, since it's a question of what ECI itself means (J2 axis, atmosphere co-rotation, Earth mesh spin axis, celestial grid all assume it fixed), not an orbital detail.
- `src/physics/kepler-orbit.ts` — the orbit math shared by every classification: `KeplerOrbit` (Kepler elements plus each angle's secular rate, fixed to a caller-chosen reference plane — see `basisToEci` below) and its evaluators `keplerOrbitState`/`keplerOrbitRotation`/`keplerOrbitNormal`. Angles are composed mean longitude L (one full turn per orbital period) → mean anomaly M = L − ϖ → true anomaly ν, then u = ν + (ϖ − Ω) for the in-plane angle — computing u directly from L would let precession speed up or slow down the apparent revolution. **`KeplerOrbit.basisToEci: Quat` is the reference plane the elements (inc/raan/argp) are measured against** (z = the plane's normal, x = the elements' own origin direction) → ECI; every evaluator — position/velocity, `normalFromAngles`, `directionFromAngles`, `rotationFromAngles` — routes its basis-relative vector through the single module-private `toEci(orbit, x, y, z)`, so the reference plane can never be applied inconsistently between an orbit's position and its rotation basis. This module holds no fixed plane of its own: `ECLIPTIC_BASIS` (= `ecliptic.ts`'s `Q_ECL_TO_ECI`) is exported as the default a caller passes in, not read internally — `planet-orbit.ts`'s `planetOrbit` always uses it (a planet's orbit is always measured from the ecliptic), while `satellite-orbit.ts`'s `satelliteOrbit` takes an optional override for a satellite whose elements are given against its planet's equator instead (see below). `keplerOrbitState` reuses `elements.ts`'s `stateFromOrbitalElements` (via `basisToEci` composed with the module's own `Q_ZUP_TO_YUP`, since that function assumes Y = pole) rather than a second position/velocity implementation; velocity adds the rotating frame's own angular velocity (ω×r, precession's contribution) on top of the radial rate. `keplerOrbitMeanDirection` returns the direction the **mean** longitude points (in-plane angle `u = L − Ω`, mean anomaly rather than true), which is what a synchronously rotating body's prime meridian tracks — a uniformly spinning body cannot follow the non-uniform true anomaly, and the two differ by the equation of center (up to 6.3° at the Moon's eccentricity). Knows nothing about stars/planets/satellites — that classification is `planet-orbit.ts`/`satellite-orbit.ts`'s.
- `src/physics/planet-orbit.ts` — a planet's orbit is its **planet-satellite system barycenter**'s heliocentric Kepler orbit, not the planet body's own (Earth is not light enough relative to the Moon, 1:81, to skip the ~4,673 km barycenter offset) — `PlanetOrbit = KeplerOrbit` plus secular element rates in deg/Julian-century (`planetOrbit`, which takes `lRateDegPerCentury` — Standish's mean-longitude rate L̇ passed straight through — rather than an orbital period, so no caller has to derive one from the other). `planetAngles` derives the sun-direction angles a satellite model needs (mean anomaly/longitude and their rates) straight from this barycenter orbit, so satellite evaluation never depends on the satellite's own state to find the sun. `AU` (the astronomical unit in meters) is exported from here too.
- `src/physics/satellite-orbit.ts` — a satellite's orbit is a two-body Kepler ellipse around its planet **plus, optionally, solar perturbation** (secular node regression/perigee advance via `raanRate`/`lonPeriRate` — the sign is this module's own, never spelled out at a call site — and periodic longitude/latitude/distance correction terms, `PerturbationTerm[]`, summed as sine/cosine series in the four fundamental arguments D/M/M′/F). `satelliteOrbit`'s `basisToEci?: Quat` picks the reference plane the two-body elements are measured against (`kepler-orbit.ts`'s `KeplerOrbit.basisToEci`), defaulting to the ecliptic (`ECLIPTIC_BASIS`) when omitted — the Moon's own call site leaves it at that default; `solar-system.ts`'s `equatorialSatelliteOrbit` overrides it to the parent planet's equatorial plane instead (see below). `satelliteState`'s periodic-term decomposition works in ecliptic (λ, β, r) coordinates and diverges (`rho0 → 0`) for an orbit whose plane passes through the ecliptic pole, which every equator-referenced satellite's does not rule out; since a satellite with **no periodic terms at all has nothing for that decomposition to add**, `satelliteState` checks `lonTerms`/`latTerms`/`distTerms` are all empty and returns the two-body `keplerOrbitState` result directly in that case, skipping the decomposition rather than routing zero-amplitude corrections through a coordinate system that can blow up. The Moon's own perturbation table (only body with one) precesses at the *measured* periods (18.612958y node / 8.85y perigee), not first-order theory's (which under-predicts perigee precession by ~2×, the historical problem Clairaut resolved with second-order terms), and its two-body part alone must never duplicate the periodic table's central-difference-equivalent terms (arguments that are `m`-only or `f`-only) — that's double counting. `orbitFrameRotationAt`/`orbitNormalAt` are built from the mean (two-body) elements only, so the Moon's true position can drift up to ~2.5° off that basis's x̂ (the sum of the periodic-term amplitudes); a perturbation-free satellite has no such drift, since its two-body elements are its entire model.
- `src/physics/solar-system.ts` — `CelestialBodyDef` (the star/planet/satellite discriminated union — this `kind` switch is the one exhaustive switch over a registered id's classification that's allowed, since it's a physical claim, not a registry lookup) and `CelestialRegistry` (`Readonly<Record<AttractorId, CelestialBodyDef>>`), the shape any `Ephemeris` reads. `SOLAR_SYSTEM` is one instance of that shape — the real solar system, and every `Ephemeris`'s default — declared with `satisfies CelestialRegistry` rather than a type annotation so `SolarSystemId = keyof typeof SOLAR_SYSTEM` derives its literal union (101 members as of this writing — 1 star, 49 `kind: 'planet'`, 51 `kind: 'satellite'`) straight from the data: registering a body means adding it to `SOLAR_SYSTEM` alone, and `game/celestial/celestial-registry.ts`'s `Record<SolarSystemId, …>` goes red until that id gets a display view too. `bodyDef(registry, id)`/`primaryOf(registry, id)`/`starOf(registry)` all take the registry as an explicit argument rather than reading `SOLAR_SYSTEM` directly. `starOf` is the sole place a registry is scanned for `kind: 'star'`, returning `null` for zero stars and throwing for more than one — this codebase supports a single primary star per registry only; genuinely comparable-mass bodies orbiting each other are `Asteroid`'s numerical n-body, not a registry concept (see `game/game-entity/asteroid.ts` below). `primaryOf` maps a body to its displayed parent (a satellite's planet, a planet's `starOf` result, and **`null` for a star itself** as well as for a registry with none — a star is never its own parent, so `null` is a usable "this is the root" signal for anything walking the hierarchy) and is the single implementation of that lookup, replacing five call sites that used to each spell out `def.kind === 'planet' ? 'sun' : def.planet`. `GRAVITATIONAL_CONSTANT` [m³/(kg·s²)] is the one physical constant this file adds beyond registry data — `Asteroid` is its sole reader, deriving `mu = G·mass` from a single mass rather than the registry's convention of a directly-measured `mu`. `MU_SUN`/`R_SUN`/`MU_MOON`/`R_MOON`/`MU_EARTH`/`R_EARTH`/`R_EARTH_EQ`/`SIDEREAL_DAY` are `SOLAR_SYSTEM`'s own numeric facts, read by callers elsewhere in `physics/` (`ephemeris.ts`'s Earth `Attractor`, `dynamics.ts`'s `degree2Accel`, `atmosphere.ts`'s co-rotating-atmosphere rate) and in `game/`. `earthAltitudeOf(r)` (position vector → altitude above Earth) sits here rather than in `kinematic-state.ts` since it's the only function that reads `R_EARTH`. The degree-2 gravity coefficients live here too — `J2_EARTH`, and `J2_MOON`/`C22_MOON`/`R_MOON_GRAVITY`/`MOON_OBLIQUITY` — all **unnormalized**. Each body's `degree2?: Degree2GravityDef` states only its own coefficients (j2/c22/refRadius) — omitting it makes a body a point mass. A body's spin axis/phase is a separate, optional field one level up: `CelestialBodyDef.pole?: PoleModel` (`'eciPole'` for the body that defines ECI; `'cassini'` for a synchronously rotating satellite, from its equatorial tilt and orbit normal; `'iau'` for a body whose pole right ascension/declination and rotation phase W are each given as a linear function of time — every `'iau'` body here is one of the seven planets, and every coefficient is NAIF `pck00011.tpc`'s `BODY_POLE_RA`/`BODY_POLE_DEC`/`BODY_PM`, WGCCRE 2015-compliant). Spin axis and gravity-field coefficients are split apart on purpose: which way a body's mesh is oriented is a fact about the body, not about its gravity field. `J2_EARTH`'s outside reader is `ship-placer-panel.ts`'s sun-synchronous-inclination preset. **There is no static per-body flag deciding what counts as a gravity source.** `game/simulation/attractors.ts`'s `gravityBodiesAt` is the sole gate, and it is a plain `mu !== 0` filter over `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)`'s full window (a body whose mass has never been measured is registered with `mu: 0` and drops out) — no separate "is this a gravity source" flag exists to keep in sync with `mu` itself. `CelestialBodyDef.lagrangeLabels?: boolean` (only earth/moon/jupiter/saturn are `true`) separately gates whether `game/camera/focus-markers.ts` mints that body's five L1–L5 focus labels. The seven planets' (and Earth's/the Moon's) orbital elements come from JPL's Standish Table 1 (mean ecliptic/equinox of J2000, valid 1800–2050) plus their own secular per-Julian-century rates; Mercury's ϖ̇ (577.7″/Cy) already includes the 42.98″/Cy general-relativistic contribution. `MU_MARS`/`MU_JUPITER`/`MU_SATURN`/`MU_NEPTUNE` are exported so a satellite's mean orbit shares the exact μ its planet's own `CelestialBodyDef` uses. Eight major moons (Phobos/Deimos around Mars, Io/Europa/Ganymede/Callisto around Jupiter, Titan around Saturn, Triton — retrograde, `incDeg` > 90° — around Neptune) are two-body-only satellites (`equatorialSatelliteOrbit`, no perturbation terms) whose elements are measured against their **parent's own equatorial plane** rather than the ecliptic: `equatorBasis(pole)` builds that plane's `basisToEci` from `body-orientation.ts`'s `equatorBasisToEci` off the same IAU pole (`MARS_POLE`/`JUPITER_POLE`/`SATURN_POLE`/`NEPTUNE_POLE`) the planet's rendered spin axis uses, fixed at its J2000 epoch value. Each satellite's mean motion is `elements.ts`'s `keplerPeriod(a, planetMu)`. 41 more `PlanetId`s round `SOLAR_SYSTEM` out to its full non-satellite population: the original nine — five dwarf planets (Pluto/Haumea/Makemake/Eris/Ceres), two large asteroids (Vesta/Pallas), two comet nuclei (1P/Halley, 2P/Encke) — plus 32 further dwarf planets/TNOs/main-belt asteroids/NEOs/comet nuclei registered later (three of which, Quaoar/Orcus/Didymos, carry their own `kind: 'satellite'` moon — Weywot/Vanth/Dimorphos). All are two-body-only heliocentric ellipses with every secular rate held at 0 — their elements come from JPL's Small-Body Database (`sbdb.api`, `full-prec=true`) at that query's own epoch, extrapolated to J2000 by mean motion (Pluto has no SBDB entry and uses known literature elements instead); comet epochs carry no non-gravitational (outgassing) correction, so position accuracy degrades with distance from that epoch even though period/shape stay exact — the 32 later bodies mostly share one query epoch (JD2461200.5) but five (churyumov/tempel1/wild2/hartley2/bennu) were queried at their own distinct epochs, all alike baked to `simTime = 0` regardless, so none of this reproduces a single real instant of the sky.

  `CelestialBodyDef.shape?: ShapeDef` is a body's static distortion — `{kind:'spheroid', equatorRadius, polarRadius}` for the eight planets (Venus omitted: its pck00011.tpc equatorial/polar radii are equal) or `{kind:'triaxial', a, b, c}` (`a >= b >= c`, `a`/`b` equatorial, `c` the spin-axis semi-axis) for haumea/phobos/deimos/vesta/ceres/io; every other registered body, including pallas (whose two published triaxial fits don't converge — NF-2 forbids picking one), stays a sphere by omitting `shape`. Source is NAIF `pck00011.tpc`'s `BODY_RADII` (a diameter-valued triple for the triaxial bodies, halved on the way in — the field itself is a radius). `shapeAxes(radius, shape)` is the sole place `ShapeDef` becomes a 3-axis scale: `undefined` → `(radius, radius, radius)`, `'spheroid'` → `(equatorRadius, polarRadius, equatorRadius)`, `'triaxial'` → `(a, c, b)` — Y is always the spin axis, matching ECI's own Y-pole convention, since `game/celestial/sphere-body.ts`/`point-body.ts` apply this scale inside the body's own pre-spin local frame. **`radius` (the scalar `Attractor.radius` used by `hitCelestialBody`'s collision sphere and `apsisAltitudes`'s altitude reference) is deliberately left un-ellipsoidized** and instead raised to the shape's circumscribing value (the longest axis: `equatorRadius` for a spheroid, `a` for a triaxial body) wherever a `shape` is added — a collision/altitude sphere sized to the *shortest* axis would let a ship pass through a pole that's actually there. `tests/physics/shape.test.ts` checks this invariant (`radius >= max(axes)`) mechanically across every shaped body, plus Saturn's/Jupiter's `Rp/Re` ratio and two triaxial axis-ratio spot checks against the source table.

  `CelestialBodyDef.rings?: RingSystemDef` is a planet's ring system — `{bands: readonly RingBandDef[]}`, each `RingBandDef` a physical description: `innerRadius`/`outerRadius`/`thickness` in metres, an optional `arcs?: readonly RingArcDef[]` for a longitude-limited bright structure like Neptune's Adams ring, an optional `texture?: RingTextureId` for the one band per system — at most one today — that has a real photographic texture, and `optics: RingOpticsDef` (`normalOpticalDepth`/`singleScatteringAlbedo`/`phaseG`/`color`, built via the file-local `ringOptics(...)` helper) — the Beer-Lambert/single-scattering parameters `physics/ring-optics.ts` and `render/ring.ts`'s TSL shader graph evaluate per fragment, not a flat opacity. `thickness > 0` marks a diffuse, non-flat structure (Jupiter's halo/gossamer rings, Saturn's E ring) drawn as a flattened torus rather than a flat annulus; `thickness === 0` is flat, and whether *that* renders as a filled annulus, a hairline circle, or a crossfade between the two is a display-time, camera-distance decision the data itself does not make (`game/celestial/ring-view.ts`, below) — this module only states the physical width and optics. `RingTextureId` is a bare string identifier, not a URL: `physics/` must not hold an asset path, so resolving `'saturn'` to an actual image is `game/celestial/celestial-registry.ts`'s `RING_TEXTURES` table's job. jupiter/saturn/uranus/neptune/quaoar/chariklo carry `rings` today — jupiter (4 bands: halo + main + two gossamer rings), saturn (5: the textured D–A band, F, G, the diffuse E ring, and the far-out Phoebe ring), uranus (13, one per named ring, all flat and thin enough to read as lines at any sane zoom), neptune (5, including the arc-bearing Adams ring), quaoar (2: Q1R/Q2R, from Morgado et al. 2023), chariklo (2: C1R/C2R) — sourced from Wikipedia's per-planet ring articles or the cited paper (in the module's own comments) via the file-local `ringBand(innerKm, outerKm, thicknessKm?, arcs?, texture?)` km→m helper.
- `src/physics/lagrange.ts` — the restricted three-body Lagrange points as pure geometry: `collinearGamma(mu, point)` (Newton's method for the collinear-point distance ratio from the quintic balance equation, seeded from the Hill radius) and `lagrangePoints(mu, place)` (the five points' dimensionless rotating-frame coordinates, mapped to a caller-supplied `place(x, y)`). Mass-ratio-only — no body identity, no ECI.
- `src/physics/shadow.ts` — `sunlitFactor(r, sunDir, penumbra)`: Earth's cylindrical-shadow日照率, split out of `ephemeris.ts` because it's geometry from a position and a sun direction, not "where is a body at time t" — a genuinely different question despite reading like ephemeris code. `penumbra` is a caller-supplied argument since `physics/` must not depend on `game/const.ts`.
- `src/physics/srp.ts` — `srpAccel(r, sun, srpCoeff, sunlit)`: solar radiation pressure as the standard cannonball model — the body is a sphere of constant reflectivity, so the force is along the sun→body line regardless of attitude and the coefficient and cross-section only ever appear as the product `srpCoeff = C_R·A/m` [m²/kg] (the same shape as drag's `bcInv = Cd·A/m`, and stored the same way: one constant per entity class). `SOLAR_PRESSURE_1AU` = 4.56e-6 N/m² and `ASTRONOMICAL_UNIT` are this module's own — the latter is the inverse-square reference distance, deliberately not read from `solar-system.ts`'s Earth semi-major axis, which is a different quantity that merely has a similar value. The shadow factor is supplied by the caller rather than computed here, so `shadow.ts`'s `sunlitFactor` stays the single answer to "is this point in sunlight" shared with the lighting, radiator and power systems. **Near Earth this is the smallest perturbation modeled** (~7e-8 m/s² for the player ship, about a quarter of the drag at 420 km), but drag vanishes with altitude while this does not, so out at the Lagrange points it is the only non-gravitational force left.
- `src/physics/body-orientation.ts` — a body's own orientation: not just its spin axis, but its spin *phase* about that axis (which way the prime meridian points) — as opposed to where the body is (`ephemeris.ts`). `cassiniSpinAxis(eclipticPole, orbitNormal, obliquity)` builds a synchronously rotating satellite's spin axis from Cassini's laws (spin axis, orbit normal and ecliptic pole stay coplanar, with the spin axis on the far side of the ecliptic pole from the orbit normal — so feeding it a precessing orbit normal makes the spin axis precess on the same 18.6-year period for free). `orthogonalizedTo(pole, reference)` orthogonalizes a reference direction against the pole — a general-purpose projection, used both for the `C22` long axis and for a meridian direction. Phase is anchored the way IAU's rotation angle `W` is: `spinPhaseRef(axis)` is the direction phase 0 points at — the ascending node of the body's equator on the ECI equator (`ECI_POLE × axis`, falling back to the vernal-equinox direction `orthogonalizedTo(axis, (1,0,0))` when the axis is ~parallel to `ECI_POLE`, i.e. the node is undefined) — `spinPhaseOf(axis, meridian)` reads the phase [rad] of an arbitrary meridian direction against that reference, `meridianDirection(axis, spinAngle)` is its inverse, and `spinOrientation(axis, spinAngle)` composes the two into a full quaternion (model +Y = spin axis, +Z = prime meridian, via `attitude.ts`'s `qFromForwardUp`) — the one function `game/celestial/sphere-body.ts` needs to orient a body's mesh. `equatorBasisToEci(axis)` builds the *reference-plane* counterpart for orbital elements rather than a mesh orientation: z = `axis` (the body's spin axis), x = `spinPhaseRef(axis)` (the same phase-0 direction `meridianDirection`/`spinOrientation` use) — so a satellite's orbit and its parent's rendered prime meridian share one IAU-ascending-node convention. It is `kepler-orbit.ts`'s `KeplerOrbit.basisToEci`'s sole non-ecliptic source, built by `solar-system.ts`'s `equatorBasis` for every equator-referenced satellite. **The orbit normal is not the spin axis** — for the Moon the two are 6.688° apart, and substituting one for the other tilts the `C22`/`J2` perturbation by that much without changing its magnitude, which is why `body-orientation.test.ts` checks the angles rather than the resulting acceleration. Pure functions, no body identity.
- `src/physics/ephemeris.ts` — `Ephemeris`: composes star → planet-satellite-system barycenter → planet/satellite (the three-stage pipeline: a planet's barycenter follows its own heliocentric Kepler orbit via `planetOrbit`/`keplerOrbitState`; each satellite's planet-relative state comes from `satelliteState` fed the planet's own `planetAngles`; the planet body itself is then the barycenter minus Σ(μ_satellite/Σμ)·(satellite's planet-relative state), applied to both position *and* velocity, and each satellite is that planet position plus its own planet-relative state), then ECI-izes by subtracting the origin body's own heliocentric state. Holds **no name-specific branch** — every step dispatches on the registry's `kind`, so adding a body needs no change here. Every evaluation adds `epochOffsetSec` (defaulting to the exported `EPOCH_T_OFFSET`, a fixed 6.9721972e6 s — the value that anchors `simTime = 0` to "the sun sits on +X" under `SOLAR_SYSTEM`; a custom registry would need its own value if that anchor is meaningful there) to `t` before consulting a body's Standish elements. **The constructor takes `registry`/`originId`/`epochOffsetSec`, each defaulted to today's values (`SOLAR_SYSTEM`/`'earth'`/`EPOCH_T_OFFSET`)** — every existing `new Ephemeris()` call site is therefore unaffected; only a stage that declares its own `ephemerisConfig` (`stage-dictionary.ts`) supplies different ones. **`frameOf(center, rotatingWith)`** is the sole mint point for a `ReferenceFrame`: it caches by the `(center, rotatingWith)` pair (a `Map<AttractorId, Map<OrbitingId | null, ReferenceFrame>>`) and always returns the same reference for the same pair, whether or not either id belongs to the active registry — a living gravity-bearing `GameEntity` used as a rotation-system center works the same way as a registered body, since `frameTransformAt` (below) already resolves both. From `registry`/`originId` the constructor derives, once, through this cache: `ids` (the registry's keys, in declaration order — the same order `attractorsAt` returns); `starId` (`solar-system.ts`'s `starOf(registry)`, `null` for a registry with no star); `inertialFrame` = `frameOf(originId, null)`; and `frames` (one inertial `ReferenceFrame` per registered body plus one rotating frame per orbiting body) — **this is what `frame.ts`'s former module-level `FRAMES`/`INERTIAL_FRAME` became**, since which frames exist is a property of the active registry, not a global constant. `frameFor(id)` is `frameOf(id, null)` under a name that reads as "the inertial frame at this body" at call sites that only ever want that. Because every one of these goes through the same cache, `render/sampled-line.ts`'s `frame === lastFrame` re-bake guard holds regardless of which of `frameOf`/`frameFor`/`inertialFrame`/`frames` produced the value being compared. Public surface: `stateOf`/`positionOf(id, t)`; `orbitFrameRotationAt`/`orbitNormalAt(id: OrbitingId, t)`; `lagrangeAt(secondary, t)` (primary resolved via `primaryOf(registry, secondary)`, throwing if `secondary` has no resolvable primary); `sunDirFrom(r, t)` (the unit direction from an ECI point to the star — the reference point is an argument because the star sits in a different direction from each point, so no privileged origin can stand in for it once the player leaves Earth's neighborhood; `starId === null` falls back to a fixed +X direction, since shadow/SRP have nothing to compute against); `frameTransformAt(frame, t, attractors)` — the sole place a `ReferenceFrame`'s origin/rotation is resolved into a concrete `FrameTransform`, now taking the frame's-worth of live `Attractor`s as a third argument so it can resolve **two ways**: `frame.rotatingWith` **registered** in the active registry uses the existing analytic `orbitFrameRotationAt` (unchanged behavior under `SOLAR_SYSTEM`); **unregistered** (a living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`) builds a frame from that body's live relative state in `attractors` (x̂ = center→body, ẑ = relative angular momentum direction) — the only model available for a body with no stored orbital elements, and deliberately not treated as an approximation of one, since a free multi-body system has no long-term analytic basis to approximate; `poleAt(id, t)` (a body's spin axis and spin phase — `{axis, spinAngle} | null`, `null` for a star or a body with no `pole` model — resolved by the private `orientationOf`, which dispatches purely on `PoleModel.kind`); `game/celestial/sphere-body.ts`'s `SphereBody.sync` is `poleAt`'s sole consumer. `attractorsAt(t)` (registry declaration order) — **the single window**, used both for gravity (narrowed to `mu !== 0` by `game/simulation/attractors.ts`'s `gravityBodiesAt` and merged with any living gravity-bearing `GameEntity` before a caller classifies it into the spatial grid — see `attractor.ts`/`attractors.ts` above) and for every other "which bodies are there" question (occlusion checks, surface-contact/`hitCelestialBody` checks, center-body resolution via `strongestAttractor`, integration/sampling-interval step sizing) — there is no second, gravity-only window to keep in sync with this one. `attractorsAt`'s per-body `Degree2Gravity` resolution also calls `orientationOf`, so a body's gravity-field pole and its rendered spin axis can never disagree. `attractorsAt`'s result, plus the intermediate `planetHelioState`/`satelliteRelState` composition steps, are each backed by a **fixed-length ring cache (4 slots) keyed on exact-match `t`**: a cache hit returns the same array reference every time (callers must not mutate it), a miss always recomputes from scratch, and because a miss never depends on what was looked up before, there is no ordering constraint between callers. `setPhaseOffsets` clears all three cache groups (`planetHelioCache`, `satelliteRelCache`, the `attractorsAt` ring). `getPhaseOffsets()`/`setPhaseOffsets(...)` are the save/load entry points — `Game.restore` writes through the shared instance rather than constructing a fresh `Ephemeris`. THREE-independent; the single instance is **owned by `Game`** and shared by reference with `EnvironmentScene`, `Simulator`, `OverviewCamera`, `PlanEditor`/`PlanDisplay`/`PlanPath`, `FocusMarkers`, `Player.behave`, and `Enemy.behave`/`Stage.behaveAllEnemies`. **The constructor's optional `absoluteSource`/`epochJdTdb` arguments are the analytic model's high-precision upgrade path** (see `src/physics/ephemeris-pack/` below): when supplied, they build `private readonly precise = new OriginCenteredEphemeris(absoluteSource, originId, epochJdTdb)`, and `stateOf`/`orbitFrameRotationAt`/`orbitNormalAt`/`attractorsAt`'s per-body lookups all check `this.precise?.hasBody(id)` first, falling back to the Standish-elements pipeline described above only for a body the precise source doesn't carry — a satellite absent from the pack falls back by adding its existing planet-relative model on top of the *precise* parent state rather than the analytic parent, so precision degrades gracefully body-by-body instead of all-or-nothing. `main.ts` supplies both by default (skipped only when the launched stage declares its own `ephemerisConfig`, since an invented registry has no real astronomical data to correct with) — the analytic pipeline above is the fallback path, not the primary one, for every stage that plays in the real solar system.
- `src/physics/time/index.ts` — `Date`-free astronomical time-scale conversion: `CalendarDate<S>`/`JulianDate<S>` are branded by `TimeScale` (`'UTC' | 'TT' | 'TDB'`) so a UTC value can never be silently fed where TDB is expected, and calendar↔Julian-date conversion (`calendarDateToJulianDate`/`julianDateToCalendarDate`, `parseCalendarDate`) is hand-rolled rather than routed through JS `Date` because `Date` can hold neither a fractional Julian day nor the five-digit years `far-future-20000` reaches. `TT`↔`TDB` differ only by a sub-millisecond periodic term this module ignores (`ttToTdb`/`tdbToTt` are near-identity here); `UTC`↔`TT` needs the accumulated leap-second/ΔT offset, which this module does not hardcode — `UtcOffsetProvider`/`TdbOffsetProvider` are injected by the caller, keeping this module free of a leap-second table it would otherwise have to keep updating. THREE-independent, no game-state dependency; `ephemeris-pack/absolute-adapter.ts` is its main consumer (a Chebyshev pack's native time axis is JD_TDB).
- `src/physics/ephemeris-pack/` — the binary Chebyshev-polynomial ephemeris format (JPL DE440/DE441-derived, the same representation SPICE/BSP files use) that gives `Ephemeris` its optional high-precision path, split by what each layer is allowed to know:
  - `types.ts` — the in-memory pack shapes (`ChebyshevManifest`/`ChebyshevSegment`/`ChebyshevBodyPack`/`ChebyshevEphemerisPack`), deliberately binary-, JD-, and frame-independent: times are plain SI seconds on a caller-chosen axis and positions are metres, so this shape (and everything built on it below) has no notion of TDB or ICRF until an explicit adapter says so.
  - `evaluator.ts` — `ChebyshevEphemeris`: the pure interpolation engine, knowing nothing about JD_TDB or ICRF. `segmentOf`/`evaluate` binary-search the body's segment list for the one covering a given `time` (`findChebyshevSegmentIndex`) and evaluate that segment's Chebyshev series (`evaluateChebyshevWithDerivative`) into a `KinematicState` — position from the series value, velocity from its analytic derivative rescaled by the segment's own `[start, end]`→`[-1, 1]` affine mapping, so no finite-difference velocity estimate is needed. The constructor snapshots every input coefficient array (`copyBody`) and deep-freezes the result, so a caller mutating the array it originally passed in can't retroactively corrupt an already-constructed evaluator. `ChebyshevBodyNotFoundError`/`ChebyshevTimeOutOfRangeError` are explicit rather than silently clamping or extrapolating outside a segment's covered span.
  - `absolute-adapter.ts` — `ChebyshevAbsoluteEphemeris`: the one place a `ChebyshevEphemeris`'s plain-seconds axis becomes JD_TDB, implementing `AbsoluteEphemeris` (below) directly over an evaluator plus a chosen `epochJdTdb`. It refuses a manifest that doesn't declare `coordinateFrame: 'ICRF-J2000'`/`timeScale: 'TDB'` (when the manifest states either field at all — `toEvaluatorEphemerisPack` already validated the canonical binary manifest and keeps only the core evaluator fields, so an *absent* declaration is accepted as "not yet checked" rather than rejected) — the runtime pack-loading path (`packed-absolute-ephemeris.ts`) performs the same JD↔seconds conversion inline rather than going through this class, so this adapter's own primary consumer today is the evaluator-level test suite exercising the JD_TDB boundary in isolation from the rest of the loading pipeline.
  - `format.ts` — the on-disk `.epk` binary layout: an 8-byte magic (`TEPUIEPK`) + version/size header, a canonical-JSON manifest (`canonicalJson` sorts object keys recursively so the same data always encodes to the same bytes, which is what makes a payload SHA-256 meaningful), then a contiguous little-endian Float64 payload of X/Y/Z Chebyshev coefficients per series. Deliberately boring and dependency-free (a Node CLI tool can write it, a browser can read it with nothing but `DataView`/`TextDecoder`) rather than reusing an existing ephemeris container format, since the game only ever needs a handful of bodies' worth of coefficients, not a general-purpose SPK reader. `decodeEphemerisPack`/`validateManifest` reject a truncated, corrupt-header, or non-finite payload outright; `toEvaluatorEphemerisPack` adapts a decoded pack into the shape `evaluator.ts` expects.
  - `index.ts` — barrel re-export of the three files above.
- `src/physics/absolute-ephemeris.ts` — the abstraction every precise ephemeris source implements: `AbsoluteEphemeris` (`hasBody`/`barycentricStateOf(id, jdTdb)`, returning a `BarycentricState` in raw ICRF/J2000 metres) plus its own `validStartJdTdb`/`validEndJdTdb`, so a source's coverage is a property of the interface, not read off a concrete class. `icrfToGameEci` is the one place ICRF's (vernal-equinox, equatorial-plane, north-pole) axes are remapped into the game's ECI (vernal-equinox, north-pole, equatorial-plane) convention — a right-handed axis relabeling, not a rotation, since both frames share the same equinox and equator. `OriginCenteredEphemeris` is what `physics/ephemeris.ts`'s `Ephemeris` actually holds as `precise`: it takes a raw barycentric `AbsoluteEphemeris`, an origin body, and the JD_TDB epoch `simTime = 0` corresponds to, and its `stateOf(id, simTime)` converts `simTime` to JD_TDB, looks up both `id` and the origin body's own barycentric state, subtracts them, and axis-remaps the result — so the barycenter never needs to be a body the rest of the game reasons about. `MissingEphemerisBodyError` fires rather than silently falling back for a body the source doesn't carry, since a silent fallback here would defeat the whole point of asking for the precise source in the first place — `Ephemeris.stateOf`'s own `precise?.hasBody(id)` check is what makes the *fallback-to-analytic* decision instead, one layer up.
- `src/physics/packed-absolute-ephemeris.ts` — `PackedAbsoluteEphemeris implements AbsoluteEphemeris`: the concrete, file-backed precise source, composing `ephemeris-pack/format.ts`'s decoder with `ephemeris-pack/evaluator.ts`'s `ChebyshevEphemeris` (converting JD_TDB↔J2000-ET-seconds itself, the same conversion `absolute-adapter.ts` offers as a reusable class — see above for why this path doesn't go through it). `verifyEphemerisPayload` is a separate, explicitly async step (SHA-256 over the payload bytes alone, via `crypto.subtle` — deliberately not folded into the synchronous `decodeEphemerisPack`, since decoding a pack that's merely *malformed* is a synchronous, offline question, while asking whether the bytes are the *expected* bytes needs the browser's crypto API and is meaningful only for data that arrived over the network) — `loadPackedAbsoluteEphemeris` composes decode+verify+construct as the one path a network-fetched pack should go through; `fromTrustedBytes` skips verification for bytes a caller already trusts (e.g. re-decoding within a session).
- `src/physics/ephemeris-profile.ts` — **the near-future/far-future split itself**: a pure declaration of which pack covers which era, with no data or game-mode logic. `EPHEMERIS_PROFILES` holds exactly two non-overlapping-by-design entries — `'modern-de440'` (JPL DE440, valid for a 10-Julian-year window starting 2026-01-01) and `'far-future-20000'` (`sourceModel: 'Tepui long-term integration anchored to JPL DE441'`, valid for a 10-year window starting in the invented year 20115) — because a single Chebyshev fit can't stay accurate across millennia, and the far-future era has no real JPL solution to draw from at all: it's Tepui's own long-term numerical integration, anchored to DE441 at its start, packaged in the exact same `.epk`/Chebyshev representation so `Ephemeris`/`OriginCenteredEphemeris` need no branch to consume either one. Each profile's `packId` embeds the expected pack's own payload hash, so `ephemeris-catalog.ts` (below) can catch a stale or mismatched binary asset before it's ever decoded. `profileAt(jdTdb, requestedProfile?)` is the sole selector — given just a JD_TDB it picks whichever profile's range covers it (`UnsupportedEphemerisEpochError` if none does), or validates a caller-named profile explicitly; the "no implicit priority between overlapping profiles" comment in the source is a guardrail for a third profile someone adds later, not a case that exists today.
- `src/physics/ephemeris-catalog.ts` — `loadAbsoluteEphemeris(profileId, epochJdTdb, requiredEndJdTdb)`: the only module that knows the `.epk` binary assets exist (`assets/ephemeris/modern-2026-10y.epk`/`far-future-20115-10y.epk`, webpack-imported as URLs via `PACK_URLS`). Fetches the profile's own file, decodes+verifies it (`loadPackedAbsoluteEphemeris`), then checks the *decoded* payload's SHA-256 against the *profile's declared* `packId` — a second, independent check beyond the loader's own tamper check, since a byte-valid pack that simply isn't the one this profile expects (a stale build artifact, a mismatched deploy) must still be caught — and finally range-checks that the requested `[epochJdTdb, requiredEndJdTdb]` window actually falls inside the loaded pack's own validity span. `main.ts` calls `profileAt(SIM_EPOCH_JD_TDB)` then this function (skipped only when the launched stage supplies its own `ephemerisConfig`) to build the `AbsoluteEphemeris` passed into `Game`'s constructor and on into `Ephemeris`.
- `src/physics/frame.ts` — a display coordinate system is **an origin body × an optional rotation**, `ReferenceFrame = {center: AttractorId, rotatingWith: OrbitingId | null}` — independent choices, so the type is their direct product rather than a single enumerated frame name. `rotatingWith: null` is an inertial frame at `center`; otherwise the frame spins with whichever orbiting body `rotatingWith` names — for a satellite that's its planet (so `{center:'earth', rotatingWith:'moon'}` holds the moon still), for a planet that's itself (`{center:'earth', rotatingWith:'earth'}` holds the sun's apparent motion still without moving the origin off Earth). **This module holds no registry and mints no `ReferenceFrame` values of its own** — `Ephemeris.frameOf`/`frameFor`/`inertialFrame`/`frames` are the only source (moved there from this module's former `FRAMES`/`INERTIAL_FRAME`/`rotatingFrameCenterOf`, since which frames exist and which body a rotating frame's origin resolves to are properties of *which registry is active*, not a fact this module could hold on its own); every UI/display consumer must reference one of `Ephemeris`'s elements rather than construct a `ReferenceFrame` literal — a fresh object would still be `==`-equal in content but not in reference, and `render/sampled-line.ts`'s `frame === lastFrame` re-bake guard is a reference check (silently degrading to "re-bake every frame" on a mismatch, not a crash, which is why the discipline has to be enforced by convention rather than caught by a type).

  A `ReferenceFrame`'s actual motion at a moment — `FrameTransform = {origin, originVel, q, omega}` — is supplied by `Ephemeris.frameTransformAt`, which this module does not import (avoiding a `frame.ts` ⇄ `ephemeris.ts` cycle); `frame.ts` itself is pure conversion arithmetic over an already-resolved `FrameTransform`. Six conversions split by what kind of quantity is being moved: **points** (`toFramePoint`/`toInertialPoint`, affine — origin translation *and* rotation, for a position) and **directions** (`toFrameDir`/`toInertialDir`, linear — rotation only, for a displacement/velocity-difference/up-vector); their outputs are the branded `FramePoint`/`FrameDir` types so a caller can't feed a direction through the point path (or vice versa) and get a silently wrong offset the moment a frame's origin starts moving. `toFrameState`/`toInertialState` do the same split within one `KinematicState`: `v_rel = R⁻¹(v − ȯ − ω×r_rel)`, the Coriolis-like `ω×r_rel` term existing because a rotating frame's own spin contributes to the relative velocity, not just its translation. `toFrameState` bakes at the state's own epoch (`s.t`) and needs no time argument; `toInertialState` still takes one, because un-baking happens at the *display* time, not the sample time. Used only by display concerns (`render/sampled-line.ts` bake/un-bake, `OverviewCamera`'s viewpoint offset/pan/up, `PlanPath`'s picking) — the simulation itself is always plain ECI.
- `src/physics/projection.ts` — the pure screen-projection math (`Viewpoint` → `projectToNdc` → `ndcToScreen`, yielding `Projected {x, y, front}`). **It lives under `physics/` deliberately**: a pinhole projection is the geometric map from Cartesian to projective coordinates, exact and free of any look-and-feel tuning — the kind of thing `physics/` is for. What would not belong here is anything that bends that map for legibility (marker nudging, distance compression, fades); those stay in `game/`. Independent of THREE.js and of the floating origin; each camera in `game/camera/` binds it into a `ProjectFn`. `metersPerPixel(view, worldPos, viewportHeight)` is the inverse question — how much world distance one screen pixel spans at a given point (`2·depth·tan(fov/2)/viewportHeight`, depth measured along the same forward axis `projectToNdc` uses and floored at `MIN_DEPTH`) — which is what lets a screen-space length be drawn as world geometry.
- `src/physics/intercept.ts` — `solveLeadTime` (the lead/interception time for a relative position+velocity and a muzzle speed, used by the enemy AI's aim) and `leadPoint` (the world position to aim at, given two `KinematicState`s and a cut-off time; `null` when there's no usable solution). `leadPoint` is what keeps `LeadMarkers` from rolling its own lead math.
- `src/physics/occlusion.ts` — `isOccluded(cameraPos, point, attractors)`: pure ray-vs-sphere geometry answering whether a camera's line of sight to a point is blocked by any `Attractor`'s sphere before reaching it (the near intersection of the ray with a body must fall strictly between the camera and the point). The map view's icon visibility (`plan-display.ts`'s apsis icons, `nav-target.ts`'s relative AN/DN, `marker/equator-node-markers.ts`'s EqAN/EqDN) and its pick candidates (`map-picker.ts`'s `refresh`, `plan/plan-path.ts`'s `nearestSample`) all call this one function, so "hidden behind a body" and "unpickable" can never disagree.
- `src/physics/halo.ts` — creative-mode-only: initial `KinematicState`s for halo/Lissajous orbits about the collinear Earth-Moon/Sun-Earth L1/L2 points. The rotating local frame (`collinearFrame`) is built entirely from existing `ephemeris.ts` API — `lagrangeAt` for the point's position, `orbitFrameRotationAt`/`orbitNormalAt` for its rotation and plane normal — so this module adds no orbital elements or rotation axes of its own; from the point's distance ratio γ it derives Richardson's `cn` coefficients and the linear in-plane/out-of-plane frequencies λ/ωz (verified against the published values: Sun-Earth L1 λ=2.0864, ωz=2.0152, k=3.2293; Earth-Moon L1 λ=2.3344, ωz=2.2688). **Halo orbits do not exist in the linear solution** — λ ≠ ωz, so the two oscillations never close into a loop. They appear at third order, where Richardson (1980)'s amplitude constraint `l1·Ax² + l2·Az² + Δ = 0` makes the frequencies coincide; `haloState` therefore takes only the out-of-plane amplitude `Az`, solves that constraint for `Ax` (reproducing the ISEE-3 halo — Az = 110,000 km → Ax ≈ 205,000 km, Ay ≈ 662,000 km, both within 1% of the published figures) and only then drives the linear solution at the single shared frequency. `lissajousState` imposes no constraint and takes both amplitudes, leaving the two frequencies independent (an open Lissajous figure). The state itself is the first-order solution — the third-order series expansion is not included. And **the game's integrator is not a restricted three-body problem** (it's full multi-body gravity + degree-2 fields + drag + SRP, same as every other entity via `dynamics.ts`'s `stepDynamics`), so a ship placed at one of these states does **not** stay on a halo/Lissajous orbit once the simulation runs — it drifts, same as it would drift off any other idealized orbit under real perturbations. `CreativeStage.buildLagrangeState` is the only caller.
- `src/physics/dynamics.ts` — the RK4 integration core, in two layers. `stepRK4(s, dt, accel)` is the bare stepper, driven entirely by a caller-supplied acceleration callback (`AccelFn`: `(rx, ry, rz, vx, vy, vz) => Vec3`, evaluated per RK4 stage) and returning the advanced `KinematicState` — epoch included — without touching its input; it holds **no gravity of its own**, so a caller wanting a bare two-body step supplies its own gravity callback (as `dynamics.test.ts` does when it checks `stepDynamics` against a hand-written legacy composition). `stepDynamics(state, dt, attractors, bcInv, srpCoeff, penumbra, thrust)` is the single RK4 step behind `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory.step` — the one place "full multi-body gravity + degree-2 fields + drag + SRP + thrust" is composed, whether the caller is `GameEntity.stepActual` (drag/SRP on via each entity's own `bcInv`/`srpCoeff`, its own `thrust`), `GameEntity.stepPredicted`, or `plan/plan-arc.ts` (`thrust = null`, since neither a prediction nor a planned trajectory thrusts). Its module-private `totalAccel(r, v, attractors, bcInv, srpCoeff, penumbra)` sums `attractor.ts`'s `attractorAccel(r, attractor)` over every body (each term already carrying its own ECI non-inertial correction), this module's own `degree2Accel` for every body that carries a `degree2` field, `atmosphere.ts`'s `dragAccel` (`bcInv = 0` skips drag), and `srp.ts`'s `srpAccel` gated by `shadow.ts`'s `sunlitFactor`, summed over every `isStar` body (`srpCoeff = 0` or zero registered stars both skip it cleanly) — `stepDynamics` adds an optional `thrust` on top and drives it all through `stepRK4`. **`totalAccel` holds no body-specific branch**: which bodies are oblate, how oblate, which are stars, and which way their poles point all ride on the `Attractor` itself, so adding an oblate body or a second star needs no change here. `degree2Accel(rRel, mu, g)` takes a *body-centered relative* position (unlike the point-mass term, which is built in ECI) and evaluates both the zonal `J2` term — `k[(1 − 5u²)r + 2(r·p̂)p̂]`, `k = −(3/2)J2·μ·R²/|r|⁵`, for an arbitrary pole `p̂` — and, when the field is not axisymmetric, the tesseral `C22` term as the gradient of `3·C22·μ·R²·(x² − y²)/|r|⁵` in the principal-axis frame. Earth uses it with the pole on Y (verified ≈ −5°/day RAAN regression at 420 km/51.6°); the Moon uses it with a Cassini-state pole and a non-zero `C22`. No out-parameter variant — see the immutability note above.
- `src/physics/attitude.ts` — rigid-body attitude: quaternion + body-frame ω via Euler's equations. ω integrated with RK4 + kinetic-energy projection (naive explicit integration diverges — this is what makes the Dzhanibekov effect on debris stable long-term); `stepAttitude` carries the sub-step ω/q as scalar locals and returns one new `Attitude`. `qFromForwardUp` (nose +Z / up +Y basis → quaternion, re-orthogonalizing, null at the fwd∥up singularity) is the shared attitude-basis builder used by `player/player.ts` (`progradeAttitude`), `attitudeAlignError`/`attitudeAlignTorque` (below), and `stages/spawner/enemy-generator.ts` (`generateApproachingEnemy`). `attitudeAlignError(desiredFwd, desiredUp, q)` reduces a target forward/up pair and the current attitude to a signed error angle and body-frame axis (`null` at the fwd∥up singularity); `attitudeAlignTorque` adds a PD torque on top (gains passed in by the caller, since `physics/` doesn't read `game/const.ts`) — the one implementation of "torque a rigid body toward a target facing" shared by `player/player-throttle.ts`'s prograde-hold autopilot and `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s burn-direction alignment. Pure functions.
- `src/physics/vec3.ts` — plain-object Vec3 math helpers (all pure, all returning new immutable `Vec3`s), the exported deterministic PRNG `mulberry32(seed)` (a `Math.random`-free `() => number` generator — the seed itself decides the sequence, which is what lets a debug stage or a save/load path reproduce the exact same layout), and the shared random helpers `randSym`/`randVec`/`randPerp` — each takes an optional `rand: () => number` (default `Math.random`), so a caller that needs determinism (a debug stage, `game/celestial/point-field.ts`) passes its own `mulberry32` instance through instead of these helpers hard-coding `Math.random`.
- `src/physics/spatial-grid.ts` — `SpatialGrid<T>`: a general-purpose uniform grid over anything with a position — `insert(item, pos)` buckets by `floor(pos / cellSize)` per axis, `neighbors(pos)` returns everything in `pos`'s own cell plus its 26 neighbors (27 cells total). Pure, no game-state dependency; cell size is a constructor argument, so the same class backs both `game/simulation/attractors.ts`'s gravity-source classification (cell size derived per call from the heaviest gridded body's `μ`) and `game/simulation/contact.ts`'s contact-pair enumeration (cell size derived per call from that frame's own radii/movement) without either caller needing to know the other exists.
- `src/physics/deque.ts` — `Deque<T>`: a generic ring-buffer double-ended queue (`pushLeft`/`pushRight`/`popLeft`/`popRight`/`at`/`peekLeft`/`peekRight`/`deleteLeftN`/`deleteRightN`/`clear`), capacity doubling on overflow. No game-state dependency; a general-purpose data structure, not physics per se, but grouped under `physics/` since it's equally pure/DOM-free and covered by the same `test:physics` runner.
- `src/physics/state-queue.ts` — `StateQueue`: a `Deque<KinematicState>`-backed time series kept newest-first (`peekLeft`/`newest` = newest, `peekRight` = oldest). `push` adds the latest sample; a push whose time doesn't exceed the current newest is treated as a correction and discards everything at-or-after its own time before inserting (the same "editing invalidates downstream" rule `Plan` node edits follow), so the pushed sample always ends up as the new head. `capCount(n)` trims down to the `n` most recent samples. `cleanup(maxAge, minCount)` is the age-based sibling — this is what backs `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory.history` window (`DynamicTrajectory.step` calls it with `keepDuration` on every recorded push): prunes samples older than `maxAge` relative to the newest one, never below `minCount`, and (when `maxAge` is nonzero) always leaves one sample older than the cutoff in place so `at(newest.t - maxAge)` still has a bracketing pair to interpolate against instead of falling outside the retained span. `discardFrom(t)` drops every sample at-or-after `t` without inserting anything — `DynamicTrajectory.reset` uses it to invalidate samples that are no longer valid after a discontinuous state overwrite. `at(t)` binary-searches the bracketing pair and reuses `kinematic-state.ts`'s `hermiteInterpolate`, returning `null` outside the retained span rather than extrapolating. The private `bisect` (lower-bound over the descending series) is the single search primitive `push`/`cleanup`/`discardFrom`/`at` all share. `toArrayOldestFirst()` reverses the internal newest-first order into the oldest-first array `render/sampled-line.ts`'s `SampledLine.syncGeometry` expects — the one place this class's storage order leaks out.
- `src/render/earth.ts` — realistic-style Earth at true radius: a 1024×768 indexed `SphereGeometry` textured with real photography (`assets/earth.jpg` + `assets/8k_clouds.jpg`) and shaded with TSL nodes. **The atmosphere is analytic, not geometric** — the stacked shells this file used to draw are gone. At near=2m with a 24-bit non-logarithmic depth buffer, any geometry floating tens-to-hundreds of km above the surface z-fights with it at grazing view angles (δz ≈ z²/near/2²⁴ grows with the square of distance), and neither shell spacing nor shell count fixes the quantization itself. So the invariant is **exactly one opaque depth-tested body below ~400 km**: clouds are baked into the surface albedo, and both the near haze and the limb glow are computed per-pixel from the view direction (`ATMO_HAZE_TAU0`, `ATMO_RIM_MIN_H`/`ATMO_RIM_MAX_H` = 340 km/`ATMO_RIM_SCALE_H`), with occlusion by the Earth resolved by analytic ray-sphere intersection rather than the hardware depth test. Aurora curtains are real geometry on the spin group — 4 `render/aurora.ts` `Aurora` instances, two per pole, the two layers of a pole sharing a `geomSeed` so they stay parallel — each advanced by `Earth.tick(simTime)`, which also refreshes the atmosphere shader's earth-center uniform.
- `src/render/aurora.ts` — `Aurora`: one wavy additive curtain ringing a pole at ~67° latitude, built as vertex-colored geometry whose breaks and color flicker come from periodic (loop-closing) noise functions. The constructor takes the pole sign plus the shape/color/radius/latitude/flicker offsets that distinguish one layer from another; `mesh` is the built curtain and `sync(phase)` moves its waving and flicker to that phase.
- `src/render/ships.ts` — low-poly meshes, **loaded rather than built**: the primitive-assembly code lives in `tools/export-models.mjs`, which serializes each mesh with `Object3D.toJSON()` into `src/assets/models/*.json`; at runtime this file parses those with `THREE.ObjectLoader` and `clone(true)`s them, so startup never re-assembles geometry. The modeled shapes are: player ship (nose = body +Z; stubby box hull, 4 aft engines, small solar paddles, two vertical gun bores on the front face — `MUZZLE_OFFSETS` is exported so `player/player-fire.ts` fires/ejects from the exact modeled positions, and the attitude thrusters come from `render/rcs-nozzles.ts` (below); the two deployable radiators sit on the hull sides just below the solar panels (`x=±2.62, y=0.30, z=-2.20`) and are accordions: each hinge group (`RADIATOR_OBJECT_NAMES`) holds `RADIATOR_FOLD_COUNT` fold groups **nested one inside the next** (`radiatorFoldName(side, i)`), each offset along its own local +X (signed by side — `up` toward `+X`, `down` toward `-X` — both in the model's fold spacing and the panel/skeleton mesh placement within a fold, which must agree or the panel ends up facing the wrong way inside its own fold), so `player/radiator.ts` drives the whole chain by writing one `rotation.y` per fold and never recomputes segment positions. The panel's thin axis (its normal) is local Z, perpendicular to both the extend direction (X) and the solar panels' own normal (Y). Stowed (`STOW_TILT`=90°), that normal is exactly ±X — flush with the hull side, matching the panel's stowed footprint; deployed (`RADIATOR_DEPLOY_TILT`, now a near-zero residual so the accordion fold visibly straightens out), the normal swings to ±Z (fore/aft), maximizing cross-section when viewed from the front — the accordion literally unfolds flat rather than settling into a fixed pleat. `RADIATOR_HINGE` (the fold chain's own anchor point, below) is re-exported from here rather than defined here, so a caller reading the ship model's constants in one place finds it alongside `RADIATOR_SEGMENT_LENGTH`/`RADIATOR_DEPLOY_TILT`), magazine belt links (`buildMagazineMesh`: caged 4×8 = 32 visible brass rounds — 1:1 with `MAG_ROUNDS`) and orbital ammo pickups (`buildAmmo`), enemy variants (`buildEnemyShip`/`buildStage0EnemyShip`), shared-geometry tracer bullets (`buildBulletMesh`/`buildPlasmaMesh`), CIWS-motif bottleneck casings (`LatheGeometry` profile: rim + extractor groove + tapered body + shoulder + neck), debris shards, ejected barrels/magazine frames, and the base station (`buildBaseModel`: central hub + 4 radial trusses/docking modules + 2 solar wings + an accent beacon, all primitive-built rather than loaded — the one shape here assembled directly with `THREE.*Geometry` calls at construction time instead of parsed from `src/assets/models/*.json`, the same way debris/barrels are).
- `src/render/radiator-hinge.ts` — `RADIATOR_HINGE`: the single body-frame position (the `up`/+X side's; `down`'s mirrors it by negating X) the radiator accordion's fold chain anchors to. Split into its own one-constant file, importing nothing, because `tools/export-models.mjs` reads it by transpiling this file in isolation through the TypeScript compiler API (see `export-assets` above) — folding it into `render/ships.ts` or `game/const.ts` directly would drag `three/webgpu` or other constants into that isolated transpile.
