Three independent readers need the exact same point or they'd disagree with each other: the baked mesh (`export-models.mjs` places the model's actual `hinge` object here), the runtime fold-chain math (`game/player/radiator.ts`'s `foldLocalPosition` walks the accordion outward from this same anchor to place each fold's collision proxy and screen geometry), and `render/ships.ts` (re-exporting it as the value other runtime modules should read) — a baked hinge position and the physics anchor it's supposed to match could otherwise silently drift apart the same way `RCS_NOZZLES`/earth-color already have to guard against.
- `src/render/scene.ts` — `createGameScene`: canvas → `WebGPURenderer` + `THREE.Scene`, the only place the renderer is created. `EnvironmentScene` itself now lives at `game/celestial/environment-scene.ts` (see above) — everything left under `render/` is THREE builders, taking `game/`-owned state as plain arguments rather than importing `game/` modules, with two narrow exceptions: `orbit-line.ts`/`sampled-line.ts` take a `FloatingOrigin` argument, since `RtoThreeV3`/`VtoThreeV3` are the sole `Vec3`→`THREE.Vector3` bridge and a line class has no other way to place its vertices; and `ships.ts` reads `game/const.ts` for gameplay-tuning values (colors, geometry sizes) that must match the rest of the game's tuning, the same read-only relationship every constant-consuming module has to that file.
- `src/render/celestial-grid.ts` — `CelestialGrid`: reference-only equatorial and ecliptic grids (a basis circle, latitude/longitude lines at 30° steps, and a pole marker per plane), each independently toggleable across all three parts (`CelestialGridVisibility`'s six flags — plane/pole/grid × equator/ecliptic). Vertices are built once at construction, fixed points on a unit sphere at `render/stars.ts`'s `STAR_SHELL_RADIUS`; `sync(visibility, cam, scale)` only toggles visibility and repositions/rescales the whole shell (`scale` relative to `STAR_SHELL_RADIUS` — `EnvironmentScene.sync` computes it from `overviewMode`, so the grid still reads at map zoom without this module reading `CameraSystem`/`game/const.ts` itself) — it never rebuilds geometry. The equatorial basis is the game's ECI basis directly (Y = pole); the ecliptic basis is ECI rotated through `physics/ecliptic.ts`'s exported `Q_ECL_TO_ECI`, so the 23.44° tilt is never hand-written here. All six toggles default to false — nothing from this module draws until something turns them on; the toggle state itself is `Navball.gridVisibility` (see `src/game/navball/` above).
- `src/render/earth-color.ts` — the Earth surface color (fBm-noise continents, biomes and clouds) as pure functions, **THREE-free on purpose**: `tools/export-earth-texture.mjs` imports it directly through the TypeScript compiler API and is the only consumer, making this file the single source of truth for what the baked texture contains. Nothing under `src/` imports it at runtime — `earth.ts` reads the finished image.
- `src/render/celestial-surface.ts` — `CelestialSurface`: a celestial body's surface mesh together with its day/night shading. **Celestial bodies never take part in scene lighting.** A body's drawn position is not its true one (combat view compresses it onto a fixed distance from the camera, `point-body.ts` puts it on the star shell), so a shared parallel light would put the terminator in the wrong place — up to 179° off for a planet, since the direction to the star from Earth and from that planet are unrelated. Instead each body holds a `sunDirection` uniform, set every `sync` from `Ephemeris.sunDirFrom(truePosition, t)`, and shades itself (`albedo × (NIGHT_AMBIENT + lambert × (1 − NIGHT_AMBIENT))` on an unlit `MeshBasicNodeMaterial`). `textured(url)`/`solid(color)` are the two surfaces bodies are built from; `render/earth.ts` follows the same rule with its own richer node graph, reusing the exported `NIGHT_AMBIENT` so every body's night side sits at one brightness. The scene's single `DirectionalLight` (`EnvironmentScene`) is left for the real-scale objects drawn near the render origin — ships, debris, casings — where one direction, taken from the render origin, is right for all of them. `setRingShadowSystem(rings, bodyCenter, bodyRadius, displayScale, axis)` is the body's own half of a ring casting shade on its planet: it holds a fixed-size array of `RING_SHADOW_BAND` uniform slots (32, comfortably above any registered `RingSystemDef`'s band count) and, per surface fragment, intersects the sun ray with each active band's ring plane and darkens the direct (lambert) term alone — never the night-side ambient floor — by that band's own Beer–Lambert transmittance at the sun's grazing angle through the ring, so a planet's evening terminator can fall inside a band's shadow while the band itself still reads correctly lit from other angles. Whichever `SphereBody`/`PointBody` owns both a surface and a `RingView` calls this once per `sync` alongside the ring's own `sync`, from the same resolved position/scale/axis, so the two can never disagree about where the ring plane sits.
- `src/render/{stars,orbit-line,sampled-line,billboard,glow-texture}.ts` — stars as a textured `SphereGeometry` shell (`createStars`, `BackSide`, depth-tested off since the shell always draws first at `renderOrder = -10`) painted with a real starfield photo, not per-star geometry — WebGPU points are still 1px and `THREE.Points` sizing still doesn't work, so any *procedural* point-style star field would still need small triangles the way the ring/asteroid point clouds already use, but a baked sky photo sidesteps the question entirely; `createSun`/`createMoon` build the sun billboard and moon mesh THREE objects that `game/celestial/{sun-body,sphere-body}.ts` place and scale each frame (`SUN_DISTANCE`=4.2e7 / `MOON_VIS_DIST`=4.5e7 m, inside the 6e7 far plane); camera-facing `Billboard` sprites (flashes, plume, RCS puffs) over a shared radial-`glow-texture`. `stars.ts` exports `STAR_SHELL_RADIUS` — the star shell's fixed radius — so `CelestialGrid` can share the exact same camera-following shell radius instead of picking its own.
- `src/physics/ring-optics.ts` — the ring's optical model as pure, THREE-free functions: `ringTransmission(tauNormal, muView)` (Beer-Lambert extinction through a slab of the given normal optical depth, foreshortened by the view angle's cosine `muView`), `henyeyGreenstein(cosTheta, g)` (the normalized scattering phase function), `ringPixelCoverage(widthMeters, metersPerPixel)` (a band's fractional screen coverage below 1px, so falling back to a line doesn't brighten the total light), `ringArcOpticalDepth(baseTau, arcScale, longitudeDeg, arcs)` (an arc's optical depth as a multiplier on the band's own, evaluated at one longitude — the non-overlapping-sector split that keeps an arc from double-compositing with its band), `ringSingleScattering(...)` (the slab single-scattering radiance a ring band actually emits toward the camera), and `ringPlanetShadow(...)` (whether a ring point's line to the sun passes through the parent body). `render/ring.ts`'s TSL shader graph and `createRingLine`'s per-frame CPU-side sync both call into this module rather than reimplementing the same optics twice.
- `src/render/ring.ts` — builds one `RingVisual` (`{object, sync}`) per `RingBandDef`, from `RingOpticsDef`'s physical parameters (`normalOpticalDepth`/`singleScatteringAlbedo`/`phaseG`/`color`) rather than a fixed opacity: `createAnnulusRing`/`createTorusRing`/`createTexturedRing` build a `MeshBasicNodeMaterial` whose TSL graph evaluates `ring-optics.ts`'s Beer-Lambert transmission and single-scattering formulas per fragment (`physicalMaterial`, shared by all three) — extinction from `exp(-tauNormal/muView)`, scattered radiance from the sun-facing optical depth times the escape transmittance times the Henyey-Greenstein phase, and a ray-vs-sphere shadow term (`inBodyShadow`) that darkens a band wherever the parent body sits between it and the sun. `createRingLine` is the degenerate sub-pixel view — a closed `THREE.Line` circle (repeating the first vertex as the last, since WebGPU's renderer has no `LineLoop`) whose opacity/color are instead recomputed on the CPU each `sync` via the same `ring-optics.ts` functions, since a 1px line has no per-fragment shading to speak of and can't shadow itself against the planet per-fragment either (an averaged, unshadowed approximation is used instead — see the module's own comment). `sectorParts(arcs)` splits a band with `RingArcDef`s into non-overlapping longitude sectors, each built as its own mesh at a scaled optical depth, so an arc's brightening composites exactly once rather than stacking with its band. All builders share `transparent: true, side: DoubleSide, depthWrite: false`, `SRGBColorSpace` where textured, and the `rotation.x = -π/2` tilt pointing the geometry's flat/circle normal along model +Y (the spin axis) — none of them decide *which* form (annulus vs. line, or the crossfade between them) a band uses at a given moment (`game/celestial/ring-view.ts`'s `RingView`, below) or resolve a `RingTextureId` to a URL (`game/celestial/celestial-registry.ts`'s job).
- `src/game/celestial/ring-view.ts` — `RingView` turns one `RingSystemDef` into the `THREE.Object3D`s `render/ring.ts` builds and drives their per-frame `RingVisualState` (body center/radius, sun direction, camera position, ring axis, coverage). **Deliberately not a child of the body's own mesh**: a body's mesh quaternion bakes in its full spin phase (`spinOrientation(axis, spinAngle)`), and while that's invisible on an axisymmetric ring, Neptune's Adams-ring arcs are not axisymmetric — inheriting spin phase would sweep them around the planet at its ~16h rotation period, when the arcs are meant to hold static. So `sync` takes the body's resolved position/scale (matching `SphereBody`/`PointBody`'s own each frame) and the pole axis alone, and rebuilds its own orientation via the same `spinOrientation` function called a second time with the angle zeroed — reusing the one existing attitude function rather than writing a new one. For each `thickness === 0` band it builds both an annulus and a line view and **crossfades** between them every `sync` via its own `thinBandBlend(bandWidthMeters, metersPerPixel)`: the `annulus`/`line` weights sum to 1 and interpolate linearly over `LINE_FADE_MIN_PX`–`LINE_FADE_MAX_PX` (0.75–1.25px) of on-screen band width, each weight multiplying that view's `coverage` so the two never together emit more light than one alone would — a hard cuts-over-at-1px switch would otherwise pop visibly as the camera zooms. It is fed the band's real physical width and `metersPerPixel` at the ring's **true** ECI position (not the `SphereBody` combat-view compressed one) — since `SphereBody`'s `visDist` compression is defined to preserve true angular size exactly, the true-position/true-width answer and the compressed-render answer always agree, so no second LOD formula is needed for the compressed case.
  - `orbit-line.ts`'s `OrbitLine` draws an **analytic ellipse from `OrbitalElements`**: vertices are stored relative to whichever body the passed-in `OrbitalElements` are centered on, moved per frame only via `line.position` (through `fo`, offset by `OrbitalElements.center.state.r` — that body's own ECI position), and regenerated only when osculating elements drift past thresholds (or `force=true` during burns/node editing) — rewriting vertices in ship-relative coords every frame made the ellipse visibly jitter under J2/third-body element wobble. **`sync` takes no center argument at all**: the ellipse and its translation both come off the `OrbitalElements`, so a caller cannot draw an ellipse at a body it isn't centered on. `densifyNear` (also center-relative) densifies vertices near a given point, and callers derive it from the same `Attractor` they picked via `strongestAttractor`/`orbitalElementsOf` (`attractor.ts`). `sync`'s optional `excludeNearBody: OrbitLineExcludeNearBody` (the eccentric anomaly and physical radius of a body that sits *on* this ellipse — a reference line's own planet, always at some point of its own orbit) makes the line **fade out as it approaches that body** rather than running through it: depth-testing a body's opaque mesh against an ellipse it's sitting on z-fights unpredictably as the camera zooms, and no `renderOrder`/`depthTest` choice fixes that once both are drawn at true position — only not drawing the line where the body already covers it does. `applyFade` runs every frame off `eAtIndex` (each vertex's own eccentric anomaly, written once per `regenerate`), so the fade tracks the body's motion along its orbit without re-deriving vertex positions. Per-vertex opacity is what a plain `LineBasicMaterial` cannot express, so the material is a `LineBasicNodeMaterial` whose `opacityNode` multiplies the uniform opacity by a `fade` vertex attribute read through TSL's `attribute()`; the attribute's `Float32Array` is allocated once and written in place like the position buffer. `fade` is a `smoothstep` over the arc distance from the body measured in its own radii — fully transparent at `FADE_TRANSPARENT_RADIUS_RATIO` (1), fully opaque at `FADE_OPAQUE_RADIUS_RATIO` (2). Because a body's angular half-size (`radius / a`, ~2e-4 rad for Jupiter) is normally an order of magnitude *below* the vertex spacing, the caller must densify vertices at the body for the gradient to have any steps at all; the line is therefore drawn as `THREE.LineSegments` over an index buffer, and `applyFade` also drops from the index any segment with a fully-transparent endpoint *or* whose endpoints **straddle** `E` (their signed differences from it differing in sign). Straddle is the floor that guarantees nothing is ever drawn across the body even where vertices are too sparse to fade — a high-eccentricity body densified at its periapsis instead degenerates to exactly that hard cut. A line with no `excludeNearBody` holds every vertex at `fade = 1`, and `fadeNeutral` keeps it from re-uploading that unchanged buffer every frame. Line colors: player light gray (`renderOrder=1`), secondary target cyan (`renderOrder=2`), primary target orange (`renderOrder=3` — a rendezvous target's orbit is often nearly coincident with the player's, and transparent draw order alone made it disappear underneath), enemies `COLOR_ENEMY_ORBIT_LINE` (a dim gray, so a target/lead highlight actually stands out), reference lines `renderOrder=0`. The planned trajectory is not an `OrbitLine` — it's `PlanArc`'s `SampledLine`, drawn white at `renderOrder=4`, since a multi-node plan is a chain of arcs rather than one closed ellipse. The `renderOrder` stack is ordered least-to-most likely to need to win a coincident-orbit tie: own orbit first, then whichever target line is more likely to matter (primary over secondary), then the planned line last since it's what the player is actively editing.
  - `sampled-line.ts`'s `SampledLine` is its sibling for **arbitrary sampled point series** — it takes a plain `readonly KinematicState[]`, so predicted arcs and entity `history` both feed it unchanged: `syncGeometry(samples, frame, ephemeris, scale)` bakes samples into `Frame`-relative vertices only when the series, frame, or `scale` (see below) changes by more than `SCALE_REBAKE_RATIO` (non-rigid, so the vertices are rewritten in place into the constructor-allocated `MAX_VERTICES` buffer and the drawn length set with `setDrawRange` — see the WebGPU gotcha below), Hermite-subdividing each edge on the way via `kinematic-state.ts`'s `hermiteInterpolate` — `toFrameState`'s frame-relative velocities are the tangents. The chord count per edge targets a fixed **on-screen** sagitta (`MAX_EDGE_SAG_PX`, sagitta ≈ chord·turn/8) rather than a fixed turn angle: a fixed angle bounds the bulge in world space only, which does not shrink with zoom, so at high zoom the world-space gap between the polyline and the true curve reads as a growing number of screen pixels. `scale: ScaleAtFn` (`(worldPos: Vec3) => number`, metres per pixel — structurally identical to `camera/camera-system.ts`'s `ScaleFn`, redeclared here rather than imported since `render/` must not depend on `game/`) is what converts the pixel target back into a per-edge turn-angle allowance, clamped to `MAX_EDGE_TURN` (5°) so a far zoom never gets coarser than before. Because the desired chord counts are picked independently per edge, their sum can exceed `MAX_VERTICES` at high zoom — and `metersPerPixel`'s depth floor (`projection.ts`'s `MIN_DEPTH`) can make a single behind-camera edge's desired count blow up by many orders of magnitude, so a global scale-down would starve every other edge to its 1-chord floor instead of shrinking the one edge that actually asked for too much. `syncGeometry` instead caps each edge at `budget / edgeCount`, an equal share of the vertex budget, before consulting its own desired count — so no one edge can spend another edge's share, and the sum is always within budget without needing a second pass. `syncTransform` does the per-frame O(1) work — rigid un-bake rotation via `toInertialQuat` plus the floating-origin translation. Passing a `DashPattern` makes it a `THREE.LineDashedMaterial` and bakes the cumulative `lineDistance` attribute alongside the vertices; `setDash(dashSize, gapSize)` rewrites the pattern without touching that geometry, which is what lets a caller hold the on-screen dash spacing constant while the camera zooms. `visible` reports the result of `applyVisible`'s "fewer than two vertices means nothing is drawn" rule, so a caller asking "is this line actually on screen" gets one answer rather than re-counting samples.

  **Solid means it will happen, dashed means it is being asked for.** `PredictedTrajectoryLine` (integrated future) and `OrbitLine` (analytic ellipse) are solid; `PlanArc` (the maneuver plan) is dashed. Nothing else may take a dash pattern without a reason to read as unrealized.
  - Both use `THREE.Line` — **`LineLoop` is unsupported by the WebGPU renderer**; close loops manually.
- `src/types/{three-shims,png-shims}.d.ts` — three.js 0.169 ships no `.d.ts` for `three/webgpu`; minimal `WebGPURenderer` typings (remove once upstream types cover it), plus PNG import typing. Import THREE **only** from `'three/webgpu'` (mixing with `'three'` would duplicate classes at runtime).

### WebGPU renderer gotchas (three.js 0.169)
- `THREE.LineLoop` and `THREE.Points` sizing are not supported; use `THREE.Line` / triangle-based sprites.
- Additive-blended materials need explicit `transparent: true`.
- **Vertex data must be updated in place, never by swapping the geometry or the attribute.** `RenderObject` caches both the geometry and the resolved attribute list when it is first built, so assigning a new `BufferGeometry` to `mesh.geometry` — or a new `BufferAttribute` onto a live geometry — keeps drawing the original vertices. Allocate the `Float32Array` once, write into it, set `attribute.needsUpdate = true`, and vary the drawn length with `geometry.setDrawRange`. Both line classes (`render/orbit-line.ts`, `render/sampled-line.ts`) are built this way, which is why their vertex capacity is a fixed constant.
- Headless-Chrome screenshots work with `--headless=new --enable-gpu --enable-unsafe-webgpu --disable-gpu-sandbox --no-sandbox` (flaky; retry a few times).

Not yet implemented: enemy *maneuvering* AI (enemies shoot back — `Enemy.behave` burst-fires lead-aimed plasma — but never thrust), solar-storm/combat auroras, ECS, full N-body cislunar phase. See `memos/mikanixonable/dev.md` for design direction.

## Project concept (from memos/mikanixonable/dev.md)

An orbital-mechanics shooting game, intended as a web game, set in the Earth-Moon system (current build: LEO only):

- Gameplay centers on realistic orbital maneuvers and physically accurate ballistic calculations, low-poly but visually appealing depictions of Earth's atmosphere, spacecraft, and spacecraft destruction.
- Orbital calculations should account for Sun-Earth-Moon three-body dynamics, atmospheric drag, and tidal forces. Reference concepts to consider: Sun-Earth and Earth-Moon Lagrange points, sun-synchronous recurrent orbits, halo orbits, low-energy transfers and ballistic capture, double lunar swingbys, and gradual altitude-raising approach maneuvers (like the Kounotori HTV's ISS approach).
- Aesthetic goals: Earth auroras, auroras from solar storms, auroras from charged particles generated by combat, shadows on Earth's evening clouds, and the Dzhanibekov effect on destroyed debris.

## Controls (current build)

**Translation is body-frame, not orbital-frame** (`PlayerThrottle.buildThrust` builds a direction in body axes and rotates it by `att.q`): W/S = nose-forward/aft (+Z/−Z), A/D = lateral (+X/−X), Q/E = vertical (+Y/−Y). All six fire at the same selected magnitude `THROTTLE_LEVELS[throttleIdx]` (4 stages — [1]/[2]/[3]/[4], the 4th at 4× the 3rd's output — no "cutoff" stage; thrust exists only while a direction key is held).

Rotation: I/K = pitch, **L/J = yaw**, **O/U = roll** (signs per `PlayerThrottle.updateTorque`'s `inX`/`inY`/`inZ`; `RcsEffects.sync` lights the nozzles whose own torque points the same way as that commanded torque, so the visible thrusters and the key held can't disagree). T = RCS rotation damping toggle, **F = prograde attitude reset** (one-shot: only sets the hold ON), **C = prograde-hold autopilot toggle** (PD controller torquing the nose to the velocity vector; any manual rotation key cancels it), **V = fine-attitude toggle**. **`Digit9`/`Digit0`** (the top-row 9/0) toggle the `up`(left/+X)/`down`(right/-X) radiator panels' deploy state (`Player.handleEdgePress` calls `radiator.toggle('up'|'down')` directly — the same panels the status-window buttons drive) — distinct from the numeric-keypad `Numpad0`/`Numpad1` below, which roll the camera instead.

G = toggle whether the chase camera's reference frame follows ship attitude vs. the orbital frame (see below). Z (hold) = gunsight zoom. Space = fire. **Right click on an enemy** (within the same `CLICK_MOVE_THRESHOLD` click-vs-drag test the left button uses) **opens a context menu offering "set/clear target" and "set/clear secondary target"** (`Targeter.updateCombatTargeting`) — a miss instead opens a menu offering "open settings menu" (the same pause menu `[Esc]` opens). Target selection has no automatic re-pick: whichever `Enemy` was last set as target/secondary stays until explicitly cleared or replaced, even off-screen or behind the camera. The secondary target only affects what's drawn (cyan orbit line/marker/enemy-list row) — board marks, ◇/◆ bearing markers and LEAD all read the primary target only. **left drag**, **right drag**, or **arrow keys** = camera yaw/pitch, **`Numpad0`/`Numpad1`** (alt: `/`/`_`) = camera roll (around the camera's own current view axis — `ChaseCamera` in combat view, `OverviewCamera` on the map, where it rolls the map's up vector), wheel or two-finger pinch = zoom distance, **middle drag** or **`@`/`:`/`;`/`]`** (up/down/left/right) = pan — the keyboard pan keys synthesize `panDx`/`panDy` at `C.CAM_KEY_PAN_RATE` px/s in `CameraSystem.update` so they drive the exact same `ChaseCamera`/`OverviewCamera` pan code path as the middle-drag, a keyboard alternative for laptops without a usable middle-click drag. `,` / `.` = time warp (thrust, guns, enemy fire and contact physics are all gated above ×`MAX_PHYS_SIM_SPEED` = 4 via `SimSpeedManager.can*`). M = orbit-planning map, N = auto-warp to the next node, X = delete selected node / discard the whole plan, H = help, R = manual reload (after the game ends it instead re-launches **the same stage** — `Stage.restart` navigates to `?stage=<id>`, since a plain reload would drop you back on the stage-select screen whenever you got there without a `?stage=` param).

On the map, **left-clicking a self-flown ship or a base selects it** (`MapPicker.handleLeftClick`, checked before a left click can instead place/select a maneuver node) — a ship becomes the operated ship (`Game.setActivePlayer`, same as the property window's "操作対象にする"), a base becomes the one `Docking.canEnterDock()` accepts (`Docking.selectBase`) without switching the current view by itself; entering the dock still needs an explicit action (the property window's "ドックビューを開く", or the `ViewBadge`'s view selector once a base is selected). **Double-clicking any pickable object moves the overview camera's focus there** (`MapPicker.handleDoubleClick`, over the whole `pickables` set — celestial bodies/ships/bases/ammo/apsis/AN-DN icons alike — the same effect as that object's property-window "move focus" item, just faster); it consumes `Input`'s native-`dblclick`-backed `takeDoubleClicks` queue and runs independently of the single left-click handling above, so a double-click on a ship both selects it (from the two constituent single clicks) and moves focus (from the double-click itself).

On the map, right-clicking a celestial label, a living ship, a base, an ammo pickup, a periapsis/apoapsis icon, or a relative AN/DN icon opens a **property window** for that object (`hud/property-window.ts`'s `PropertyWindow`, opened/owned by `MapPicker` — see `map-picker.ts` above): a title/subtitle, a live-updating property block (armor/temp/power/ammo for a self-flown ship; distance/closing speed/relative speed/orbital elements/relative inclination for an enemy; money/docked-ship count/distance/orbital elements for a base; distance/orbital elements for an ammo pickup; display name/kind/μ/radius/distance/orbital elements for a celestial body; altitude/time-to-crossing for an apsis or relative AN/DN icon), and the same action items `MapPicker.itemsFor` always built (by `MapPickable.kind`): every kind offers "move focus" (`Game.frameControls.setFocus`); a ship additionally offers "set/clear navigation target" (`NavTarget.toggleTarget` — independent of `Targeter`'s combat target, see the Architecture section); an apsis or relative-AN/DN icon additionally offers "add node here" (`PlanEditor.addNodeAt`, at that icon's crossing time) and, for relative AN/DN only, "warp here" (`SimSpeedManager.startAutoWarpTo`). In creative mode only, a self-flown ship/enemy/ammo pickup/base additionally offers "duplicate" — opens `ShipPlacerPanel` pre-filled with that object's current orbital elements (基準天体/近地点+遠地点高度/傾斜角/Ω/ω/ν all copied — ν included, so the duplicate starts exactly where the original is; nothing offsets it), object type fixed to match, ready to place a lightly-tweaked copy. A self-flown ship or a base additionally shows a ✎ rename button beside the window's title (`Player.displayName`/`Base.name`).

A property window can be dragged by its header, and its 📌 button **clips** it — an unclipped window is a *temporary* one: at most one exists at a time (opening or right-clicking a second object closes the previous temporary window), and it closes on an outside click, on picking any action item, or on leaving the map view. A clipped window survives all of those, so several can be pinned open side by side to compare objects; only its own ✕ button, deleting the object, or the object itself vanishing (destroyed/picked up/removed) closes it. Right-clicking an object that already has an open window (of either kind) just moves that window to the click position and brings it to front, rather than opening a second one. Right-clicking empty space still opens a plain context menu (`ContextMenu`, not a property window — there's no object to show properties of) offering "show orbit object window" — a top-right list of the same candidate set, click to focus, right-click to open that object's property window (`object-list-panel.ts`'s `ObjectListPanel`) — and "open settings menu" (`Game.openSettingsMenu`, forwarding to `SettingsPanel.toggle(true)` — the same pause menu `[Esc]` opens) — in any mode; creative mode's menu additionally offers "place object" there (`ShipPlacerPanel`, below).

Esc (or the ⚙ gear) = pause menu, owned by `hud/settings-panel.ts`: `SettingsPanel.toggle` fires `onSettingsOpenChange`, which `main.ts` syncs to `Game.pause()`/`Game.resume()` (so every open/close path — Esc, gear click, [閉じる] — pauses/resumes), plus a BGM toggle and a "quit to title" button (`onQuitToTitle` → `location.assign` with `?title=1`, which forces `resolveLaunchSelection` to show the title screen instead of resuming the active slot's last launch). While paused `Game.update` returns early, so HP auto-regen (`HP_REGEN_RATE` = 1 HP/s, inside `Player.behave`) can't be farmed from the menu. `handleInput` still runs before the pause check, so each key's owner decides for itself what a paused frame means — `ViewManager.update` leaves `[M]` unconsumed while a dock is open, and `Docking.handleInput` takes `[Esc]` before `SettingsPanel` sees it so closing a dock never also opens the pause menu.

F5 = clip the current moment as a named snapshot, F9 = open/close the snapshot browser (`save/` + `hud/save-browser.ts` above; the `[Esc]` menu's スナップショット item is the same entry point). Both the browser and the settings menu sit in the same `system` overlay layer (`overlay-layer.ts`), so opening one closes the other, and `SaveBrowser.open()`/`close()` pause and resume the game themselves rather than leaving that to whoever opened them — `Game.handleInput` takes `[Esc]` for the browser *before* `SettingsPanel` sees it, so closing the browser can't also open the pause menu. F5 is refused (with a hint, nothing written) once the active stage has decided (`!activeStage.isPlaying`), since a decided `phase` restores as plain data with no result-screen replay and would otherwise load into a frozen screen. Autosave takes an unnamed, unclipped snapshot every 60 real seconds on the same condition.

Firing implies fine-attitude authority (`FINE_ATTITUDE_SCALE` = 0.5): `Player.updateTorque` passes `fineAttitude || fire.isFiring`, so releasing the trigger restores full authority with no edge-tracking state.

### Creative mode

The title screen (`launch-select.ts`) opens on a ステージモード/クリエイティブモード tab pair; picking クリエイティブ or loading `?mode=creative` starts `CreativeStage` with `ViewManager`'s initial view set to `'map'` (the map is where placement happens, so creative mode opens on it rather than a combat view with nothing yet to fly). `[M]` is disabled — `ViewManager.handleInput`'s `canToggleView` argument, `Game.handleInput`'s `this.player.alive` — until at least one placed ship is active, since the combat view has no meaning without a ship to fly. The マップモード-only 艦艇配置 panel (`creative/ship-placer-panel.ts`) places ships by orbital elements or by a Lagrange-point halo/Lissajous orbit (see `src/physics/halo.ts` above for why the latter drifts once the simulation runs); a placed ship's right-click menu (`kind: 'player'` in `MapPicker.itemsFor`) opens with 軌道計画の実行 — a single item cycling OFF→瞬間移動→自動操縦→OFF on each click (`keepOpen: true`, so the window stays open across clicks; label shows the current mode, `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s `PlanExecutionMode` above), shown only in creative mode (same `isCreativeMode()` gate `duplicateItems()` uses — no other stage drives `PlanExecutor`/the `'instant'` teleport, so offering the choice there would be a control with no effect) — followed by 操作対象にする/操作対象を解除 (`Game.setActivePlayer`/`setActivePlayerOrNull(null)`, one label replacing the other by whether this ship is already active — deactivating leaves `Game.player` `null`, the same "no ship flown" state a Creative wipe-out leaves), フォーカスを移動, 航法ターゲットに設定/解除 (`NavTarget`, same `navTargetItems` helper the `'body'`/`'ship'` kinds use), and 削除 — the last is simply absent from the menu for the currently-active ship (deleting it would leave `player` dangling).

The orbital direction markers (`player/player-markers.ts`) deliberately carry no key hints in their labels: combat-view translation is body-frame, so none of those six directions has a key. The keys that *do* mean prograde/normal/radial are the map-mode Δv edit keys, and the direction markers are hidden there.

Chase camera reference frame (`camFollowAttitude` on `ChaseCamera`, default **on**, toggled via `[G]`, which `CameraSystem.update` forwards through `CombatCameraSystem.toggleFollowAttitude`): the third-person camera's orientation is stored as a single quaternion offset (`rot`) from a reference frame, but *which* frame is configurable. ON (default) references the ship's own attitude (`player.att.q`), so rotating the ship with I/K/J/L/U/O visibly swings the whole camera (including its roll) right along with it, RCS-linked. OFF references ECI directly, so the camera holds a fixed orientation in inertial space regardless of how the ship rotates, until dragged. Toggling reinterprets `rot` through the ship's current attitude so the view doesn't jump. While the ship is destroyed, ON stops folding in the ship's attitude (which would otherwise be its post-destruction tumble) and holds the last orientation instead. `CombatCameraSystem` itself only decides between `ChaseCamera` and `GunsightCamera` (via `zoomActive`) and animates the FOV each holds in its own `view`.

Ammo system: guns fire alternately from the two front bores and consume magazine rounds (`MAG_ROUNDS`=32/mag, `INITIAL_MAGS`=3 linked at start, `MAGS_PER_BARREL`=3 consumed before a barrel-swap reload — doubling the per-mag capacity from an original 16-round design let the visible chain be roughly half as long while carrying comparable total ammo). The belt of unused mags hangs off the ship's port (+X) side as child meshes of `player.obj`, and the belt slides continuously into the hull as rounds are fired (`beltFeed` = 1 − rounds/`MAG_ROUNDS` offsets the anchor by up to one pitch; on mag consumption the feed wraps 1→0 exactly when the link count drops, so the motion reads as seamless). The head link sits half-inside the hull. `BeltPhysics.update` (in `player/belt-physics.ts`) simulates the belt's sag as an actual physics chain, not a canned animation: link joints are point masses (`beltPos`/`beltPrevPos`, Verlet-integrated in the ship's body frame) connected by rigid-rod distance constraints (position-based dynamics, 6 relaxation iterations per frame) to `MAG_BELT_PITCH`. Since the ship is in free fall, ordinary gravity can't sag anything (equivalence principle — belt and hull fall together); the only real driver is the fictitious force felt in the ship's non-inertial (accelerating/rotating) frame: `-a_thrust - α×r - ω×(ω×r) - 2ω×v` (translational inertia, Euler, centrifugal, Coriolis), built from `thrustAccelVec` (world-frame thrust accel, rotated into body frame, passed in each call from `Player.behave` via `Belt.update`) and `player.att.w` (with `α` estimated by finite-differencing `w` against `prevShipW`, both tracked inside `BeltPhysics`). When the player isn't thrusting or rotating, the chain just hangs straight — no ambient wobble is faked in.

Each link's *orientation* is derived separately from its position, via parallel transport: `qBend = qDelta * qPrev` where `qDelta` is the minimal (zero-twist) rotation taking the previous link's local +X to this link's tangent — so bending (the chain folding link-to-link) is unconstrained by construction, matching a real ammo belt's easy up/down/left/right flex. Roll (twist about the link's own +X, the one DOF parallel transport leaves undefined) is instead tracked explicitly per link in `beltTwist[]`, driven by the ship's own roll rate (`player.att.w.z * MAG_CHAIN_ROLL_GAIN`) at link 0 and propagated down the chain with a one-frame lag (`prevTwist`) and exponential smoothing (`MAG_CHAIN_ROLL_RATE`), clamped every frame to `±MAG_CHAIN_MAX_ROLL_DEG` — so however it's driven, twist can never exceed that bound. The final per-link quaternion is `qBend * qTwist(local +X)`.

`ContactPhysics` (in `game/simulation/contact.ts`; rigid-sphere contact for casings/enemies/pickups/radiator folds/belt) is driven from `Simulator.advance` in two cadences: `resolveSubstep` runs once per substep alongside the integration, while `resolveBelt` runs once per frame at the real (unwarped) `dt`, since the belt is a ship-local simulation on its own real-time cadence. Both are gated on `simSpeedManager.canResolvePhysicalCollisions` — at higher warp contact is skipped entirely, since substeps up to 20s each make rigid-body contact resolution meaningless. The ship's contact and hit radius are the one `PLAYER_HULL_RADIUS` (real hull size, 2.6m), so a freshly-ejected casing (spawned ~2.9m from the hull) clears it rather than being flung outward on the same frame. A casing/hull impact plays `Sfx.clank()` from the colliding entity's own `collideWith` (`DebrisPiece` for the casing side, `Player` for the hull side), not from the contact resolver.

When mags run low (`LOGISTICS_LOW_MAGS`), resupply pickups spawn phase-shifted 1.25–2.5 km ahead on the player's own orbit (`stage-utils/logistics.ts`'s `Logistics.spawnForPlayer`; ▣ AMMO marker, absorb within `AMMO_PICKUP_RADIUS`). Casings use a CIWS-style bottleneck profile (rim/extractor groove/tapered body/shoulder/neck via `LatheGeometry`) with **low** ejection speed but widely varying per-casing spin, on an asymmetric inertia tensor so the Dzhanibekov effect shows on tumbling casings the same way it does on debris. Spin-up SFX is filtered-noise friction (no oscillator). Enemy ships are modeled at `ENEMY_SCALE` (20×) via `obj.scale.setScalar(...)` with `ENEMY_RADIUS` (180 m) matching for hit detection; their destruction VFX (`spawnFlash` sizes, `scatterFragments` size range) scale by the same factor so the explosion reads proportionate to the larger hull.

Mouse buttons are swapped from the naive default: **left button drags the camera; a left click that moves less than `CLICK_MOVE_THRESHOLD` (6px) is instead queued as a click** (in map mode: selects the self-flown ship/base it lands on — see `MapPicker.handleLeftClick` — or, failing that, places/selects a maneuver node) — see `Input.clicks()`. **Right button also drags the camera** (feeding the same `dx`/`dy` as the left drag — a two-finger trackpad drag, since right-click has no natural trackpad equivalent), and a right click within `CLICK_MOVE_THRESHOLD` (`Input.pushIfClick`, shared across left/middle/right) instead opens a target/context menu. Firing is Space-only now — right click no longer fires. This lets map-mode node placement and camera dragging share the left button, and camera dragging and menu-opening share the right button, without a modifier key.

Map mode ([M]) does not freeze physics: `Simulator.advance` runs every frame while `activeStage.isPlaying && !paused`, and firing stays disabled (`Player.behave`'s `mapMode` branch only ticks the reload timer). Manual rotation (RCS, IJKLUO) is never gated by map mode — it shares no keys with Δv editing. Translation (WASDQE) is gated on whether a plan node is currently selected (`Game`'s `dvEditActive = editor.editMode && editor.selectedNodeIdx !== null`, passed to `Player.behave`): with no node selected, WASDQE fire the real engines exactly as in combat view; with one selected, the same keys instead edit that node's Δv (prograde/normal/radial, not body axes) and real thrust is suppressed for the frame. So the player can fly for real while the map is open, and only loses direct thrust control while actively shaping a burn. Only `editor.updateEditing` and the pointer dispatch are layered on top of `update`; the map-mode panels (MAP VIEW / PREDICT / 座標系) show and refresh themselves from their owners' `sync`. The map camera's drag yaw sign is intentionally opposite the combat chase camera's convention (`+=` vs `-=` on `mouse.dx`) so panning left/right reads the same in both views.

Each of the six translation keys can be **latched** by pressing it twice within `THRUST_LATCH_DOUBLE_TAP_SEC` (`PlayerThrottle`'s `updateThrustLatches`/`isThrustHeld`): a latched key keeps firing that thruster as if held, independent of the physical key state, until double-tapped again to release it. This lets sustained burns run without holding a key down — useful in particular while the map is open and the mouse is busy dragging the camera/plan. Latches are per-key (not per-axis), cleared on `clearTransientState` (deactivating/parking the ship); holding a key's opposite direction also holds that key's latch cleared for as long as the opposite key stays down, so latching W and then holding S gives reverse thrust instead of the two canceling out, and releasing S leaves W's latch gone rather than reviving it. Above `MAX_PHYS_SIM_SPEED` (where thrust doesn't apply anyway) double-tapping neither latches nor consumes the press, so a latch can't accumulate silently during high warp and surprise-fire once warp drops back down. Holding **any** opposite pair together — W+S, A+D, or Q+E — is an emergency all-stop: it zeroes thrust on *every* axis (not just the held pair's own) and clears every latch, regardless of what else is held or latched at the time.
